# English Study Notes: Review Book

Date: 2026-06-05  
Category: english-writing

## Overview

A structured English review guide compiled from 219 lessons covering American pronunciation, advanced vocabulary, idioms, grammar accuracy, and executive communication. Each entry focuses on core takeaways, structured reference tables, and critical usage notes.

## Study Method

1. **Target one lesson**: Review the concept summary and key distinctions.
2. **Internalize through audio**: Read expressions and example sentences aloud to absorb natural American stress and rhythm.
3. **Active reuse**: Apply target collocations and sentence patterns directly in writing and speech.

## Avoid This Pronunciation to Sound Educated

### Summary

Articulate complete consonant clusters without omitting sounds, such as the /sk/ in *asterisk* or /ksθ/ in *sixth*, and avoid adding non-existent letters like pronouncing *height* with a final /θ/ instead of /t/. Distinguish subtle phonetic and semantic differences in confusable pairs like *moot* versus *mute* and *prostate* versus *prostrate*.

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake | Meaning / natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Asterisk | AS-tuh-risk | AS-tuh-risks | A star symbol (*). "Please put an asterisk next to an item you consider urgent." |
| Prostate | PRAH-state | prostrate | A part of the male reproductive system. "That test is used to diagnose prostate cancer." |
| Prostrate | PRAH-strate | prostate | Lying face down on the ground. "I saw a man prostrate on the ground." |
| Arctic | ARK-tik | AR-tik | The area around the North Pole. "His grandfather was an arctic explorer." |
| Height | HYT | HYTH | How tall something is. "I need to adjust the height of this chair." |
| Sixth | SIKSTH | SIKS | Number six in a sequence. "We threw a big party for his sixth birthday." |
| Realtor | REEL-ter | REE-luh-ter | A real estate agent. "The realtor showed us some houses last weekend." |
| Supposedly | suh-PO-zed-lee | suh-PO-zab-lee | Assumed to be true but doubtful. "He supposedly graduated from college." |
| February | FEB-roo-air-ee | FEB-yoo-air-ee | The second month. "Valentine's Day is on February 14th." |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- Moot vs. Mute: "Moot" means irrelevant to the discussion (e.g., "a moot point"). "Mute" means unable to speak or making no sound. A very common mistake even among native speakers is saying "mute point" instead of the correct "moot point".
- Height vs. Length/Strength: "Length" and "strength" end in a "TH" sound, which causes people to incorrectly add a "TH" to "height". Remember that "height" ends with a sharp "T" sound.

### Reusable Sentence Patterns

| Pattern | Use it for | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Please put an asterisk next to... | Highlighting important items in a document. | Please put an asterisk next to the budget figures. |
| It's a moot point. | Ending a debate when the outcome no longer matters. | I disagree with your plan, but it's a moot point now since the deadline passed. |

## 10 English Proverbs about LOVE

### Summary

English love proverbs serve as cultural shorthand for complex relationship advice and function as fixed, holistic units. Situational context dictates the choice between contradictory truths, such as 'absence makes the heart grow fonder' versus 'out of sight, out of mind.'

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ignoring flaws | Love is blind | When you love someone, you don't see their faults. | You know what they say, love is blind; she can't see what a bad person he is. |
| There are other people | There are other fish in the sea | Used to comfort someone after a breakup, meaning they will find someone else. | Don't worry about the breakup, you'll meet a great guy soon. There are other fish in the sea. |
| Looks aren't everything | Beauty is only skin deep | A person's character matters more than their physical appearance. | She's very attractive, but when you get to know her you realize beauty is only skin deep. |
| Everyone has different tastes | Beauty is in the eye of the beholder | Different people find different things beautiful. | Jenny is beautiful to him, but I think she's plain. It just proves beauty is in the eye of the beholder. |
| Different people like each other | Opposites attract | People who are very different often fall in love. | She loves to socialize, but he prefers staying home. Opposites attract. |
| Distance makes love stronger | Absence makes the heart grow fonder | You love someone more when you are apart from them. | Ever since her boyfriend moved away, she can't stop thinking about him. Absence makes the heart grow fonder. |
| Distance ruins love | Out of sight, out of mind | If you don't see someone, you forget about them. | When he went away for a year, she lost interest. Out of sight, out of mind. |
| Pain will pass | Time heals all wounds | Emotional pain and sadness fade as time passes. | I know the breakup is painful, but remember that time heals all wounds. |
| Heartbreak is worth it | It's better to have loved and lost than never to have loved at all | Experiencing love is valuable, even if it ends in sadness. | Even though it ended badly, I don't regret it. It's better to have loved and lost than never to have loved at all. |
| Dating takes time | You have to kiss a lot of frogs to find your prince | You must date many wrong people before finding the right partner. | Online dating is frustrating, but you have to kiss a lot of frogs to find your prince. |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- Fixed Expressions: Proverbs are fixed idioms. Do not change the vocabulary inside them (e.g., do not say "there are more fish in the ocean"). Say them exactly as written.
- Context: "There are other fish in the sea" and "Time heals all wounds" are specifically used to comfort someone who is grieving a relationship.

## 10 Ways to Apologize in English with a Perfect American Accent

### Summary

Authentic American apologies use an open /ɑː/ vowel in *sorry* ('SAH-ree') paired with structures like 'sorry that I [past tense]' or 'sorry for [gerund]'. Convey accountability by explicitly admitting fault with phrases such as 'I was wrong to...' or 'I shouldn't have...'

### Grammar and Structure Rules

When giving a reason for your apology, use one of two structures:

- Subject + Verb (Past Tense):
- I'm sorry (that) I said that.
- I'm sorry I came late.
- For + Gerund (Verb-ing):
- I'm sorry for saying that.
- I'm sorry for coming late.

### Reusable Sentence Patterns

| Pattern | Use it for | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| I'm [truly/so/very] sorry for [verb+ing] | General sincere apologies. | I'm truly sorry for hurting your feelings. |
| I [sincerely] apologize for [verb+ing] | Formal or professional apologies. | I sincerely apologize for forgetting the meeting. |
| It was wrong of me to [verb] | Taking full responsibility for a bad action. | It was wrong of me to blame you. |
| I shouldn't have [past participle] | Expressing regret about a past action. | I shouldn't have spoken to you that way. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word / Phrase | Correct pronunciation | Important rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sorry | SAH-ree | Use an open 'ah' sound, not 'so-ree'. |
| Apologize | uh-PAH-luh-jize | Stress the second syllable with an open 'ah'. |
| Hurting | HER-ding | The 't' between 'r' and a vowel often becomes a fast flap 'd'. |
| Shouldn't have | SHUD-ent-uv | Often casually pronounced as "shouldn't of". |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- Sorry vs. Apologize Formality: "I'm sorry" is used universally. "I apologize" or "my apologies" is slightly more formal, making it excellent for business or written contexts.
- Slipped my mind: This is a very natural idiom to use when you forget an appointment, a birthday, or a small task. It implies the forgetting was an honest accident.

## Breath or Breathe? 3 Rules

### Summary

The noun *breath* uses a short /ɛ/ vowel (as in *bed*) and ends with an unvoiced /θ/. In contrast, the verb *breathe* adds a final 'e', uses a long /iː/ vowel (as in *please*), and ends with a voiced /ð/.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Stop breathing normally | Out of breath | Breathing heavily after exercise or exertion. | That was really difficult; I'm out of breath. |
| Rest and breathe normally | Catch my breath | To rest until your breathing returns to normal. | I need a few minutes to catch my breath. |
| Very amazing / beautiful | Breathtaking / Takes my breath away | So beautiful that you stop breathing in shock. | The view from my hotel was breathtaking. |
| Don't bother talking | Save your breath | Stop talking because it won't make a difference. | He's not listening to you, save your breath. |
| Something new and better | A breath of fresh air | A refreshing new change or approach. | The new manager is a breath of fresh air compared to the old one. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake | Meaning / natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Breath | BRETH (voiceless th) | BREE-th | (Noun) The air taken in and out. "Take a deep breath." |
| Breathe | BREE-TH (voiced th) | BRETH | (Verb) The action of inhaling/exhaling. "Breathe in and out." |

### Common Mistakes

| Mistake | Correct version | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Take a deep breathe. | Take a deep breath. | "Breath" is the noun object here. |
| I can't breath. | I can't breathe. | "Breathe" is the verb needed after "can't". |

## Advanced English Expressions with a Psychologist

### Summary

Differentiate the noun form of *complex* (first-syllable stress) from its adjective form (second-syllable stress), and distinguish neutral evaluation (*critique*) from negative judgment (*criticize*). Common expressions for high-pressure performance include *hit the mark*, *be on*, and *difficult to take*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| In many ways | In a multitude of ways | In various, numerous ways. | The experience impacted me in a multitude of ways. |
| Notice subtly | Pick up | Notice something difficult to see or feel. | I'm surprised nobody else picked up that he was angry. |
| Subtle / relaxed | Low-key | Subtle, or referring to a relaxed person/event. | It's not a big party, just a low-key gathering. |
| Exactly | The very | Emphasizing one particular specific thing. | Beethoven lived in this very house. |
| Unemployed | Out of work | Not having a job currently. | He was out of work for two years. |
| Evaluate | Critique | Give a detailed evaluation (not necessarily bad). | Could you please critique my speech? |
| Judge negatively | Criticize | Point out faults and negative aspects. | The politician was criticized for his bad decisions. |
| Finish on time | Hit a deadline (meet) | To finish something by the required time. | I hope we can hit the deadline this Friday. |
| Exactly what's needed | Hit the spot | Perfectly satisfying (often for food/drink). | I was so thirsty, that cold drink really hit the spot. |
| Examine closely | Scrutinize | Look at every detail very carefully. | The actor's every move is being scrutinized by the media. |
| A lot / intensely | To a very high degree | To a large extent. | It's done to a very high degree of detail. |
| Succeed / be perfect | Hit the mark | Succeed in pleasing people or meeting expectations. | They need to look good and be ready to hit the mark. |
| In performance mode | To be on | Ready to perform; acting engaged and perfect. | My job requires me to be on all the time. |
| Hard to tolerate | Difficult to take | Hard to deal with emotionally. | When people are brutal, it's difficult to take. |
| Over and over | Time and again | Repeatedly over a long period. | I've asked you to do that time and again. |
| Completely | Altogether | (One word) On the whole; entirely. | I don't altogether agree with you. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Important rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Complex (noun) | COM-plex | Stress first syllable (e.g., apartment complex). |
| Complex (adjective) | com-PLEX | Stress second syllable (e.g., a complex industry). |
| Critique | cri-TEEK | Stress second syllable. |

## Advanced Vocabulary for Buying a House in the US

### Summary

US real estate transactions rely on industry terminology including *escrow*, *fund*, *closing costs*, *condo*, and *fixer*. In professional communication, use *key* as an adjective meaning crucial, *x amount* as a placeholder for unknown numerical quantities, and workplace idioms like *putting out fires* and *on top of it*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| All types of people | All walks of life | Various social classes or professions. | I get to meet people from all walks of life. |
| Very important | Key | (Adjective) Crucial to success. | Good communication is key in my profession. |
| Finalizing a sale | Closing skills | The ability to get a buyer/seller to say yes. | A salesperson needs good closing skills; he's a good closer. |
| New buyers | First-time buyers | People buying a house for the first time. | They were first-time buyers and almost lost the deal. |
| Third-party account | Escrow | A neutral company holding money during a sale. | We opened escrow on a house. |
| An unknown number | X amount | An unspecified variable or amount. | You have X amount of days for that to fund. |
| Provide money/loan | To fund | Bank sending the loan money to escrow. | We made sure everything was getting funded. |
| Handle crises | Put out fires | Dealing with urgent emergencies at work. | I spent the workday putting out fires. |
| In control / aware | On top of it | Managing a situation successfully. | Good job, you're really on top of it. |
| Forbidden | A no-no | Something unacceptable or not allowed. | Cheating on a test is a major no-no. |
| Perfect condition | Move-in ready | A house requiring no repairs. | Buyers nowadays are looking for move-in ready homes. |
| Needs repairs | A fixer (-upper) | A house that requires a lot of work. | We can only afford a fixer-upper. |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- X amount: Say the literal letter "X". You can also say "X, Y, and Z" to refer to a list of unspecified tasks or things.
- Key: When used as an adjective, it does not take an article. Say "Communication is key" (NOT "Communication is a key").

## English Fluency Practice - Buying a House

### Summary

Real estate property metrics distinguish *square footage* (interior living area) from *lot size* (total parcel land). Market conditions and mortgage financing are described using terms such as *inventory*, *lagging*, *holding*, *listings*, *HOA*, *pre-approval*, *underwritten*, and *credit score*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Desirable | Sought after | Highly desired and in demand. | Houses in good school districts are highly sought after. |
| Land size | Lot / Lot size | The area of land a house is built on. | The closer you get to the beach, the smaller the lot. |
| Staying stable | Holding | Prices maintaining their current level. | Real estate prices will continue to hold. |
| Houses for sale | Listings | Properties currently advertised for sale. | There are 14 listings in this neighborhood. |
| Houses available | Inventory | The total supply of houses for sale. | Inventory is low right now. |
| Falling behind | Lagging | Developing or moving more slowly than expected. | Inventory is lagging behind demand. |
| Workable / doable | Feasible | Possible and practical to do. | A townhome is more financially feasible for me. |
| Monthly building fee | HOA (Homeowners Assoc.) | Fee paid for outside maintenance of condos. | What is your top HOA fee budget? |
| Didn't hear | Didn't catch | A polite way to ask someone to repeat their name. | I'm sorry, I didn't catch your name. |
| Financially verified | Underwritten | Bank has deeply researched your financials for a loan. | He put in an offer that has been underwritten. |
| Bank letter | Pre-approval letter | Letter stating you qualify to borrow a certain amount. | I have been pre-approved for $700,000. |
| Polite word for man | Gentleman | Respectful term for a male. | The gentleman outside is another buyer. |
| Submit an offer | Put in an offer | To formally propose buying a house. | We'd like to put in an offer. |
| Ready to go | Ready to rock and roll | Fully prepared to begin an activity. | They're underwritten and ready to rock and roll. |
| Detailed analysis | Deeper dive | A closer, more detailed examination. | We decided to take a deeper dive into the financials. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Important rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Gentleman / Gentlemen | JEN-tul-min | Singular and plural sound identical because the final syllable is unstressed. |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- Get vs. Catch: If someone never told you their name, say "I didn't get your name." If they said it but you didn't hear it, say "I didn't catch your name."
- Pre-approved vs. Underwritten: Pre-approval is a light check; underwriting is a "deeper dive" into a buyer's financials.

## 10 English Expressions for Business

### Summary

Corporate communication utilizes compound nouns like *turnover rate*, *downturn*, and *turnout*, alongside phrasal verbs like *turn down* (reject) and *turn in* (submit). Pronouncing the American 'tur-' prefix requires smoothly blending the /t/ and /r/ without inserting extraneous vowel sounds.

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Important rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Turn | CHURN / T-URN | The "T" before "ur" is pronounced halfway between a "T" and a "CH". |
| Turtle | TER-dul | Start with the "tur" sound, hit a fast flap "D" for the "t", and hold the "L" up. |
| Colleague | CAH-leeg | Stress the first syllable with an open "AH" sound. |

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Employee quit rate | Turnover rate | The percentage of employees leaving a company. | That company has a very high turnover rate. |
| Economic decline | Downturn | A period when business/economy is doing poorly. | There's an increase in unemployment during a downturn. |
| Attendance | Turnout | The number of people who attend an event. | We had a great turnout at the conference. |
| Make money | Turn a profit | To generate financial profit. | They expect to turn a profit next quarter. |
| Reject | Turn down | To refuse an offer or applicant. | I turned down the job offer for a higher salary. |
| Submit | Turn in | To submit a document or item. | Please turn in your paperwork to HR. |
| Resulted / Happened | Turn out (verb) | The unexpected end result. | We hesitated, but it turned out to be a great decision. |
| Improve | Turn a corner | To begin improving after a bad period. | The company turned a corner when they launched the product. |
| Ask for advice | Turn to (someone) | To go to someone for help or support. | When I need help, I turn to my co-worker. |
| Alternate | Take turns | To alternate doing a task. | My colleague and I take turns working on Saturdays. |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- Turnout vs. Turn out: Turnout (noun) is attendance. Turn out (verb) is the result. (e.g., "The turnout was low, but it turned out to be a good meeting.")
- Colleague pronunciation: A very common mistake is stressing the second syllable or using an "o" sound. It is CAH-leeg.

## 10 Common English Proverbs

### Summary

Traditional English proverbs encapsulate cultural truths and moral advice while presenting phonetic challenges. Distinguish the /ɜːr/ vowel across phonetically similar words like *early*, *bird*, *word*, and *worm*, alongside irregular pronunciations such as *leopard* (/ˈlɛp.ərd/) and *iron* (/ˈaɪ.ərn/).

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression / Proverb | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Don't plan too early | Don't count your chickens before they hatch | Wait until you actually have something before making plans based on it. | I might buy a new car if I get the bonus, but I do not want to count my chickens before they hatch. |
| Deeds matter | Actions speak louder than words | What you do is more important than what you say. | You promise you'll finish by Friday, but actions speak louder than words. |
| Be early | The early bird gets the worm | If you wake up or act early, you will be successful. | Let's arrive early to get the best seats; the early bird gets the worm. |
| Jealousy | The grass is always greener on the other side (of the fence) | Other people's lives always seem better than our own. | You think her job is easy, but the grass is always greener on the other side. |
| Don't risk everything | Don't put all your eggs in one basket | Don't put all your resources or efforts into one single place/plan. | Apply for several jobs so you don't put all your eggs in one basket. |
| Looks deceive | You can't judge a book by its cover | Don't judge the value of something simply by its appearance. | He doesn't look impressive at first, but don't judge a book by its cover; he has a PhD. |
| Like parent | The apple doesn't fall far from the tree | A child has a similar character to their parents (often used negatively). | That politician is corrupt, and his father was the same; the apple doesn't fall far from the tree. |
| People don't change | A leopard doesn't change its spots | A person's true character never changes. | Be careful trusting him again; a leopard doesn't change its spots. |
| Act quickly | Strike while the iron is hot | Take advantage of a good opportunity while it lasts. | The value will increase soon; we need to strike while the iron is hot. |
| Don't hurt your provider | Don't bite the hand that feeds you | Don't make someone angry if they are paying or helping you. | I want to yell at my boss, but I shouldn't bite the hand that feeds me. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake | Meaning / natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Word | WURD (rhymes with bird) | WARD / rounding lips | Do not round lips for the "O". "Actions speak louder than words." |
| Leopard | LEP-urd | LEO-pard / LAIR-pard | The "eo" is just an "eh" sound. "A leopard doesn't change its spots." |
| Iron | I-ern | I-ron / I-own | The "r" is pronounced before the "o". "Strike while the iron is hot." |

## 10 Useful Idioms From LA Natives

### Summary

Casual American discourse uses lifestyle idioms like *24/7*, *going on [number]*, and *what's not to love*, with financial figures framed informally as *seven figures* or *drop  million*. In relaxed connected speech, the medial /t/ is frequently elided in numbers like *twenty-four* ('twenny-four').

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| All the time | 24/7 | 24 hours a day, 7 days a week; constantly. | Living on a boat is like being on vacation 24/7. |
| Cut the grass | Mow the lawn | Cutting the grass in front/behind a house. | I don't have to mow the lawn anymore. |
| Almost | Going on | Approaching a certain age or length of time. | I've lived here going on six years. |
| From here | Originally from the area | Born or raised in this specific location. | Are you originally from the area? |
| Finish at | End up | To finish or find yourself in a place unexpectedly. | I stuck a pin in a map and ended up in Arizona. |
| Regret | Look back | To regret a past decision. | I moved from a small town and I've never looked back. |
| Perfect | What's not to love? | A rhetorical question meaning it is perfect; there's nothing to dislike. | It has a big kitchen and it's clean; what's not to love? |
| One million dollars | Seven figures | A number with 7 digits (e.g., $1,000,000+). | They can spend seven figures on a boat. |
| My social group | The circle I run in | The group of people you spend time with. | Rich celebrities? That's not a circle I run in. |
| Spend a lot of money | To drop (money) | Slang for spending a large amount of cash. | He dropped five million dollars to buy a boat. |
| Good income | Fair source of income | A good salary; a lot of money. | You've got to have a fair source of income for that. |

## 12 Advanced Words Instead of "Very"

### Summary

Replace the overused intensifier *very* with precise, high-register adverbs like *woefully*, *exceedingly*, and *utterly*. Pronunciation requires employing a flap /ɾ/ for the medial double 't' in *utterly* and reducing unstressed vowels, such as the 'a' in *particularly* (/pərˈtɪk.jə.lər.li/).

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Very bad/sad | Woefully | Used for something extremely negative or inadequate. | We were woefully unprepared for the test. |
| Very good/difficult | Exceedingly | Better or more than standard. | You did exceedingly well in that class. |
| Very obvious | Undeniably | Absolutely true; impossible to deny. | He is undeniably qualified for the job. |
| Completely | Utterly | Complete or entire (often with extreme feelings). | That is utterly absurd. |
| Very much/suddenly | Dramatically | In a big, sudden, or intense way. | Your English has dramatically improved. |
| Very hard to believe | Unimaginably | Hard to comprehend or imagine. | It is unimaginably hard to do this. |
| Very expensive/hard | Inconceivably | Cannot be conceived or imagined. | The tickets were inconceivably expensive. |
| Very much | Extremely | To a large extent. | The process was extremely straightforward. |
| Very/Especially | Particularly | More than usual (often negative: not particularly). | That's a particularly dangerous job. |
| Very large/different | Vastly | Large in size, amount, or degree. | Humans are vastly more social than other mammals. |
| Very helpful/popular | Tremendously | Great, excellent, or intense. | The new software is tremendously helpful. |
| Very clear | Unmistakably | Cannot be mistaken; obvious. | The change was unmistakably evident. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Utterly | UD-er-ly | Pronouncing hard T's. Use a fast flap "D" instead. |
| Dramatically | dru-MAT-ik-ly | Saying "dra-". The "dr" sounds like "jra" or "dru". |
| Particularly | par-TIC-yu-ler-ly | Saying "par-tic-u-LAR-ly". Reduce the "a" to "er". |
| Vastly | VAST-ly | Use a big open American "A" (like apple). |

## 13 Expressions with "TALK"

### Summary

Distinguish between manipulative personas such as a flattery-driven *sweet talker* and a persuasive, polished *smooth talker*, utilizing both separable and inseparable phrasal verbs with *talk*. Phonetically, words like *talk*, *walk*, and *call* require an open American /ɔː/ or /ɑː/ vowel sound rather than a rounded diphthong.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Persuade to do | Talk into | To convince someone to do something. | The agent talked us into buying the house. |
| Persuade not to do | Talk out of | To convince someone NOT to do something. | His friends talked him out of jumping. |
| Be condescending | Talk down to | To speak to someone as if they are stupid or inferior. | He thinks he's smarter, so he talks down to me. |
| Interrupt / talk loudly | Talk over | To speak while someone else is still talking. | Please stop talking over me and let me finish. |
| Discuss a problem | Talk it over | To discuss a situation to get advice/opinion. | I'd like to talk it over with my wife first. |
| Casual chat | Small talk | Light, informal conversation with strangers. | We engaged in small talk before the interview. |
| Reply rudely | Talk back | To answer disrespectfully (usually children to adults). | I wasn't allowed to talk back to my parents. |
| Empty promises | All talk | Someone who says they'll do things but never does. | She doesn't keep her promises; she's all talk. |
| Words don't matter | Talk is cheap | It's easy to say things; actions matter more. | He said he'd finish, but talk is cheap. |
| Manipulator | Smooth talker | Someone who manipulates using clever words. | Don't trust him; he's a smooth talker. |
| Flatterer | Sweet talker | Someone who says nice things to get what they want. | He tells girls what they want to hear; he's a sweet talker. |
| Talk too much | Talk someone's ear off | To exhaust someone by talking continuously. | Don't ask about politics, he'll talk your ear off. |
| Tired of talking | Talked out | Exhausted from talking too much. | After giving the lecture, I'm all talked out. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Talk | TAHK | The 'L' is silent. Open mouth wide for an American 'AH' sound. |
| Sentences | SEN-ten-sez or SEN-en-sez | Dropping the 'T' to say 'sen-en-sez' is common in fast natural speech. |

## 15 Idioms with "Hit"

### Summary

Common idioms built on *hit* span academic diligence (*hit the books*) to sudden success (*hit the jackpot*). Differentiate the relaxed short /ɪ/ in *hit*, *bit*, and *fit* from the tense /iː/ in *heat*, *beat*, and *feed*, and hold the final unreleased /t/ before consonant-initial words (e.g., *hit the*).

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Exactly right | Hit the nail on the head | To have exactly the correct answer or assessment. | When you said the battery was dead, you hit the nail on the head. |
| Study hard | Hit the books | To begin studying intensely. | If I don't hit the books, I'll fail. |
| Big success | Hit the jackpot | To win money or have a massive, lucky success. | You got the job? You really hit the jackpot! |
| Leave | Hit the road | To depart or leave a place. | We'd better hit the road before traffic gets worse. |
| Get very angry | Hit the ceiling (or roof) | To explode with anger. | Your boss will hit the ceiling when he finds out. |
| Go to sleep | Hit the sack | To go to bed. | I have to wake up early, so I'd better hit the sack. |
| Drink alcohol | Hit the bottle | To start drinking heavily. | He was doing okay, but he started to hit the bottle again. |
| Unfair attack | Hit below the belt | To say something inappropriately personal/cruel. | When he's angry, he hits below the belt. |
| Become famous | Hit it big | To become a massive success. | He was popular here, but he hit it big in New York. |
| Flee an accident | Hit and run | Causing a car accident and driving away illegally. | The hit and run driver was in a blue car. |
| Stop / Slow down | Hit the brakes | To stop doing something. | Let's hit the brakes on this project. |
| Perform perfectly | Hit it out of the park | To do something exceptionally well. | Your presentation was amazing; you hit it out of the park. |
| Flirt | Hit on (someone) | To express romantic interest aggressively. | Was he friendly, or was he hitting on me? |
| Connect instantly | Hit it off | To become friends immediately upon meeting. | We met on a blind date and hit it off right away. |
| A huge success | A hit | Something extremely popular (song, movie, idea). | Your delicious cake will be a hit at the party. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word/Sound | Correct pronunciation | Rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hit vs. Heat | HIT vs. HEET | "Hit" uses a relaxed throat and tongue. "Heat" is tense. |
| Hit the | HI-(stop)-the | If 'T' is followed by a consonant, hold the 'T' (don't release it). |
| Hit it | HID-it | If 'T' is followed by a vowel, it becomes a fast flap 'D'. |

## Describing a Beautiful Place

### Summary

Nuanced aesthetic descriptions employ precise adjectives like *stunning*, *captivating*, and *picturesque*, contrasting feelings of high-energy excitement (*exhilarated*) with calm invitation. Watch irregular pronunciations: *extraordinary* drops the 'a' (/ɪkˈstrɔːr.dən.er.i/), while *ancient* pronounces 'ci' as an /ʃ/ sound.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Very beautiful | Stunning | Extremely impressive or beautiful. | You look stunning in that dress. |
| Excitingly beautiful | Spectacular | Beautiful in a dramatic, impressive way. | We got a hotel room with a spectacular view. |
| Mixed styles | Eclectic | A combination of different styles/elements. | He has eclectic taste in music. |
| Very special | Extraordinary | Unusual, not ordinary, exceptional. | You're making extraordinary progress in English. |
| Beautiful views | Scenic | Having beautiful or impressive natural scenery. | Let's take the scenic road on the way home. |
| Balanced perfectly | Symmetrical | Both sides are exactly the same or balanced. | The four buildings are perfectly symmetrical. |
| Very detailed | Intricate | Very complicated with fine details. | Her wedding dress has intricate details. |
| Very exciting | Exhilarating | Making you feel happy, excited, and alive. | The roller coaster ride was exhilarating. |
| Welcoming | Warm and inviting | Making you feel happy and welcome to be there. | You decorated your living room in a warm and inviting way. |
| Exact | Precise | Very accurate and paying attention to detail. | Please make sure your measurement is precise. |
| Crowded & noisy | Bustling | Noisy and crowded with a lot of energetic people. | New York is a bustling city. |
| Immediately noticeable | Striking | Noticeable because it is so beautiful or amazing. | She is strikingly beautiful. |
| Holds your attention | Captivating | So interesting and charming you can't look away. | The architecture and views are truly captivating. |
| Like a picture | Picturesque | Visually attractive, especially in a quaint way. | We visited a picturesque little town. |
| Very old | Ancient | From a very long time ago in history. | Jerusalem is an ancient city. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Extraordinary | ex-TROR-di-ner-ee | Do not say "extra-ordinary". The first 'a' is completely silent. |
| Ancient | AYN-shent | The 'c' sounds like an 'sh', and the 'a' is a long 'ay'. |

## 17 Expressions with "Pick"

### Summary

Prepositional shifts alter the meaning of *pick*, differentiating *pick at* (eat listlessly), *pick on* (harass), and *pick over* (scrutinize), while *pick up* spans paying a bill, acquiring a skill, contracting an illness, improving mood, or resuming an activity. Phonetically, preserve the short, relaxed /ɪ/ in *pick* to prevent confusion with the tense /iː/ in *peak*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Bully / criticize | Pick on | To bully or treat someone unfairly. | Why do you always pick on me when something goes wrong? |
| Eat without interest | Pick at | To play with food without eating much. | She's not hungry; she's just picking at her food. |
| Examine carefully | Pick over | To examine items to reject the bad ones. | The customers had already picked over all the sale items. |
| Analyze flaws | Pick apart | To deeply analyze something looking for flaws. | The lawyer picked apart the witness's testimony. |
| Recover from failure | Pick yourself up | To recover emotionally/physically after a setback. | Life can knock you down, but you must pick yourself up. |
| Resume an activity | Pick back up | To resume something after stopping. | Let's pick back up where we left off. |
| Romantic intro line | Pickup line | A sentence used to flirt with a stranger. | She laughed at his funny pickup line. |
| Pay the bill | Pick up the tab / check | To pay the bill for everyone in a group. | I'm going to go ahead and pick up the check. |
| Tidy a room | Pick up (a room) | To organize and clean up a space. | Make sure you pick up your bedroom. |
| Give someone a ride | Pick up (a person) | To collect someone in your car. | I'll pick up the children after school. |
| Get arrested | Pick up (by police) | To be apprehended or caught by police. | He was picked up by the police for questioning. |
| Flirt / get a date | Pick up (someone) | To start a romantic conversation with a stranger. | He goes to that club to pick up girls. |
| Improve mood | Pick-me-up | Something that makes you feel energetic or happy. | My morning coffee is a great pick-me-up. |
| Learn by exposure | Pick up (a skill) | To learn something informally by being around it. | I picked up Spanish while traveling in Mexico. |
| Catch an illness | Pick up (a virus) | To catch an infection or illness. | I must have picked up the flu from my grandma's house. |
| Increase intensity | Pick up (wind/convo) | To become stronger or more intense. | The wind has really picked up. |
| Detect/notice | Pick up on | To notice a feeling or clue through observation. | I picked up on the tension in the room. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pick vs. Peak | PIHK vs. PEEK | "Pick" requires a relaxed tongue (short 'ih'). "Peak" requires a tense tongue (long 'ee'). |
| Picked up | PIKT-up | The 'ed' sounds like a 'T', which connects directly to the 'u' in 'up'. |

## 17 Expressions During a Crisis

### Summary

Navigating workplace disruption requires precise employment terminology like *give notice*, *file for unemployment*, *accrue PTO*, and *cash in*. Describe sequential timelines using *every other*, *in a row*, or *going on*, and frame psychological responses with idioms such as *panic mode*, *spare time on my hands*, and *see the light at the end of the tunnel*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Tell in advance | Give notice | Informing an employer you are leaving (usually 2 weeks). | They gave us a one-day notice. |
| Eat at restaurant | Dine in | Eating food inside the restaurant. | We prefer to dine in, but we have to order takeout. |
| Look at | Check out | To look at, observe, or notice something. | Check out that beautiful sunset. |
| Alternating | Every other | Skipping one in between (e.g., Monday, Wed, Fri). | I exercise every other day. |
| Without stopping | In a row | Doing something consecutively without breaks. | I exercised six days in a row. |
| Customers/business | Traffic | The volume of people visiting a store or website. | Our restaurant traffic slowed down quite a bit. |
| Apply for benefits | File for unemployment | Submitting paperwork to get paid when you lose a job. | He lost his job, so he needs to file for unemployment. |
| Accumulate | Accrue | To grow or accumulate over time (usually money or time off). | I have 50 hours of accrued PTO. |
| Exchange for money | Cash in | To receive money for something you give/have saved. | If I cash that in, that's what I'll get. |
| Approximately | Roughly | About, approximately. | It takes roughly two hours to get there. |
| Dollars (slang) | Bucks | Casual slang for dollars. | They'll send you 500 bucks a week. |
| Not very | Not that [adjective] | A casual way to say "not so much" or "not very." | Is it painful? It's not that bad. |
| State of panic | In panic mode | The current condition or state of feeling intense fear. | Everyone is in panic mode right now. |
| Extra free time | Spare time on my hands | Having extra time available to you currently. | Everyone has a lot of spare time on their hands. |
| Have hope | See the light at the end of the tunnel | Having hope that a bad situation will soon end. | They finally see the light at the end of the tunnel. |
| Exactly the same | No exception / no different | A stronger way to say "not an exception." | This situation is no exception. |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- Two-week notice: Do not say "two weeks notice." "Notice" is the noun, so "two-week" acts as an adjective and is singular.
- Every other: Do not say "every second day." Say "every other day."

## Advanced Words From News Headlines

### Summary

Journalistic headlines use punchy metaphorical verbs like *derail*, *overshadow*, *echo*, and *cloud*, alongside evaluative adjectives such as *fragile*, *somber*, and *scarce*. Distinguish phonetically similar verb pairs like *seize* (take by force) and *cease* (stop).

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Take by force | Seize | To capture or control by force/law (pronounced with Z). | The police seized the stolen goods. |
| Stop | Cease | To stop doing something (pronounced with S). | The noise finally ceased. |
| Temporary stop of war | Ceasefire | A temporary pause in fighting. | Both sides agreed to a ceasefire. |
| Permanent stop of war | Truce | A longer or permanent agreement to stop fighting. | They called a truce after years of conflict. |
| Lessened / quieted | Muted | Made quieter, less intense, or less noticeable. | The joy is muted by doubt over long-term peace. |
| Thinking carefully | Weighing options | Thinking carefully about choices before deciding. | She weighed the pros and cons before buying. |
| Ruin a plan | Derail | To cause a plan or career to fail unexpectedly. | The bad weather derailed their travel plans. |
| Tax on imports | Tariff | A specific tax placed on imported/exported goods. | The government imposed new tariffs on steel. |
| Make less noticeable | Overshadow | To make something seem less important by comparison. | The scandal overshadowed his career success. |
| Repeat/Agree | Echo | To repeat or agree with someone's words or ideas. | Her concerns echoed those of the public. |
| Make unclear | Cloud | To make something difficult to understand or see. | Fear is clouding your judgment. |
| Exceed | Top | To go over a certain amount or expectation. | The damage topped $1 million. |
| Very serious/sad | Somber | Very serious, gloomy, or depressing. | The mood in the room was somber after the bad news. |
| Lack of something | Scarcity / Scarce | Not enough of something. | There is a scarcity of clean water. |
| Took a long time | Years in the making | Something that took years to develop or happen. | The new policy was years in the making. |
| Block / prevent | Stymie | To block or prevent the progress of something. | Health problems stymied his career growth. |
| Rule / control | Reign | To rule, dominate, or be in control (spelled r-e-i-g-n). | Confusion reigned during the emergency. |
| Threatening to happen | Loom | To appear large, threatening, and likely to happen soon. | An economic crisis is looming. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Seize vs Cease | SEEZ vs. SEES | The 'z' vibrates; the 's' does not. |
| Fragile | FRA-jul | In American English, it ends in "jul". In British English, it ends in "jile" (like mile). |
| Reign | RAYN | The 'g' is completely silent. |
| Uncertainty | un-SER-ten-tee | Hold the 'T's; do not drop them. |

## 20 Business English Expressions

### Summary

Elevate corporate speech by replacing colloquial phrases with formal business descriptors like *subpar* (poor quality) and *high grossing* (lucrative). Project management and operational milestones rely on idioms and prepositions such as *sign off on*, *up in the air*, *get going*, *pay up front*, and *move up in the company*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Below average | Subpar | Not as good as expected; poor quality. | The quality of that work is subpar. |
| Area of specialty | In the field | Working in a specific industry or profession. | How long have you been in the field? |
| Right skills | Skill set | A specific group of skills for a job. | He doesn't have the skill set for the job. |
| Get a promotion | Move one's way up | To get promoted within the same company. | He moved his way up in the company. |
| Urgent | Time sensitive | Needing to be done right away; cannot wait. | This document is time sensitive. |
| Make understood | Get my point across | To successfully communicate an idea to others. | Did I get my point across clearly? |
| Makes lots of money | High grossing | Generating significant financial revenue. | That is a high grossing store. |
| Approve | Sign off on | To officially approve a document or budget. | Did the boss sign off on the budget? |
| Pay in advance | Up front | Paying before the work begins. | You need to pay 50% up front. |
| Spend less | Cut costs | To figure out ways to reduce business expenses. | Let's figure out a way to cut costs. |
| Become successful | Taking off | Suddenly becoming very successful or popular. | Their business is really taking off. |
| Existed | Been around | Having existed for a certain amount of time. | Our company has been around for 30 years. |
| Good social skills | People skills | The ability to communicate well with others. | He's a good consultant because he has people skills. |
| Undecided | Up in the air | Uncertain; a plan that is not yet finalized. | The plan to start construction is up in the air. |
| Don't change plans | Stick to (our plans) | To continue with the original plan. | Let's stick to our original plans. |
| Cancel a commitment | Back out of | To withdraw from an agreement or project. | They backed out of the project. |
| Start | Get going | To begin doing something without delay. | Let's get going on the project. |
| Tell boss I'm quitting | Give notice | Informing an employer you will leave. | I gave my two-week notice yesterday. |
| Skip one | Every other | Alternating (e.g., working Monday, skipping Tuesday). | I work every other Saturday. |
| Making no progress | Not getting anywhere | Failing to improve or make progress. | We're arguing but we're not getting anywhere. |
| Making progress | Getting somewhere | Finally improving or making progress. | We are finally getting somewhere. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Phrase | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Point across | POIN-uh-cross | The 't' in 'point' is dropped when speaking quickly. |
| You'd cut costs | YOOD cut costs | "You would" contracts to "You'd". |
| Two-week notice | TWO-week notice | Do not say "two weeks". It acts as an adjective here. |

## 20 Executive Business Expressions

### Summary

Executive communication emphasizes soft skills and emotional intelligence through expressions like *get triggered*, *get in the way of*, and *innately*. Professional trajectory and workplace alignment are articulated using terms like *go by*, *make a shift*, *get on the same page*, and *going through the motions*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Job name | Job title | The official name of your job position. | What is your job title? |
| Also called | Go by | The name/title you use or prefer to be called. | I go by the term 'business coach'. |
| Prevent | Get in the way of | To prevent something from happening or succeeding. | Don't let emotions get in the way of your goals. |
| Naturally | Innately | Instinctively; something you are born knowing how to do. | People don't innately know how to do this. |
| Express carefully | Phrase (something) | To express something in a particular way. | Be careful how you phrase your criticism. |
| Become highly emotional | Get triggered | To have a strong emotional reaction (anger/fear) to something. | If I'm very direct, they might get triggered. |
| Take control | Take over | To assume control of a situation. | When we get emotional, the amygdala takes over. |
| Disconnected | Offline | Disconnected; not functioning or available. | It takes our executive brain function offline. |
| Good judgment | Common sense | The ability to think logically and practically. | All our common sense goes away when emotional. |
| Communicate | Deliver a message | To express yourself or communicate information. | He doesn't know how to deliver a message effectively. |
| Agree / align | Get on the same page | To have the same understanding or agreement. | It's important to get on the same page. |
| Interpersonal skills | Soft skills | Non-technical skills (communication, leadership). | This falls into the category of soft skills. |
| Working without care | Going through the motions | Doing a job without effort or interest. | He doesn't care; he's just going through the motions. |
| Getting promoted | Moving up | Getting promotions inside a company. | He is moving up through the company rapidly. |
| Change / transition | Make a shift | To change from one position or mindset to another. | You have to make the shift from expert to leader. |
| Get involved in | Get into | To enter a situation (often a difficult one). | They do not know what they are getting into. |
| Smart / intelligent | Sharp | Very intelligent or quick-witted. | You'll learn these skills quickly because you're sharp. |
| This is your role | Where you come in | The point where your specific role or help is needed. | It's not enough, and that's where you come in. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Phrase | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Entrepreneur | ahn-truh-pruh-NUR | Saying it with an "en" sound instead of the French "ahn". |
| How to | HOW-doo / HAR-oo | Fast speech turns the 't' into a soft 'd' or flap 'r'. |
| Conflict (noun) | CON-flict | Stress the first syllable for the noun. |
| Conflict (verb) | con-FLICT | Stress the second syllable for the verb. |

## 20 Conversational Expressions

### Summary

Natural conversational English describes interpersonal rapport using phrases like *people person*, *hit it off*, and *make conversation*. Personal pursuits and transitions use constructions like *big [enthusiast]*, *got into*, and *ended up*, supported by vivid adjectives like *absolutely massive*, *choppy*, and *year-round*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Have the chance to | Get to | Having the opportunity to do something enjoyable. | I get to meet people from all over the country. |
| Very social person | People person | An extrovert who naturally enjoys talking to others. | You need to be a good people person. |
| Small talk | Make conversation | Speaking to strangers to be polite or friendly. | He has a talent for making conversation with strangers. |
| Finally | Lastly | Used to introduce a final point (instead of "in conclusion"). | And lastly, you need to know how to park a boat. |
| Connect immediately | Hit it off | Becoming friendly with someone the moment you meet. | Him and I hit it off immediately. |
| Huge fan/enthusiast | A big (noun) | Being very passionate or interested in an activity. | I'm a big snowboarder. |
| Resulted in | Ended up (+ing) | How a situation finished or resulted. | We ended up going out to lunch together. |
| Become interested | Get into | Becoming passionate or involved in a new hobby. | In college, I got really into cliff jumping. |
| Very large | Absolutely massive | Huge; incredibly large. | Lake Tahoe is absolutely massive. |
| Rough waves | Choppy | Having many small, rough waves (or disconnected speech). | The lake gets choppy in the afternoons. |
| Number two | The second (+est) | The item immediately following the absolute most/best. | It's the second deepest lake in the US. |
| Yes (casual) | Yup | A very common, casual way to say "yes". | Yup, we have the cleanest water. |
| All year | Year-round | Happening throughout the entire year. | The homes are lived in year-round. |
| Vacation | To vacation | Used as a verb: to spend a holiday somewhere. | They started wanting to vacation in Lake Tahoe. |
| Short trip | Getaway | A short, spontaneous trip or escape. | It's a close getaway for San Francisco residents. |
| Crazy / unbelievable | Insane | Crazy or unbelievable (often relating to price/events). | 75 million dollars? That's insane! |
| Negative aspect | Downside (to) | A bad or negative aspect of a situation. | The one downside to living there is the heat. |
| Many | Multiple | More than one; many. | We had multiple 100-degree days. |
| At least that | If not more | Meaning "at least that much, possibly more." | We had 100 days over 100 degrees, if not more. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Phrase | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Get to | GET-too | Make sure to link the words smoothly without pausing. |

## 20 Modern American Slang Words

### Summary

Modern American informal slang characterizes atmosphere and awareness with terms like *chillin'*, *lit*, and *woke*, while negative emotions are described using *salty*, *shook*, and *done with it*. Digital communication relies on internet slang like *ghosting*, *canceled*, and hyperbole such as *I'm dead*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Relaxing | Chillin' | Relaxing, hanging out, having a good vibe. | Everything's going good; I've just been chillin'. |
| Perfect / flawless | On fleek | Looking perfect (often used for eyebrows/hair/outfits). | Your eyebrows are on fleek. |
| Bitter / upset | Salty | Being upset or bitter over something minor. | He was salty because he lost the game. |
| Insult subtly | Throw shade | Making a subtle, indirect insult about someone. | He was throwing shade at me in front of the boss. |
| Amazing party | Lit | Exciting, fun, or amazing (usually describing an event). | Is that party going to be lit? |
| Show off | Flex | To show off your wealth, body, or belongings. | He's just flexing his new car. |
| Scared / shocked | Shook | Extremely shocked, surprised, or scared. | When the dog fell in the water, he was shook. |
| Give up | I'm done with it | To be completely finished or over a situation. | I can't take this anymore; I'm done with it. |
| Socially aware | Woke | Being aware of social injustices, lies, or truth. | He's very woke; he doesn't believe the media. |
| Ignore suddenly | Ghost (someone) | To abruptly cut off all communication with someone. | I was texting him, and then I got ghosted. |
| Laughing very hard | I'm dead / weak | Laughing so hard that you feel "dead" or "weak". | That meme was so funny; I'm dead. |
| Dismiss someone | Bye Felicia | A dismissive way to tell someone annoying to leave. | You're annoying me; Bye Felicia. |
| Do perfectly | Slay | To do something incredibly well; to look amazing. | You slayed that outfit! |
| Greatest Of All Time | GOAT | Acronym for Greatest Of All Time (sports/music). | Michael Jordan is the GOAT. |
| Boycott socially | Canceled | Publicly rejecting someone for doing something wrong. | He said something terrible, so he is canceled. |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- Shook: This is grammatically incorrect (the proper word is "shaken"), but "shook" is the accepted slang term.
- Ghost: Usually a noun, but in modern slang, it is used as a verb ("I ghosted him" / "I was ghosted").

## 20 Ways to Use "Deal"

### Summary

The word *deal* spans commercial agreements (*close the deal*, *done deal*), financial bargains (*a good deal*, *sweeten the deal*), and situational complications (*deal with it*, *deal breaker*, *no big deal*). Context determines whether it functions as a transaction, an incentive, or an emotional coping mechanism.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| I agree | It's a deal | An expression used to finalize an agreement. | You want $5,000? Okay, it's a deal. |
| Let's negotiate | Let's make a deal | An invitation to negotiate and compromise. | Let's make a deal; how about $5,500? |
| A good price | A good/great deal | Buying something at a very favorable price. | You got a really good deal on that car. |
| A lot of | A great deal of | A large amount of something. | He has a great deal of money. |
| Finalized | A done deal | An agreement that is completely finalized. | After you sign the contract, it's a done deal. |
| Manipulate for profit | Wheel and deal | Dishonestly manipulating business for self-profit. | He made his money by wheeling and dealing. |
| Manipulative seller | Wheeler dealer | A salesperson who is dishonest and manipulative. | Stay away from him; he's a wheeler dealer. |
| Make an offer better | Sweeten the deal | Adding something extra to make an offer attractive. | The seller sweetened the deal by leaving the furniture. |
| Reason to cancel | Deal breaker | A specific issue that causes a negotiation/relationship to end. | Not wanting kids was a deal breaker for her. |
| Finalize the contract | Close the deal | To reach a final agreement in business. | We finally closed the deal after months of talks. |
| Suspicious agreement | Shady deal | An agreement that seems dishonest or illegal. | He was involved in many shady deals. |
| Not a problem | No big deal | Something that is not inconvenient or not serious. | It's no big deal; I can drive you to the airport. |
| Don't overreact | Don't make a big deal out of it | Advising someone not to turn a small issue into a crisis. | Relax, don't make a big deal out of it. |
| Handle a situation | Deal with (something) | To handle, manage, or accept a difficult situation. | You need to learn to deal with difficult people. |
| Distribute cards | Deal cards | Giving out playing cards in a game. | It's your turn to deal the cards. |
| Car seller | Car dealer / dealership | The person or place that sells cars. | There is a Toyota dealership near my house. |
| Authentic/excellent | The real deal | A person or thing that is genuine, authentic, and excellent. | That jazz musician is amazing; he's the real deal. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Deal | DEE-ul | Do not abruptly stop the tongue. Use a dark 'L' and a prolonged vowel. |
| Salesman | SAYLZ-mun | Reduce the "man" sound to "mun." |

## 20 Advanced Pronunciation Fixes

### Summary

Advanced phonetic accuracy requires suppressing silent letters in *salmon* (silent 'l'), *bomb* (silent 'b'), and *extraordinary* (silent 'a'). Recognize irregular consonant values like the /k/ sound for 'ch' in *chaos* and *choir*, the /ts/ sound in *pizza*, and note the four-syllable American *aluminum* versus the five-syllable British *aluminium*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake / spelling trick |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Broccoli | BRAH-klee | The second 'o' is completely silent. |
| Salmon | SA-mun | The 'l' is completely silent. |
| Aluminum | uh-LOO-mi-num | American spelling ends in -um. British is -ium (aluminium). |
| Chaos | KAY-ahs | The 'ch' makes a 'K' sound. |
| Bombing | BAH-ming | The second 'b' is completely silent. |
| Choir | KWY-er | The 'ch' makes a 'K' sound, followed by a 'W' sound. |
| Close (verb) | CLOZE | Used as a verb, the 's' vibrates like a 'z'. |
| Close (adj) | CLOCE | Used as an adjective, the 's' is soft like a snake. |
| Extraordinary | ex-TROR-di-ner-ee | The first 'a' is completely silent. |
| Foreign | FOR-in | The 'g' is silent, and the ending is reduced to "in." |
| Heir | AIR | The 'h' is completely silent. (Female: heiress). |
| Hierarchy | HY-er-ar-kee | Pronounce the "r" clearly in the middle. |
| Lease | LEES | Rhymes with "peace," not "please." |
| Pizza | PEET-suh | The 'zz' makes a 'ts' sound. |
| Protein | PRO-teen | The "ei" makes a single "ee" sound. |
| Series | SEE-reez | Do not confuse with "serious." |
| Southern | SUH-thern | The "ou" makes an "uh" sound, unlike "South." |
| Thermometer | ther-MAH-mi-ter | The stress falls on the second syllable ("MOM"). |
| Of | UV | The 'f' sounds like a 'v'. |
| Receipt | re-SEET | The 'p' is completely silent. |
| Recipe | REH-si-pee | The 'e' at the end is pronounced as "ee." |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- Series vs Serious: Series (SEE-reez) is a noun for a TV show. Serious (SEER-ee-us) is an adjective for a grave situation.
- Of vs Off: Of uses a soft 'v' sound (UV). Off uses a hard 'f' sound (AWF).

## 21 Professional Expressions

### Summary

Professional business communication utilizes networking and discourse idioms such as *reach out blind*, *reiterate*, and *have a word with you*. Long-term career resilience and outcomes are framed with terms like *recession-proof*, *people skills*, *nest egg*, *pan out*, and *go with the flow*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Contact without knowing | Reach out blind | To contact someone without any prior knowledge or connection to them. | One of the most difficult aspects is having to reach out blind. |
| Accept the situation | Go with the flow | To accept a situation without trying to change or control it. | You have to be flexible and go with the flow. |
| Economy-safe | Recession-proof | Not affected by a bad economy. | The medical field is almost recession-proof. |
| The absolute max | As [adj] as it gets | The maximum level of something. | It's about as stable as it can get. |
| Social abilities | People skills | The ability to communicate and interact well with others. | You need both technical background and people skills. |
| Be defensive | Have a guard up | To be cautious and protect oneself from getting hurt or manipulated. | They are skeptical and usually have a guard up. |
| Work out / happen | Pan out | To happen or develop in a certain way (often used negatively). | My plan to go to optometry school didn't pan out. |
| Plus extra | And then some | And even more; in addition to what was already mentioned. | Doctors go to medical school, and then some. |
| Between 30 and 40 | Thirty-something | Between the ages of 30 and 40. | I didn't want to be thirty-something when I graduated. |
| Somewhat small | Smallish | Quite small; kind of small. | I came from a smallish town in Ohio. |
| Except for | Apart from | Besides; other than that. | Apart from the weather, the biggest difference is the diversity. |
| Say again | Reiterate | To say something again to emphasize it. | Let me reiterate the importance of this meeting. |
| To such an extent | So much so that | To a very great extent. | There are beautiful views, so much so that I take pictures constantly. |
| Traffic light | Stoplight | A red, yellow, and green light that controls traffic. | I come to a stoplight and just appreciate the view. |
| Amaze someone | Take one's breath away | To be extremely beautiful, amazing, or spectacular. | The view took my breath away. |
| Imagine your future | See yourself doing | To imagine your future goals or career. | What do you see yourself doing in five years? |
| Successful future | Bright future ahead of you | Having a strong likelihood of success in the future. | You work hard, so you have a bright future ahead of you. |
| Savings for later | Nest egg | Money saved for the future, especially for retirement. | I want to build up a nest egg. |
| Smart people agree | Great minds think alike | A proverb used when two people have the same thought or idea. | We had the same idea-great minds think alike! |

### Usage Notes And Common Mistakes

- -ish suffix: The suffix -ish can be added to numbers, times, and adjectives to mean "approximately" or "kind of." Examples: four-ish (around 4 o'clock), forty-ish (around 40 years old), brownish (kind of brown).
- Stoplight: Interchangeable with traffic light and red light.

## 22 Conversational Expressions in Context

### Summary

Major life transitions and independent initiatives are expressed through idioms like *start from scratch*, *fend for myself*, and *trial and error*. Conversational narratives describe emotional reactions using *freak out* and *wholeheartedly*, while urban driving introduces terms like *split lanes*, *road rage*, and *get out of your way*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Bold decision | Power move | An important, confident action that has a big impact. | I made a power move and moved to California. |
| Old, damaged | Beat up | Old, damaged, or in poor condition (often describing a car). | I bought an old, beat up minivan. |
| Start from nothing | Start from scratch | To start over completely from the beginning. | The business failed, so we had to start from scratch. |
| Take care of oneself | Fend for oneself | To take care of yourself without help or support. | Since my parents passed away, I've had to fend for myself. |
| Panic | Freak out | To panic, get scared, or lose emotional control. | Don't freak out, but we have a big problem. |
| Trying and failing | Trial and error | Finding a solution by trying methods and learning from mistakes. | I learned how to build websites through trial and error. |
| Almost entirely | Pretty much | Almost; nearly. | We're pretty much finished with the project. |
| Determine to do | Put one's mind to | To decide to do something and try very hard to achieve it. | You can do anything if you put your mind to it. |
| Completely | Wholeheartedly | Without hesitation; completely; with your whole heart. | I agree with you wholeheartedly. |
| Motivate / cause | Lead (led) to | To motivate or cause someone to do something. | What led you to move to Los Angeles? |
| Travel around | Get around | To travel or move from place to place. | It's hard to get around LA without a car. |
| Recreational Vehicle | RV | A large vehicle you can live or sleep in (camper/motorhome). | I sold my car and my RV. |
| Unique | Like none other | Very special; unique; incomparable. | The feeling of freedom on a motorcycle is like none other. |
| Move aside | Get out of your way | To move out of someone's path so they can pass. | In LA, drivers will actually get out of your way. |
| Driving lines | Lanes | The divisions on a highway/road for traffic. | It's legal for motorcycles to split lanes in California. |
| Hit lightly | Bump into | To hit something very lightly (like a car fender). | Someone bumped into my car in the parking lot. |
| Angry driving | Road rage | Extreme anger and aggressive behavior between drivers. | There's a lot of road rage in big cities like New York. |
| Continuously all year | All year long | Continuously throughout the entire year. | You can ride a motorcycle all year long in California. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Phrase | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Wholeheartedly | hole-HAR-tid-lee | Don't forget the 'ed' sound in the middle. |
| Cost (past tense) | COST | Do NOT say "costed." |

## 25 Essential Business Idioms

### Summary

Corporate discourse relies on conventional idioms for project lifecycles: initiating actions (*get the ball rolling*, *green light*, *pull the trigger*), addressing complications (*elephant in the room*, *put out fires*, *drop the ball*), and balancing financial and strategic priorities (*bottom line*, *big picture*, *cross the t's and dot the i's*).

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Think creatively | Think outside the box | To think in an original, creative way, not limited by rules. | We need to think outside the box to solve this. |
| Most advanced | Cutting edge | The newest, most advanced, or innovative technology/design. | They are on the cutting edge of new technology. |
| Estimate | Ballpark figure | An approximate estimate; not an exact number. | Just give me a ballpark figure of the cost. |
| Start | Get the ball rolling | To start a process or project. | Let's get the ball rolling on this new campaign. |
| Main point | The bottom line | The final result; the most important aspect or main point. | The bottom line is we don't have the budget. |
| Risk everything on one plan | Put all your eggs in one basket | To rely entirely on one option or strategy. | Apply to multiple jobs; don't put all your eggs in one basket. |
| In agreement | On the same page | To be in agreement and working towards the same goal. | I just want to make sure we're on the same page. |
| Do more than required | Go the extra mile | To put in extra effort beyond what is expected. | She always goes the extra mile for her clients. |
| Pay attention to details | Cross the t's and dot the i's | To pay careful attention to every small detail. | The lawyers are crossing the t's and dotting the i's. |
| Obvious ignored issue | Elephant in the room | An obvious problem or issue that everyone is ignoring. | We need to address the elephant in the room. |
| Exactly right | Hit the nail on the head | To identify something accurately; to be exactly right. | Your analysis really hit the nail on the head. |
| Check everything | Cover all the bases | To do everything necessary to ensure success. | We need to cover all the bases to avoid mistakes. |
| Decide as we go | Play it by ear | To make decisions as a situation develops, without a firm plan. | Let's just play it by ear tomorrow. |
| Try to keep up | Play catch up | To try to reach the same level as competitors, or meet deadlines. | We fell behind, so now we're playing catch up. |
| Handle urgent crises | Put out fires | To deal with urgent problems or crises constantly. | I spent the whole morning putting out fires. |
| Get permission | Get the green light | To get approval or permission to start something. | We finally got the green light to launch the app. |
| Undecided | Up in the air | Uncertain; not yet determined or decided. | Our vacation plans are still up in the air. |
| Get distracted | Go down the rabbit hole | To get lost in details or distractions (often online). | I went down a rabbit hole researching that topic. |
| Easy success | Pick low-hanging fruit | To pursue the easiest tasks or opportunities first. | Let's pick the low-hanging fruit to boost revenue. |
| Get fired | Get the axe | To lose your job; to be fired. | He was shocked when he got the axe yesterday. |
| Make a bad mistake | Drop the ball | To make a mistake or miss an opportunity at a crucial moment. | We really dropped the ball on that client presentation. |
| Very busy | Have a lot on my plate | To have many responsibilities, tasks, or problems. | I can't help you right now; I have a lot on my plate. |
| Take shortcuts | Cut corners | To reduce quality, time, or cost in a way that harms the result. | The builders cut corners, and now the roof leaks. |
| Overall situation | The big picture | The overall perspective or broad situation. | You need to step back and look at the big picture. |
| In the future | Going forward | From this point onward in the future. | Going forward, we will require weekly reports. |
| Make the final decision | Pull the trigger | To commit to a course of action; make a final decision. | It's time to pull the trigger on this investment. |

## 25 "Out Of" Idioms

### Summary

In fast connected speech, *out of* reduces to /aʊt əv/ or informal 'outta' (/aʊtə/). The phrase anchors diverse idiomatic expressions describing functional or physical deficiency (*out of order*, *out of breath*, *out of shape*) as well as informational and situational states (*out of the blue*, *out of the loop*, *out of the picture*).

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Idiom | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Out of the question | Impossible; unacceptable. | Buying a new car is out of the question. |
| Out of the blue | Suddenly and unexpectedly. | He called me out of the blue after ten years. |
| Out of line | Behaving inappropriately or disrespectfully. | Your comments at the meeting were out of line. |
| Out of my league | Someone/something that is superior to you. | I can't date her; she is totally out of my league. |
| Out of nowhere | Suddenly appearing unexpectedly. | That truck came out of nowhere! |
| Out of order | Broken; not working correctly. | The elevator is out of order. |
| Out of place | Feeling uncomfortable or not fitting in. | I wore a suit to the beach and felt very out of place. |
| Out of shape | Not physically fit; unhealthy. | I need to go to the gym; I'm out of shape. |
| Out of the loop | Not informed; not having the information others have. | Nobody told me about the meeting; I'm out of the loop. |
| Out of breath | Difficulty breathing after heavy exercise. | I ran up the stairs and now I'm out of breath. |
| Out of character | Behaving differently than normal personality. | It was completely out of character for him to yell. |
| Out of the way | Inconvenient; far away. | That restaurant is a bit out of the way. |
| Out of this world | Amazing; fantastic. | Her mother's cooking is out of this world. |
| Out of touch | Not communicating anymore. | We've been out of touch since college. |
| Out of turn | Speaking when you have no right or permission to. | I'm sorry, I spoke out of turn. |
| Out of pocket | Paying with your own money (not insurance/company). | I had to pay $500 out of pocket for the repairs. |
| Out of the ordinary | Strange; unusual; different. | Did you notice anything out of the ordinary? |
| Out of your mind | Crazy; insane. | You must be out of your mind to quit that job. |
| Out of practice | Losing skill due to lack of recent practice. | I used to speak French well, but I'm out of practice. |
| Out of the picture | No longer involved in a situation or relationship. | Her ex-boyfriend is finally out of the picture. |
| Out of my budget | Too expensive. | That house is out of my budget. |
| Out of my hands | Beyond my control; I cannot change it. | I wish I could hire you, but the decision is out of my hands. |
| Out of the frying pan into the fire | Going from a bad situation to an even worse one. | Quitting now would be jumping out of the frying pan into the fire. |
| Out of control | Chaotic; unmanageable. | The wild party got completely out of control. |
| Out of [something] | Having no more left (time, gas, milk). | We need to go to the store; we're out of milk. |

### Pronunciation Guide

| Phrase | Correct pronunciation | Slang pronunciation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Out of | OUT-uv (Fast 'D' sound for the T, 'V' sound for the F) | OW-duh (Outta) |

## 27 Expressions with the Word "Word"

### Summary

The noun *word* anchors idioms of commitment and trust (*give you my word*, *man of his word*, *take it to the bank*) alongside confrontation and distortion (*break your word*, *have a word with you*, *put words in my mouth*). Complementary advanced descriptors include *connotation*, *outlandish*, *rowdy*, and *in awe*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions

| Simple phrase | Advanced expression | Meaning / usage | Natural example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| I promise | I give you my word | Promising someone that what you are saying is true. | I'll pay you back tomorrow, I give you my word. |
| Reliable man | Man of his word | A person who keeps their promises and can be trusted. | You can trust him; he is a man of his word. |
| Break a promise | Break your word | Failing to do what you promised to do. | You promised you'd help me; don't break your word. |
| Assume wrongly | Put words in my mouth | Claiming someone said something they didn't actually say. | I didn't say I hated the movie; don't put words in my mouth. |
| Negative meaning | Negative connotation | A negative feeling or idea associated with a word/phrase. | That phrase has a very negative connotation. |
| Strange / bizarre | Outlandish | Looking or sounding very strange, bizarre, or unusual. | The politician made some outlandish claims. |
| Final argument | Have the last word | Having to say the final thing in an argument to feel you won. | My sister is stubborn and always has to have the last word. |
| Speak privately | Have a word with you | Speaking in private, usually about something serious/negative. | My boss said, "Can I have a word with you in my office?" |
| Recommend | Put in a good word | Recommending someone positively to a person in power. | I'll put in a good word for you with the manager. |
| Retail store | Retail | The sale of goods to the public (like a clothing store). | I worked in clothing retail for three years. |
| Curse word | Four-letter word | A bad word, curse word, naughty word, or dirty word. | He got in trouble for using a four-letter word. |
| Believe me | Take my word for it | Trusting that what someone is saying is true. | Take my word for it, that restaurant is terrible. |
| Guaranteed | Take it to the bank | Meaning a statement is guaranteed, absolutely true, or legitimate. | He will win the election, you can take it to the bank. |
| Real / truthful | Legit | Short for legitimate; real, authentic, or legal. | Don't worry, the offer is completely legit. |
| Believe them | Take someone at their word | To believe someone based only on what they say. | I take him at his word that he didn't steal it. |
| Verbal recommendation | Word of mouth | Hearing about something from people talking, not advertising. | The restaurant became popular solely by word of mouth. |
| Haven't heard anything | I haven't heard a word | Having zero information, or hearing total silence. | Do you know what happened? I haven't heard a word. |
| Noisy / unruly | Rowdy | Noisy, disorderly, and difficult to control. | The kids were getting very rowdy. |
| Speechless | I have no words | Being so shocked or amazed that you cannot speak. | That sunset is so beautiful, I have no words. |
| Amazed | In awe | Having a strong feeling of amazement or respect. | I was in awe of his musical talent. |
| Charismatic speaker | Have a way with words | Being a smooth, charismatic speaker who says the right things. | He has a way with words and is very persuasive. |
| Charming | Smooth | Charming and having excellent social skills. | He's very smooth when talking to clients. |
| Pay attention | Mark my words | Used to tell someone to remember a prediction or warning. | Mark my words, that company will be bankrupt in a year. |

### Alternative Expressions

When Drake explained the meaning of "break your word", he used three excellent alternatives that you can use interchangeably: 1. You didn't hold true to your promise. 2. You didn't follow through with it. 3. You didn't deliver on your word.

### Pronunciation Guide

| Word | Correct pronunciation | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Word | WERD | Eliminating the 'o' entirely. Do not say "w-OH-rd" with rounded lips. |

## 30 Idioms with a Native Speaker

### Summary

Professional routines and career evaluations utilize idioms like *nine-to-five job*, *downtime*, and *pros and cons*. Experiences of betrayal, rejection, and setback are captured by *cut down at the knees*, *let down*, and *throw under the bus*, balanced by lifestyle idioms like *live it up* and *lay low*.

### Vocabulary And Expressions
