Declarative English statements require falling intonation to project authority, conviction, and executive presence, reserving rising intonation exclusively for questions. The vocal habit of "up talk"—elevating pitch at the end of affirmative sentences—inadvertently signals insecurity, self-doubt, or a need for external validation. Cultivating confident workplace communication relies on grounding sentences with a definitive, downward pitch trajectory.

### The Basic Rule of Intonation

- Rising Intonation (Pitch goes UP up): Used for asking questions.
- Example: "Is he from China? up"
- Example: "Has the meeting started yet? up"
- Falling Intonation (Pitch goes DOWN down): Used for statements, facts, and giving orders.
- Example: "He's from mainland China. down"
- Example: "The meeting has already started. down"

### The Problem: "Up Talk"

"Up Talk" is a bad habit where a speaker uses rising intonation at the end of a normal statement, making it sound like a question.

Example of Up Talk: "The meeting is at 12:00? up And you should bring a pen? up"

#### Why is Up Talk bad?

- It makes you sound like you are asking for permission.
- It makes you sound insecure and unsure of what you are saying.
- It is highly associated with immature speech patterns (often used by young teenagers or certain influencers).
- In a professional environment, 85% of supervisors reported that up talk would hurt an employee's chances of getting a promotion. It destroys your authority.

### How to Sound Like a Leader

When making a statement, giving an opinion, or giving an instruction at work, your pitch MUST go down at the end of the sentence. Tell, don't ask.

Practice saying these sentences with a strong, confident, falling pitch down at the end:

- "Let's start the meeting. down" (If you go up, it sounds like: Is it okay if we start?)
- "I need you to do this for me. down" (You are telling, not asking).
- "You made a few mistakes on this. down"
- "We need to finish it by Friday. down"
- "I believe this is our best option. down"

## Vowel Reduction in Unstressed Syllables (The Schwa)

### Summary

As a stress-timed language, English demands articulating stressed vowels with full clarity while systematically reducing unstressed vowels (a, e, i, o, u) into the weak neutral schwa /ə/ ("uh"). Attempting to pronounce every vowel according to its spelling destroys natural cadence and clarity. High-frequency reductions appear in banana (/bəˈnænə/), problem (/ˈprɑːbləm/), police (/pəˈliːs/), and support (/səˈpɔːrt/).

### The Rule of Stress and Reduction

- Find the stressed syllable: The vowel in this syllable is pronounced clearly and is held longer.
- Reduce the unstressed syllables: The vowels in these syllables lose their spelling sound and become a short, weak "uh" sound.

If you pronounce every vowel equally, you will sound very unnatural.

### Examples of Vowel Reduction

#### 1. Words with "A"

- Banana: The stress is on the middle syllable (ba-NA-na). The first and last 'a' reduce to 'uh'.
- Pronunciation: buh-NA-nuh. (Not: ba-na-na).
- Japan: The stress is on the second syllable. The first 'a' reduces.
- Pronunciation: juh-PAN. (Not: ja-pan).
- Brazil: The stress is on the second syllable. The 'a' reduces.
- Pronunciation: bruh-ZIL. (Not: bra-zil).

#### 2. Words with "E"

- System: The stress is on the first syllable. The second 'e' reduces.
- Pronunciation: SIS-tuhm. (Not: sis-tem).
- Problem: The stress is on the first syllable. The 'e' reduces.
- Pronunciation: PRAH-bluhm. (Not: prob-lem).
- Sweden: The stress is on the first syllable. The 'e' reduces.
- Pronunciation: SWEE-duhn. (Not: swee-den).

#### 3. Words with "I" / "O" / "U"

- Belgium: The stress is on the first syllable. The 'i' and 'u' blend into one reduced sound.
- Pronunciation: BEL-juhm.
- Finland: The stress is on the first syllable. The 'a' reduces.
- Pronunciation: FIN-luhnd.
- Jordan: The stress is on the first syllable. The 'a' reduces.
- Pronunciation: JOR-duhn.

### Practice Sentences

Exaggerate the stress on the capitalized syllables, and make the reduced vowels very short "uh" sounds.

- "That's a SPE-shuhl MEH-thuhd." (Special method)
- "That's a MAH-duhrn SIS-tuhm." (Modern system)
- "The DAWK-tuhr said it was a CAH-muhn PRAH-bluhm." (Doctor / common / problem)
- "LIN-kuhn and EH-di-suhn are FAY-muhs uh-MEHR-i-kuhns." (Lincoln / Edison / famous Americans)

## The 3 Sounds of "CH" in English

### Summary

The English digraph "CH" yields three distinct phonemes determined by linguistic etymology: standard /tʃ/ in words of Old English origin (child, champion, archery); French-derived soft /ʃ/ (chef, chic, Chicago, machine); and Greek-derived velar stop /k/ (character, chemistry, echo, stomach, orchestra). Additionally, the digraph remains completely silent in loanwords such as yacht (/jɑːt/).

### 1. The Standard "CH" Sound (ch)

This is the most common pronunciation (like in child, teacher, much).

- Chief: A leader.
- Chick: A baby bird.
- Arch: A curved structure.
- Archery: The sport of shooting a bow and arrow.
- Champion: A winner.
- Channel: A TV station or body of water.

### 2. The "SH" Sound (sh)

Words that have a French origin usually retain the "SH" sound.

- Chef: A professional cook.
- Chic: Elegant and stylish.
- Chicago: The US city. (Also note the state: Illinois has a silent 's' - pronounced Ill-in-oy).
- Charlatan: A fraud; a dishonest person pretending to have skills.
- Chalet: A mountain house. (The 't' is also silent).
- Champagne: The sparkling wine.
- Chandelier: A hanging crystal light fixture.
- Chauffeur: A person hired to drive a car.
- Chivalry / Chivalrous: Honorable, polite, gentlemanly behavior.
- Michigan: The US state.

### 3. The "K" Sound (k)

Words with Greek origins often pronounce "CH" as a hard "K".

- Choir: A group of singers.
- Chaos / Chaotic: Complete confusion.
- Archeology: The study of ancient societies.
- Architect: A person who designs buildings.
- Ache / Headache / Stomachache: Continuous pain.
- Chemical / Chemistry / Chemotherapy: Science and medical terms.
- Chord: A combination of musical notes. (Note: A cord is a wire or rope. They sound exactly the same).
- Chronic: Continuing for a long time (e.g., chronic pain).
- Echo: A repeating sound.

### 4. The Silent "CH"

- Yacht: A large, expensive boat. The 'ch' makes no sound at all. (Pronounced: yot).

### Bonus Rule: Schedule

This word is pronounced differently depending on the country: - American English: Pronounced with a "SK" sound (SKED-jool). - British English: Pronounced with a "SH" sound (SHED-jool).

### Practice Sentences

Read these out loud, paying close attention to the CH sounds:

- The rich man from Chicago (sh) has a chauffeur (sh) and a chef (sh).
- The champion (ch) celebrated with champagne (sh).
- The chef (sh) works at a chic (sh) restaurant.
- I heard the echo (k) from the yacht (silent).
- Charlie (ch) sings in a choir (k) in Chicago (sh).
- The charlatan (sh) caused complete chaos (k).

## Voiced vs. Voiceless Consonants

### Summary

Voiced consonants require vocal cord vibration (/v/, /d/, /z/, /b/, /ɡ/), whereas voiceless counterparts utilize only expired airflow (/f/, /t/, /s/, /p/, /k/). Preventing terminal consonant devoicing—such as erroneously shifting /v/ to [f] in five or /d/ to [t] in bed—is vital to maintaining word distinctions. Crucially, the phonological voicing of the preceding stem consonant dictates plural 's' pronunciation, producing voiced /z/ after voiced segments (dogs, beds) and voiceless /s/ after unvoiced ones (cats, caps).

### The Mistake: Dropping the Voice

A voiced consonant uses your vocal cords (you can feel the vibration in your throat). A voiceless consonant is just air.

Many languages automatically change a voiced consonant into a voiceless one at the end of a word. English does not. You must force the vibration until the very end.

| Voiced | Voiceless Mistake | Correct Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| V (Vibration) | F (Air only) | Five (Not fife). Twelve (Not twelf). Have (Not haf). |
| D (Vibration) | T (Air only) | Limited (Not limitet). Bed (Not bet). |
| J (Vibration) | CH (Air only) | Judge (Not chuch/juch). |
| G (Vibration) | K (Air only) | Egg (Not ek). Dog (Not dok). |

Warning: If you drop the voice, you might change the meaning of the word entirely. - Save (Keep safe) vs. Safe (A secure box). - Bed (Furniture) vs. Bet (Gambling). - Believe (Verb) vs. Belief (Noun).

### The Plural "S" Rule

The voicing of the final consonant determines how you pronounce a plural 's'.

- If the word ends in a VOICED consonant, the plural 's' is pronounced as a Z.
- Egg ends in voiced 'g'. -> Eggs (eg-Z).
- Dog ends in voiced 'g'. -> Dogs (dog-Z).
- Friend ends in voiced 'd'. -> Friends (friend-Z).
- Believe ends in voiced 'v'. -> He believes (believ-Z).
- If the word ends in a VOICELESS consonant, the plural 's' remains an S.
- Cat ends in voiceless 't'. -> Cats (cat-S).
- Let ends in voiceless 't'. -> Lets (let-S).

## Fixing the "Es-pecial" Mistake

### Summary

Native Spanish phonotactic interference often causes epenthetic vowel insertion (/ɛ/) before initial s-consonant clusters, erroneously transforming Spain, student, and special into Es-pain, Es-tudent, and Es-pecial. Eliminating this fossilized habit is achieved through connected speech linking, binding the terminal consonant of the preceding word directly to the initial /s/ (e.g., this-school, good-student). When speaking in isolation, speakers must consciously start with a pure alveolar hiss without pre-vocalizing.

### The Problem

In Spanish, words do not naturally start with an 'S' + consonant. They always start with an 'E' (e.g., Espana, estudiante).

Because of this, Spanish speakers unconsciously add an "eh" sound to English words: - Spain becomes Es-pain. - Student becomes Es-tudent. - Special becomes Es-pecial. - Stomach becomes Es-tomach.

### The Solution: Linking Words

The best way to force yourself to drop the extra "E" is to connect the 'S' word directly to the final sound of the word before it.

- "I'm sleeping"
- Mistake: "I am... es-leeping."
- Fix: Connect the 'M' directly to the 'S'. Mmmmm-Ssss-leeping. "I'm sleeping."
- "It's special"
- Mistake: "It's... es-pecial."
- Fix: Connect the final 'S' of "it's" to the beginning 'S' of "special". "It's_special."
- "He's strong"
- Mistake: "He is... es-trong."
- Fix: Connect the 'Z' sound of "he's" to the 'S'. "He's_strong."

### Practice Sentences

Try reading these sentences out loud. Focus on immediately starting the 'S' sound without vibrating your vocal cords first.

- I want to study in the United States.
- Spanish is spoken in Spain.
- That student is smart.
- She's scared and she doesn't want to stay here.
- Don't stop smiling.

## 15 English Proverbs about Money

### Summary

Money-related discourse begins with the proper pronunciation of money using the neutral vowel /ʌ/ (pronounced muh-nee /ˈmʌni/). Classic English economic proverbs warn against fiscal recklessness and overconfidence, including a penny saved is a penny earned, don't put all your eggs in one basket, put your money where your mouth is, and a fool and his money are soon parted. Philosophical perspectives balance wealth against human values through adages like money talks, money can't buy happiness, and the best things in life are free.

### The Proverbs

| Proverb | Meaning / Usage |
| --- | --- |
| Money doesn't grow on trees. | Money is hard to earn; it is not free. (Often said to children who want to buy things). |
| Time is money. | Time is valuable; do not waste it when you could be working or producing. |
| Money talks. | People with a lot of money have power and influence to get what they want. |
| Money can't buy happiness. | Wealth will not cure depression or guarantee long-term joy. |
| The best things in life are free. | Family, love, and health are more valuable than anything you can buy. |
| Save it for a rainy day. | Save your money for a future emergency or a difficult time in your life. |
| Beggars can't be choosers. | If you get something for free (or ask for help), you shouldn't complain about the quality of what you are given. |
| You can't take it with you. | When you die, your money stays behind. Don't be overly materialistic. |
| There is no such thing as a free lunch. | Nothing is truly free; there is always a hidden cost or a catch. |
| A fool and his money are soon parted. | Foolish people make bad financial decisions and lose their money quickly. |
| You get what you pay for. | If you buy something very cheap, it is probably going to be bad quality. |
| Neither a borrower nor a lender be. | Do not borrow money from friends, and do not lend it to them, or it will ruin the friendship. (From Shakespeare). |
| Money makes the world go round. | Money is essential; everything in society relies on it to function. |
| The love of money is the root of all evil. | Greed causes people to do terrible, criminal, and immoral things. (Biblical). |
| It takes money to make money. | You need initial capital or an investment if you want to start a business and generate wealth. |

## The 10 Negative Prefixes (Vocabulary Test)

### Summary

English derives negation and opposition using ten distinct prefixes governed largely by etymology and phonological assimilation: un- (unreliable), in- (incompetent), dis- (disobedient), mis- (misinformed), non- (non-verbal), a- (atypical), and ab- (abnormal). The prefix in- assimilates phonetically to im- before labials (impatient), il- before liquids (illogical), and ir- before rhotics (irresponsible). Crucially, semantic differences arise in pairs like unsatisfied (unfulfilled in quantity or appetite) versus dissatisfied (discontented with quality or conduct).

### The Prefix Test Answers

#### Prefix: UN-

- Aware -> Unaware: Not knowing about something. ("I was unaware of the situation.")
- Caring -> Uncaring: Unkind; not showing sympathy. ("His uncaring attitude shocked me.")
- Reasonable -> Unreasonable: Not fair or sensible. ("It's unreasonable to expect me to work Sunday.")
- Reliable -> Unreliable: Someone you cannot depend on. ("He was fired for being unreliable.")
- Satisfied -> Unsatisfied: Feeling unfulfilled (e.g., still hungry, or a goal not reached).

#### Prefix: IN-

- Accurate -> Inaccurate: Not correct; wrong. ("I apologize for the inaccurate information.")

#### Prefix: DIS-

- Prove -> Disprove: To show that something is not true. ("You cannot disprove the existence of God.")
- Respect -> Disrespect: To treat rudely. ("I didn't intend to disrespect you.")
- Satisfied -> Dissatisfied: Unhappy or displeased with a situation or product. ("If you are dissatisfied with the product, return it.")

#### Prefix: MIS-

- Behave -> Misbehave: To act badly (usually used for children). ("If you misbehave, I will call your parents.")
- Fortune -> Misfortune: Bad luck; an unfortunate event. ("Losing his house was a great misfortune.") Note: Unfortunate is the adjective, misfortune is the noun.
- Judge -> Misjudge: To have an unfair/wrong opinion, or to guess incorrectly. ("I completely misjudged him.")
- Manage -> Mismanage: To handle a business or situation badly. ("The CEO mismanaged the finances.")
- Spell -> Misspell: To spell a word incorrectly. ("I misspelled your name.")
- Understand -> Misunderstand: To interpret something wrongly. ("I feel very misunderstood.")

#### Prefix: AB- / A-

- Normal -> Abnormal: Not normal. ("This warm weather is abnormal for December.")
- Symptomatic -> Asymptomatic: Having an illness but showing no symptoms. ("He has the virus but is asymptomatic.")

#### Prefix: IR-

- Repairable -> Irreparable: Impossible to repair or rectify. (Pronounced: ih-REP-er-uh-bl). ("The scandal caused irreparable damage.")

#### Prefix: NON-

- Smoker -> Non-smoker: A person who doesn't smoke. ("This section is for non-smokers.")
- Toxic -> Non-toxic: Not poisonous. ("I need to buy non-toxic paint.")

#### Prefix: UN- (For Verbs)

- Plug -> Unplug: To disconnect from electricity. ("Try unplugging the router.")

## 5 Reasons Word Stress is Critical

### Summary

English is an intensely stress-timed language where placing stress on the correct syllable determines intelligibility, grammatical category, and speaker intent. Stress shifts convert identical lexical forms from nouns to verbs (RE-cord vs. re-CORD; CON-tract vs. con-TRACT) and dictate the comprehension of initialisms, which require stressing the final letter (F.B.I., M&A). At the sentence level, modulating tonic stress radically recalibrates pragmatic emphasis and emotional undertones.

### Reason 1: You will be misunderstood.

A native speaker's brain listens for a specific melody. If you stress the wrong syllable, the word becomes unrecognizable. - Abbreviations: Always stress the last letter. (e.g., M & A, U.C.L.A., F.B.I.). If you say M & A, people won't know what you mean. - Syllable Stress: A doctor asked, "Would you like a re-FILL?" and her patients were confused. She needed to stress the first syllable: "Would you like a RE-fill?"

### Reason 2: You will send the wrong message.

The word you choose to stress changes the emotional intent of the sentence. - "I want to talk to you." (Neutral). - "I want to talk to YOU." (I don't want to talk to anyone else; can sound dictatorial or angry). - "I want to talk to you." (You might not want to, but I do). - "I WANT to talk to you." (I really do! Believe me).

### Reason 3: You will sound like a robot.

If you stress every single word equally (which is common in some languages), you will sound like an automated computer. You will sound "unenthusiastic" and lack passion. English requires peaks (stressed words) and valleys (reduced words) to sound engaging.

### Reason 4: Stress forces you to slow down.

Many learners speak too fast. When you properly stress a word, you must open your mouth wider and hold the vowel longer. This automatically slows your pace down to a natural, understandable rhythm. - Example: "When I joined a re-CRUIT-ing e-VENT... I talked to con-SULT-ing companies." (Holding those syllables forces clarity).

### Reason 5: The dictionary meaning changes.

In English, the same words can have entirely different definitions based on stress. - Compound Nouns: The stress goes on the first word. - "I live in the White House." (I am the President of the United States). - "I live in a white house." (My house is painted white). - Nouns vs. Verbs: - "RE-cord" (Noun - a vinyl disc). - "re-CORD" (Verb - to capture audio). - "DE-sert" (Noun - the Sahara). - "de-SSERT" (Noun - cake after dinner).

## Fixing the "L" Sound (For Portuguese Speakers)

### Summary

Brazilian Portuguese speakers frequently substitute a rounded labiovelar [w] or [u] for terminal dark 'L', turning words like Brazil, feel, and call into Brazi-w, feew, and caw. Correct American pronunciation requires keeping the lips completely relaxed and neutral rather than rounded. The articulation is perfected by raising the tongue tip firmly against the alveolar ridge behind the upper front teeth, maintaining contact through the vocalization.

### The Mistake: Rounding the Lips

If you are from Brazil, you probably say "Brazil" and it sounds like "Brazi-w" or "Brazi-u".

- Feel sounds like "feew"
- Call sounds like "caw"
- Will sounds like "wiw"

This happens because you are pushing your lips forward and making them into a small circle (like saying "Oh" or "Ooh").

### The Fix: Neutral Lips, Tongue Up

To make a proper American "L" sound at the end of a word: 1. Do NOT round your lips. Keep them completely relaxed and neutral. Some teachers recommend slightly smiling to prevent the lips from pushing forward. 2. Move the tip of your tongue UP. Place it right behind your top front teeth.

## The "N" vs "NG" Sounds (For Chinese Speakers)

### Summary

Distinguishing final alveolar /n/ from velar /ŋ/ in minimal pairs like thin versus thing and sin versus sing depends on tongue placement. For terminal /n/ (can, man), the tongue tip must press firmly against the alveolar ridge behind the upper teeth to direct airflow through the nasal cavity. Conversely, for terminal /ŋ/ (ring, rang), the tongue tip remains flat while the back of the tongue seals upward against the soft palate.

### The Problem with "N"

When an "N" is at the beginning of a word (never) or middle (Canada), Chinese speakers pronounce it perfectly.

However, when it is at the end of a word (can, man, seven), Chinese speakers often keep their tongue down. The result is that the "N" disappears completely, and "can" sounds like "ca".

### How to Fix the "N"

To pronounce the "N" at the end of a word: 1. Move the tip of your tongue UP to the roof of your mouth, just behind your teeth. 2. Keep the tongue up, and push the air out through your nose. 3. Example: "yes I ca-nnn" (hold the tongue up).

#### Linking the "N"

A great trick is to link the final "N" to a vowel in the next word. Because Chinese speakers are good at middle-N sounds, linking solves the problem. - Instead of "can I", connect it: Ca-nI. - Instead of "when I", connect it: Whe-nI.

### "N" vs. "NG"

The difference between these two sounds is entirely based on which part of the tongue goes up.

| Sound | Tongue Position | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| N | The FRONT of the tongue goes up. | Thin, sin, run |
| NG | The BACK of the tongue goes up (like you are about to make a 'G' sound). The front is relaxed. | Thing, sing, rung |

## 8 Classic English Proverbs

### Summary

Essential English proverbs offer timeless social guidance: look before you leap cautions deliberation before decisive action, while strike while the iron is hot urges immediate seizure of fleeting opportunities. Perspective and interpersonal judgment are anchored by don't judge a book by its cover, the grass is always greener on the other side, and actions speak louder than words. Operational and cultural adaptations are captured by warning that too many cooks spoil the broth and advising travelers that when in Rome, do as the Romans do.

### The Proverbs

#### 1. Don't make a mountain out of a molehill.

- Meaning: Do not exaggerate a minor problem into a huge crisis.
- Usage: "It's just a small scratch on the car. Don't make a mountain out of a molehill."
- Pronunciation Note: Native speakers often "hold" the 'T' in mountain (moun-n), they do not clearly pronounce it (moun-tin).

#### 2. Look before you leap.

- Meaning: Think carefully about the consequences before you make a big decision.
- Usage: "I know you want to quit your job and start a business, but look before you leap."

#### 3. Let sleeping dogs lie.

- Meaning: Leave a situation as it is to avoid causing conflict; do not unnecessarily create a problem by talking about a sensitive issue.
- Usage: "She didn't mention the argument from last week, so I decided to let sleeping dogs lie."

#### 4. All that glitters isn't gold.

- Meaning: Just because something looks beautiful or valuable on the outside, doesn't mean it actually is.
- Usage: "That cheap watch looks great, but all that glitters isn't gold."

#### 5. Don't bite off more than you can chew.

- Meaning: Do not take on too much work or responsibility that you cannot handle.
- Usage: "I took a second job and signed up for three classes. I bit off more than I could chew."

#### 6. You can lead a horse to water, but you can't make him drink.

- Meaning: You can give someone an opportunity or advice, but you cannot force them to take it or accept your help.
- Usage: "I got him an interview, but he refused to go. You can lead a horse to water, but you can't make him drink."

#### 7. It's no use crying over spilled milk.

- Meaning: Do not waste time being upset about a past accident or mistake that cannot be changed.
- Usage: "I accidentally deleted the file. Well, there's no use crying over spilled milk. I'll just rewrite it." (Note: spilled in US English, spilt in UK English).

#### 8. We'll cross that bridge when we come to it.

- Meaning: Do not worry about a potential future problem until it actually happens. Deal with it later.
- Usage: "What if it rains on the day of the wedding?" "We'll cross that bridge when we come to it."

## Advanced Vocabulary: Real Conversation (Pets & Business)

### Summary

Contemporary pet care culture and service entrepreneurship utilize specialized colloquialisms such as pooch, fur child (or fur baby), and managing animal temperament within a canine pack. Small business operations rely on identifying their financial bread and butter (primary income source) and driving growth via organic word of mouth. Operational terms also highlight autonomy, accommodating flexible hours while catering to an upscale clientele.

### Animal Vocabulary

| Word | Meaning | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pooch | Slang/informal word for "dog." | "That's a cute little pooch." |
| Spoiled | Treated very well, often with too much generosity. | "He behaves like a spoiled child." |
| Bunny | An informal word for a rabbit (often used by or with children). | "We do cat and bunny visits." |
| Fur child / Fur baby | A pet that an owner loves and treats like a human baby. | "He's not just a dog; he's my fur baby." |
| To roam | To wander or walk around freely without a clear direction. | "The dogs used to roam on 10 acres of land." |
| Breed | A specific type of dog or animal (e.g., German Shepherd, Poodle). | "What breed is your dog?" |
| A rescue | (Noun). An adopted dog that was previously homeless or abandoned. | "Her dog is a rescue." |

### Business & Daily Expressions

- Clientele: A group or type of customers. "That store attracts wealthy clientele."
- Interestingly enough: Used to introduce a fact that is unexpected or interesting. "Interestingly enough, the hardest part was the interview."
- That's an "L.A." thing: Something that is highly associated with a specific city or culture. "Treating dogs like babies is an L.A. thing."
- That's my thing / That's not my thing: Something you enjoy or do not enjoy. "Hiking just isn't my thing."
- The utmost: The absolute greatest amount or highest degree. "We treat the dogs with the utmost care." "This is of utmost importance."
- A quirk: An unusual habit or slightly strange personality trait. "Everyone has their own quirks."
- There's more to it: It is more complicated than it appears. "Dog walking looks easy, but there's more to it than I thought."
- Transportation (L.A. specific): In Los Angeles, asking if you have "reliable transportation" specifically means asking if you have a working car. (In NY, it means the subway).
- Trustworthy: Someone you can trust; honest and reliable. "You must be trustworthy to work in someone's home."
- To fill in: To do someone else's job temporarily because they are absent. "Can you fill in for me on Saturday?"
- Words to live by: Great life advice to follow forever. "Find something you love, and you'll never work a day in your life. Those are words to live by."
- Instagram worthy: A photograph or location that looks beautiful enough to be posted on social media. "The lighting makes this photo Instagram worthy."

## Word Stress: The "Adjective + Noun" Rule

### Summary

Standard English prosody mandates placing primary stress on the noun rather than the preceding adjective in descriptive noun phrases (e.g., a new HOUSE, a big ROOM). Stress shifts to the adjective exclusively for contrastive emphasis or explicit correction (e.g., the BLUE shirt, not the green one). This rhythmic pattern extends directly to idiomatic constructions such as a silver LINING, a bitter PILL, and a piece of CAKE.

### The Rule: Stress the Noun!

In English, when you have an adjective followed by a noun, you must stress the noun more than the adjective.

Many languages stress the adjective. If you do this in English, you will sound very unnatural.

Exceptions: You only stress the adjective if you are correcting someone (e.g., "I wanted the blue sweater, not the green one!").

#### Examples:

- "That's a new house." (Not: new house).
- "That's a big room." (Not: big room).
- "You did a good job." (Not: good job).

### Idioms Using the "Adjective + Noun" Rule

Practice saying these common idioms, making sure to hit the noun hard and reduce the adjective.

#### 1. Once in a blue MOON.

- Meaning: Very rarely; almost never.
- Practice: "I see my old friend once in a blue moon." (Stress "moon", do not stress "blue").

#### 2. No big DEAL.

- Meaning: It's okay; it's not a problem; it's not serious.
- Practice: "We had a little disagreement, but it's no big deal."

#### 3. On the same PAGE.

- Meaning: Two people are thinking the same way or have the same understanding of a situation.
- Practice: "They are on the same page about wanting a small wedding."

#### 4. Come a long WAY.

- Meaning: To have made a lot of good progress compared to the past.
- Practice: "You've come a long way in only 3 months."

## 16 Commonly Mispronounced Words

### Summary

Silent consonants feature prominently in advanced pronunciation pitfalls, including silent 'b' in subtle (/ˈsʌtəl/), debt (/dɛt/), and the vowel-shifting triad comb (/koʊm/), bomb (/bɑːm/), and tomb (/tuːm/). Silent 'l' defines salmon (/ˈsæmən/) and almond (/ˈɑːmənd/), while silent 'p' marks receipt (/rɪˈsiːt/), and silent 'g' marks phlegm (/flɛm/). Additionally, syllable reduction syncopates comfortable into three syllables (/ˈkʌmftərbəl/), elides the 'th' in plural months (/mʌns/), and initial stress silences the 'h' in vehicle (/ˈviːɪkəl/).

### Tricky Consonants and Silent Letters

| Word | Pronunciation Notes |
| --- | --- |
| Months | Most native speakers drop the "th" entirely. Just say muns. ("two muns"). |
| Phlegm | The 'ph' is an 'f' sound, and the 'g' is silent. Flem. |
| Salmon | The 'L' is completely silent. Sam-un. |
| Subtle | The 'b' is completely silent. Suh-dl (with a flap-t). |
| Comb / Bomb / Tomb | The 'b' is silent in all three. Note the vowels: Comb = o, Bomb = ah, Tomb = ooh. |
| Sixth | Very difficult cluster. Make a long 's', then slide your tongue to a 'th'. Siks-th. |
| Water | Round lips for 'W', open wide for 'AH', use a fast-D (flap-T). Wah-der. |

### Word Stress & Vowel Shifts

| Word | Pronunciation Notes |
| --- | --- |
| Literature | Lih-der-uh-chur. (Fast-D, schwa sound). |
| Maintenance | Main-te-nens. Hold the middle 't'. Do not say "maintain-ance". |
| Photograph vs. Photographer | PHO-to-graph (Stress 1st syllable, 'O' sound). pho-TOG-ra-pher (Stress 2nd syllable, 'AH' sound). |
| Regularly | Reg-you-ler-ly. Do not say "reg-ooh-lar". |
| Theater | Thee-uh-der. (Fast-D). |
| War vs. Wore | They are pronounced exactly the same. Wore. |
| Were vs. Where | They are NOT the same. Were sounds like wer (silent e). Where sounds like wear. ("Where were they?"). |
| One vs. Won | They are pronounced exactly the same. ("Wun"). |
| Woman vs. Women | Woman = Wuh-man (like book). Women = Wih-min (like big). |

## Stop Adding "S" to Uncountable Nouns

### Summary

Uncountable mass nouns in English never take a plural "-s" suffix and must agree with singular verbs. High-frequency non-count nouns include luggage, advice, information, furniture, equipment, vocabulary, slang, and software. To express numerical quantity, speakers must pair them with countable counters such as pieces of (e.g., two pieces of luggage or a piece of advice) or substitute discrete countable equivalents like suggestions, words, or programs.

### The "Never Add S" List (Uncountable Nouns)

The following words are uncountable. You cannot add an "S", and you must use a singular verb (e.g., is, not are).

- Luggage: "I have three pieces of luggage." (Not: luggages). Note: Suitcase is countable (suitcases).
- Advice: "She gave me some good advice." (Not: advices). Note: Suggestion is countable (suggestions).
- Vocabulary: "I want to learn more vocabulary." (Not: vocabularies). Note: Word is countable (words).
- Slang: "He uses a lot of slang." (Not: slangs).
- Equipment: "The gym has new equipment." (Not: equipments).
- Furniture: "I bought two pieces of furniture." (Not: furnitures).
- Jewelry: "She bought some beautiful jewelry." (Not: jewelries).
- Information: "He gave me a piece of information." (Not: informations).
- Homework: "I finished my homework." (Not: homeworks). Note: Assignment is countable (assignments).
- Clothing: "The store sells clothing." (Not: clothings). Note: Clothes is always plural.

[!TIP] How to count them: If you need to specify a number, use a "unit word" like "a piece of" or "an item of". Correct: "Two pieces of advice," "Three items of clothing," "Four pieces of luggage."

### Irregular Animals (Plural without S)

Some animal words are both singular and plural. Never add an "S". - Fish: One fish, ten fish. (Fishes is only used when talking about multiple scientific species of fish). - Deer: One deer, five deer. (Not: deers). - Sheep: One sheep, a hundred sheep. (Not: sheeps). - Moose: One moose, two moose. (Not: mooses). - Mice: One mouse, three mice. (Not: mouses).

## The Ultimate Prepositions Guide

### Summary

Use 'in' for cities, enclosed spaces, and months; 'at' for specific locations, activities, and numbered addresses; and 'on' for streets without numbers and specific dates. Fixed adjective collocations require 'good/bad at', 'known for', 'married/engaged to', 'disappointed in' (a person) versus 'with' (a thing), and 'dressed in'. Distinguish material transformations using 'made of' when the original substance remains visible, 'made from' when it is transformed, and 'made out of' for creative repurposing.

### Places, Addresses, and Dates

| Scenario | Rule | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cities / Enclosed Spaces | Use IN | in New York, in the building. |
| Specific Locations / Activities | Use AT | at the airport, at the bus stop. |
| Street Names (No number) | Use ON | I live on Main Street. |
| Full Addresses (With number) | Use AT | I live at 537 Main Street. |
| Months | Use IN | Her birthday is in April. |
| Specific Dates | Use ON | Her birthday is on April 10th. |

Note: "Let's meet at the restaurant" means the location (maybe outside). "Let's meet in the restaurant" means physically inside the building.

### Adjectives + Prepositions

You must memorize which prepositions follow these common adjectives: - Good / Bad / Terrible AT: "I am bad at math." - Known FOR: "She is known for her beautiful voice." - Married / Engaged TO: "She is married to John." (Never use with!). - Disappointed IN (a person): "I am disappointed in you." - Disappointed WITH (a thing): "I was disappointed with the movie." - Absent FROM: "He was absent from work yesterday." - Dressed IN: "He is dressed in black." - Composed OF: "Water is composed of hydrogen and oxygen."

### Verbs + Prepositions

- Consist OF: "The meal consists of rice."
- Discriminate AGAINST: "It's illegal to discriminate against employees."
- Decide ON: "I can't decide on which restaurant to go to."
- Decide AGAINST (+ ing): "I decided against buying the car." (Meaning: I decided not to).
- Plan TO (+ verb): "I plan to finish it." (A definite intention).
- Plan ON (+ ing): "I plan on finishing it." (An expectation or preparation).

#### Made Of vs. From vs. Out Of

- Made OF: The original material is still visible. ("The table is made of wood").
- Made FROM: The original material is transformed and no longer visible. ("Wine is made from grapes").
- Made OUT OF: Emphasizes creativity or unusual use. ("She made a heart out of paper").

## The Short "I" vs. Long "E"

### Summary

The long 'E' (/iː/) is tense with a raised tongue and stretched lips ('beat', 'feel'), whereas the short 'I' (/ɪ/) is relaxed, rapid, and produced with a slightly dropped jaw ('bit', 'fill'). Confusing these phonemes alters meaning across pairs such as live/leave, fill/feel, and ship/sheep. English words ending in an 'e' or 'y' vowel sound always terminate in the tense long 'E' ('busy', 'happy'), never the relaxed short 'I'.

### The Long "E" (Tense)

- How to make it: Your tongue is high, and your lips are stretched into a slight smile. The muscles in your face are tense.
- Spelling: Usually spelled with EE or EA.
- Examples: Beat, seat, heat, feel, sheep, leave.

### The Short "I" (Relaxed)

- How to make it: Your tongue is slightly lower, your jaw drops a tiny bit, and your lips are completely relaxed. It is very fast.
- Spelling: Usually spelled with the letter I.
- Examples: Bit, sit, hit, fill, ship, live.

[!WARNING] If you use the tense Long E instead of the relaxed Short I, you will say the wrong word: - "Where do you live?" -> "Where do you leave?" - "Fill the glass." -> "Feel the glass." - "Look at that ship." -> "Look at that sheep." - "I was hit." -> "I was heat."

### Rule: Endings are always Long E

When an English word ends in an "E" or "Y" sound, it is always the long, tense "E" sound. There are no English words that end in the short, relaxed "I" sound.

- Examples: He, we, busy, happy, cazzy.

## The 3 Essential Comma Rules

### Summary

Relative clauses require commas only when non-restrictive (supplying non-essential, parenthetical information), but omit commas when restrictive (essential to specifying the noun). With subordinating conjunctions ('because', 'although'), insert a comma only when the dependent clause introduces the sentence, omitting it when placed medially. For coordinating conjunctions (FANBOYS), insert a comma before the conjunction only when joining two complete independent clauses with separate subjects.

### Rule 1: Relative Clauses (Who, Which, That)

A relative clause gives more information about a noun. You only use commas if the information is "extra" and not essential to the sentence.

- Non-Restrictive (Extra Info) = COMMAS
- Example: "My uncle, who is a dentist, lives in New York."
- Why: If you take out the clause ("My uncle lives in New York"), the sentence still makes perfect sense. The clause is just bonus information.
- Restrictive (Essential Info) = NO COMMAS
- Example: "The students who study hard will get an A."
- Why: If you take out the clause ("The students will get an A"), the meaning of the sentence completely changes. The clause is essential to identifying which students.

### Rule 2: Subordinating Conjunctions

Subordinating conjunctions (because, after, although, before, even if, whenever) attach a dependent clause to an independent clause.

- Conjunction in the middle = NO COMMA
- Example: "I couldn't call you yesterday because I was studying."
- Conjunction at the beginning = COMMA in the middle
- Example: "Because I was studying, I couldn't call you yesterday."

### Rule 3: Coordinating Conjunctions (FANBOYS)

Coordinating conjunctions (For, And, Nor, But, Or, Yet, So) connect things together.

- Connecting two complete sentences = COMMA REQUIRED
- Example: "He speaks English well, but he needs to work on his writing."
- Why: Both halves of the sentence have a subject and verb and could stand alone as separate sentences.
- Second half is incomplete = NO COMMA
- Example: "He likes to watch movies and play video games."
- Why: "play video games" does not have a subject, so it cannot stand alone as a sentence. Do not use a comma before 'and'.

## Tricky Food Pronunciations & Silent Letters

### Summary

'Salmon' is pronounced 'sam-un' (/ˈsæmən/) featuring a completely silent 'L', an open short 'A' (/æ/), and an unstressed second syllable reduced to a schwa (/ə/). Silent consonants similarly occur in 'half', 'almond', 'buffet' and 'filet' (silent 'T'), 'lamb' (silent 'B'), and American 'herb' (silent 'H'). Unstressed vowel reductions to schwa likewise govern food terms like 'lemon', 'carrot', and 'onion'.

### Rule 1: The Silent "L"

The "L" in salmon is completely silent. Do not say "Sal-mon". - Food words with silent letters: Salmon, half, almond (sometimes), buffet (silent T), filet (silent T), lamb (silent B), herb (silent H in American English). - Other silent L words: Lincoln, walk, talk, would, could, should.

### Rule 2: The Reduced "O" (Schwa)

The second syllable in "salmon" is not stressed. Therefore, the "O" does not sound like an "O". It reduces to an "uh" sound (the schwa). It is pronounced "Sam-un." - Other foods with a reduced O: Lemon (lem-un), melon, watermelon, carrot (car-rut), onion (un-yun).

### Rule 3: The Short "A" Sound (/a/)

The first letter of "salmon" uses the wide, short "A" sound (/a/). You must open your mouth wide and bring your tongue down. - Other foods with the short A: Half, sandwich, hamburger, cabbage, salad, eggplant.

## Advanced Business Idioms & Phrasal Verbs

### Summary

Workplace phrasal verbs include 'come across as' (project an impression), 'bring around' (persuade), 'wrap up' (conclude), and 'step down' (resign leadership). Common business idioms include 'run a tight ship' (manage with strict discipline), 'ballpark figure' (rough estimate), 'sweeten the deal' (add incentives), 'wear many hats' (juggle multiple roles), and 'bring to the table' (contribute valuable skills).

### Phrasal Verbs in Business

| Phrasal Verb | Meaning | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Come across (as) | The impression you make on other people. | "He came across as very confident in the interview." |
| Bring around | To persuade or convince someone to agree with you. | "It was hard, but I finally brought him around to my point of view." |
| Wrap up | To finish a task, meeting, or project. | "Before we wrap up the meeting, let's review the notes." |
| Bring on | To cause something (usually bad) to happen. | "Stress brings on my anxiety." |
| Bring it on! | An expression meaning: "I am ready for the challenge!" | "You want to play me in tennis? Bring it on!" |
| Step down | To resign or quit from an important leadership position. | "The CEO decided to step down." |

### Business Idioms

- Run a tight ship: To manage a team or business with strict control, discipline, and efficiency. "You can't be lazy; the boss runs a tight ship."
- Get down to business: To stop chatting and start focusing on the serious work. "Okay, let's get down to business."
- Get the ball rolling: To initiate an activity or take the first step. "Let's schedule a meeting to get the ball rolling."
- A ballpark figure: A rough estimate; an approximate number. "I don't need the exact price, just give me a ballpark figure."
- Sweeten the deal: To make a negotiation more attractive by adding an extra benefit. "To sweeten the deal, we'll offer a free gift."
- Go through the roof: To increase or rise significantly and rapidly. "Ticket sales went through the roof."
- Wear many hats: To have many different roles and responsibilities. "As a small business owner, I have to wear many hats."
- Bring someone up to speed: To update someone with the latest information. "I was on vacation. Can you bring me up to speed?"
- Bring (something) to the table: To contribute a valuable skill or asset to a team. "He brings strong leadership skills to the table."
- Deep pockets: Having abundant financial resources (usually referring to an investor or company). "We need an investor with deep pockets."

## Corporate Communication & Negotiation

### Summary

Corporate evaluation discussions leverage expressions like 'going above and beyond', 'taking on' new responsibilities, and positioning oneself as an 'asset' relative to industry 'peers'. Negotiation strategy requires leading conversations with data, framing proposals as reasonable 'asks', and avoiding blindsiding managers or reaching negotiation dead ends.

### Performance and Evaluation

- Go above and beyond: To do much more than what is expected or required in your job description. "He always goes above and beyond."
- Exceed: To surpass expectations or go past a limit. "I have been exceeding in my role."
- Take on: To accept a new responsibility or challenge. "I have taken on extra projects this quarter."
- An asset: A highly valuable and useful person or thing. "I am an asset to this company."
- Peers: People of a similar age, professional level, or status. "I am being underpaid compared to my peers."

### Corporate Communication Styles

- XYZ areas / Point A, B, C: Used as placeholder variables to summarize a list of items without naming them all. "I have improved in XYZ areas."
- Side note: A short comment that gives extra, but not essential, information. "As a side note, the deadline is Friday."
- Lead with: To start a conversation or presentation with a specific emphasis. "Always lead with the data."
- Open to: Willing to consider or accept something. "Are you open to a discussion about my salary?"
- Make your requests known: A formal way of saying "tell them what you want."
- Say your piece: To express your opinion or viewpoint. "I hope I get a chance to say my piece at the meeting."
- Get (something) out of: To receive a benefit or value from an experience. "Did you get what you needed out of that meeting?"

### Conflict and Challenges

- Catch off guard: To surprise or shock someone by doing something unexpectedly. "Don't catch your boss off guard with a surprise meeting."
- Blindside: To surprise or attack someone from an unexpected direction (a negative surprise). "I was blindsided when I got fired."
- Anticipate: To predict or plan for a future event. "I am anticipating a difficult conversation."
- An ask: (Used as a noun). A request or demand. "Asking for 30 minutes of their time is a reasonable ask."
- Feel defeated / Undervalued: Feeling like a failure, or feeling unappreciated. "When he ignored my email, I felt defeated and undervalued."
- Dead end: A situation where no more progress is possible. "If the negotiation fails, it becomes a dead end."

## Los Angeles Slang & Casual Conversation

### Summary

Casual descriptive phrases include 'born and raised' to designate origin, 'hands down' for indisputable certainty, 'the way to go' for optimal choices, and the suffix '-wise' to denote 'in terms of' (e.g., 'price-wise'). Action and contemporary slang include 'sleep in' (intentionally wake up late), 'hit' a place (visit casually), 'overplayed' (overused or stale), and 'fire' (exceptionally good).

### Descriptive Expressions

- Born and raised: Used to emphasize that you were born in a place and grew up there. "I am Los Angeles, born and raised."
- Hands down: Absolutely; without a doubt. "This is hands down the best burger in the world."
- The suffix "-wise": Added to a noun to mean "in terms of." "Price-wise, it's great. Taste-wise, it's okay."
- It beats...: It is better than something else. "This cheap coffee beats the expensive brand."
- Trusty: Reliable, dependable (often used affectionately). "I brought my trusty water bottle."
- Overplayed: Something that has been done or heard too much and is no longer special. "That song is overplayed." "Five Guys is overplayed."
- Jack of all trades: A person who is skilled in many different areas. (Brandon called herself a "Jane of all trades").
- Taste buds: The sensors on your tongue. "This meal will make your taste buds sing."

### Action Slang

- Sleep in: To intentionally sleep later than usual in the morning. "I love sleeping in on Sundays."
- Grab: To quickly get or take something (usually food/drink). "Let's grab a coffee." "Do you want to grab a bite?"
- The way to go: The best or most effective choice. "If you want a cheap meal, cooking at home is the way to go."
- Hit (a place): To go to a place casually. "Let's hit the farmer's market." "I need to hit the gym."

### Modern Slang

- Fire: Extremely good, impressive, or exciting. "That breakfast sandwich is absolutely fire."
- Hype: Excitement or buzz around a product or event (often due to limited availability). "I don't understand the hype around these bags."

## 15 Expressions About Money & Accuracy

### Summary

Financial capacity is expressed through idioms such as 'rolling in dough' (extremely wealthy), 'money to burn' (excess disposable cash), and 'not made of money', while value is gauged by 'getting your money's worth'. Certainty and precision are conveyed by 'bet your bottom dollar' (absolute conviction), 'right on the money' (exact), 'hit the nail on the head', and 'down to a T'.

### Money & Wealth Idioms

- Rolling in it / Rolling in dough: Extremely wealthy; having so much money you could roll around in it. ("Dough" is slang for money). "He's so rich, he's rolling in dough."
- Get your money's worth: To receive good value for the money you spent. "The concert was expensive, but it was 3 hours long. I definitely got my money's worth."
- Money talks: Money has power and influence to change rules or decisions. "How did he avoid going to jail? Well, money talks."
- Money to burn: Having so much money that you can spend it carelessly on useless things. "She bought another designer bag. She has money to burn."
- All kinds of money: A massive amount of money. "He's got all kinds of money."
- Not made of money: A response used when someone asks you to pay for too many things. "I can't buy you a new phone. I'm not made of money!"

### Feeling Great

- Look / Feel like a million bucks: To look gorgeous, or to feel incredibly healthy and happy. ("Bucks" is slang for dollars). "After my haircut, I feel like a million bucks."
- Feel on top of the world: To feel wonderful and unstoppable. "When I got the job, I felt on top of the world."
- A million dollar smile: A bright, perfect, beautiful smile (like a movie star). "Julia Roberts has a million dollar smile."

### Accuracy & Certainty

- Bet your bottom dollar: To be absolutely certain that something is true. "You can bet your bottom dollar that team will win."
- On the money / Right on the money: Exactly right; perfectly accurate. "Your prediction was right on the money."
- Hit the nail on the head: To say something that is exactly correct. "You hit the nail on the head when you described the problem."
- Down to a T: Perfectly or completely accurate. "That job suits you down to a T." "Follow the instructions down to a T."

### Bonus Vocabulary

- Give me a ride: To drive someone in your car. "Can you give me a ride to the airport?"
- Run on: What a machine uses for power. "My car doesn't run on water; I need gas money!"
- Run: To work or function (for a machine/engine). "My car is old, but it still runs great."

## 35 Expressions with the Word "Heart"

### Summary

Idioms containing 'heart' signify courage and character ('have heart', 'have a big heart'), transparent emotion ('wear your heart on your sleeve'), and lacking enthusiasm ('my heart isn't in it', 'half-hearted'). Sincerity, grief, and conviction are articulated through 'a heart-to-heart', 'from the bottom of my heart', 'with a heavy heart', and knowing something 'by heart' (entirely memorized).

### Emotion and Character

- Have heart: To be determined, brave, and ambitious (especially in sports). "He's not the biggest player, but he has heart."
- Have a big heart: To be generous and kind. "She has a big heart; she would give you the shirt off her back."
- Have a heart!: An expression asking someone to be compassionate. "Come on man, have a heart. He's having a hard time."
- Wear your heart on your sleeve: To show your emotions openly so everyone can see them. "You always know how he feels because he wears his heart on his sleeve." (You can also say: "It's written all over his face.")
- My heart isn't in it: To lack passion or motivation for an activity. "I can do the job, but my heart isn't in it."
- Half-hearted: Doing something with very little effort or passion. "He made a half-hearted attempt to apologize."
- Cold-hearted: Lacking empathy; cold and unfeeling.

### Conversations and Sincerity

- A heart-to-heart talk: A serious, emotional conversation (usually between loved ones or close friends). "We need to sit down and have a heart-to-heart."
- Cross my heart: A promise that you are telling the truth. (Often: Cross my heart and hope to die). "I won't tell anyone, cross my heart."
- From the bottom of my heart: Sincerely, completely, and wholeheartedly. "I want to thank you from the bottom of my heart."

### Sadness and Shock

- With a heavy heart: Experiencing deep sadness or grief. "It is with a heavy heart that I announce his passing."
- My heart goes out to you: An expression of deep sympathy for someone's pain. "I am so sorry for your loss. My heart goes out to you."
- Broken-hearted: Deeply hurt, usually because a romantic relationship ended. "He was broken-hearted when she broke up with him." (You can also say: "He is all torn up about it.")
- Give me a heart attack: To scare or surprise someone. "You jumped out of the closet and almost gave me a heart attack!"
- My heart skipped a beat: Being suddenly very surprised, shocked, or excited. "When I heard the news, my heart skipped a beat."

### Desire and Knowing

- Follow your heart: To do what you are passionate about, even if it isn't logical. (Similar to: Follow your gut instinct).
- Have your heart set on something: To deeply desire a specific goal and refuse to accept anything else. "I really had my heart set on going to Hawaii."
- The heart wants what it wants: An expression meaning you cannot control who you fall in love with, even if there are red flags (warning signs).
- Know something by heart: To have something perfectly memorized. "I know that song by heart." (Similar to: I know it like the back of my hand).

## Advanced Expressions for Money and Wealth

### Summary

Substantial wealth is denoted by 'deep pockets', 'high net worth', 'money is no object', and contrasting generational 'old money' with ostentatious 'new money'. Squandering wealth is described as 'money to blow' or 'money down the drain', whereas illicit transactions involve 'hush money' (silencing payoffs) and 'paying someone off' (bribery).

### Having Lots of Money

- Deep pockets: Having abundant financial resources. "We need an investor with deep pockets."
- Money is no object: The price does not matter because you are wealthy and willing to pay anything. "When she goes shopping, money is no object."
- New money vs. Old money: New money refers to people who recently became rich (and are often flashy). Old money refers to wealthy families who inherited their fortune across generations.
- High net worth: Having a very high total financial value (properties, stocks, cash). "He is a high net worth individual."
- Marry money: To marry someone who is rich. "She didn't work, she just married money."

### Spending and Wasting Money

- Money to blow / Money to burn: Having so much money that you can spend it carelessly on things you don't need. "He bought three sports cars; he has money to blow."
- Money down the drain: Wasting money completely on something useless or broken. "That cheap laptop was money down the drain."
- The best that money can buy: The highest quality item available on the market. "This watch is the best that money can buy."
- Money doesn't grow on trees: A warning that money must be earned through hard work. "Stop wasting electricity, money doesn't grow on trees!"
- Hard-earned money: Money made through difficult work. "I don't want to waste my hard-earned money."

### Making Money

- Bring in: To earn or produce profit. "How much do you bring in per month?"
- Make good money: To earn a comfortable, high salary. "She works as a nurse and makes good money."

### Crime and Secrets

- Hush money: Money paid to someone to keep them quiet about something illegal or embarrassing. "The politician paid him hush money."
- Pay someone off: To bribe someone. "The cartel paid off the guards."
- Rat someone out: To betray someone by telling the authorities about their bad actions. "He ratted out his best friend to the police."

## Reading the News: Advanced Journalistic Vocabulary

### Summary

Journalistic prose employs formal terminology such as 'household name' (widely recognized figure), 'candidly' (forthrightly), 'ousting' (removal from office), 'reinstatement', and deliberate 'disinformation' versus accidental misinformation. Pronunciation conventions require stressing the final letter of acronyms smoothly (A-I, C-E-O) and dropping the 'T' into a glottal transition in words like 'threaten' ('threa-uhn').

### Advanced Vocabulary

| Word / Phrase | Meaning | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Household name | Someone or something that is very famous and known by everyone. | "The actor became a household name." |
| Candidly (adverb) | Speaking openly, honestly, and directly. | "He spoke candidly about his mistakes." |
| Ousting (noun) | The act of forcing someone out of a position of power. The verb is "oust." | "The CEO's ousting shocked the company." |
| Reinstatement | Giving someone their job or position back after it was taken away. | "He demanded his reinstatement as leader." |
| Threaten to | To be likely to cause something negative, or to warn that you may do something. | "AI threatens to cause job losses." |
| Disinformation | False information spread intentionally to deceive people. (Misinformation = an accident). | "The election was targeted by disinformation." |
| Wide-ranging | Covering a large variety of subjects. | "They had a wide-ranging conversation." |
| Proved to be | Turned out to be (different from what was first expected). | "The test proved to be very difficult." |
| At large | As a whole; in general. | "The decision affects society at large." |
| Downsides | Negative parts or disadvantages. | "There are some downsides to this new technology." |
| Wary of | Cautious or suspicious of potential danger. | "You should be wary of strangers online." |
| Stands to | Has the potential to; is likely to. | "The company stands to make a lot of money." |

### Pronunciation Notes

- Acronyms: When pronouncing acronyms (like AI, CEO, FBI, ASAP), say them smoothly as one word and place the stress on the final letter. (A-I, C-E-O, F-B-I).
- Threaten: Do not say "threat-tens". Hold the T and drop straight to the N. "Threa-uhn". (Similar to cotton, button, curtain).

## A Blueprint for English Fluency

### Summary

Durable language mastery requires anchoring daily practice to an emotionally resonant 'big goal' and mentally translating routine native-language conversations into English immediately. Learners should utilize AI as an on-demand tutor to audit and explain written errors, deliberately targeting intimidating weak areas rather than settling into passive consumption.

### 1. Connect With a Big Goal

Motivation disappears quickly. You must visualize a "big goal" that emotionally connects you to the English language. When studying feels boring, think about why you started. Will English get you a dream job? Let you travel? Help you date? Visualize it.

### 2. Recreate Daily Conversations

A common problem is that learners are fluent in specific topics (like their job), but freeze when talking about daily life. The Strategy: Every time you have a conversation in your native language (e.g., at the mechanic, hair salon, dentist), imagine how you would say those exact phrases in English. If you don't know the vocabulary, look it up immediately.

### 3. Make AI Your Private Tutor

You do not need a native speaker to correct your grammar anymore. When you write something or say something, put your text into an AI (like ChatGPT) and write: "Correct my grammar mistakes and explain why they are wrong." It will identify your errors and teach you the rules.

### 4. Do What Scares You

If you only do what is easy (like passively watching YouTube videos), you will not grow. You must step out of your comfort zone. - If you hate grammar, study grammar. - If you are afraid of public speaking, record yourself giving a presentation. - If you are nervous talking to native speakers, go find them.

[!IMPORTANT] "Successful people do what unsuccessful people are not willing to do."

## Essential Expressions with "Go"

### Summary

Idiomatic constructions with 'go' convey initiative and intensity, including 'give it a go' (attempt), 'go all out' (exert maximum effort), and being a 'go-getter' (driven achiever). Boundary and status expressions include 'go overboard' (act excessively), 'don't go there' (avoid a sensitive topic), 'it's a go' (officially approved), and 'it's a no-go' (canceled).

### Trying and Effort

- Give it a go: To try doing something for the first time. "I've never skied before, but I'll give it a go." (You can also say: Take a whack at it).
- Go for it!: An expression of encouragement telling someone to take action without hesitation. "You want to start a business? Go for it!"
- Go all out: To put maximum effort, energy, and resources into something. "For her birthday, he went all out and booked an expensive restaurant."
- Go-getter: An ambitious, determined person who works hard to succeed. "She got promoted twice; she's a real go-getter."
- Go all the way: To fully complete a task and see it through to the end. "The team went all the way and won the championship."

### Excess and Rules

- Go overboard: To do way too much; to be excessive. "I went overboard at the gym and now I can't walk." "Don't go overboard with the decorations."
- Go out of your way: To make a special, extra effort to help someone. "He went out of his way to drive me to the airport."
- Don't go there: A warning meaning "do not talk about that topic." It implies the topic will cause anger or emotional distress. "When he brought up politics, I said 'Don't go there.'"

### Events and Planning

- It's a go: It has been officially approved or confirmed to happen. "The weather is good, so the concert is a go."
- It's a no-go: It will not happen; it is cancelled or impossible. "The tickets are sold out, so it's a no-go."
- Fall through: (Phrasal verb) When a plan or deal fails to happen. "Our trip to Italy fell through."
- It's go time: The exact moment to start taking action. "The presentation is in 5 minutes; it's go time!"

### Bonus Expressions

- I can attest to it: I can confirm that this is true based on my personal experience. "This restaurant is amazing, I can attest to it."
- The task at hand: The current job or problem that requires your immediate attention. "Let's stop chatting and focus on the task at hand."

## Upgrading Your Professional Vocabulary

### Summary

Professional greetings distinguish informal 'hey' (restricted to peers and friends), warm but polite 'hi' (standard workplace interactions), and formal 'hello' (interviews and senior clients). Generic praise words like 'cool' and 'awesome' should be replaced with high-register adjectives such as 'exceptional', 'commendable', 'remarkable', 'noteworthy', 'superb', or 'top-notch'.

### Hey vs. Hi vs. Hello

Choosing the right greeting depends on the situation and your relationship with the person.

- Hey: Super casual. Use it with friends or close co-workers in casual situations. Do not use it in a job interview or with a boss/client, as it makes you seem like you aren't taking the situation seriously.
- Example: "Hey John, do you want to grab lunch today?"
- Hi: Friendly, polite, and slightly more formal. It is perfect for most everyday workplace situations because it is warm but professional.
- Example: "Hi John, can we discuss the project today?"
- Hello: The most formal and polite greeting. Use this for job interviews, emails to senior colleagues, or meeting a client for the first time. Think of "hello" like wearing a suit.
- Example: "Hello Mr. Carter, I am here for the 10:00 AM interview."

### Stop Saying "Awesome" and "Cool"

"Awesome" and "cool" are acceptable with friends, but in a professional setting, they make you sound like you are not a clear thinker. Use these specific, high-level adjectives instead:

- Astonishing: "It's astonishing how quickly you finished the report."
- Exceptional: "He constantly delivers exceptional results."
- Magnificent: "She did a magnificent job on the project."
- Marvelous: "You gave a marvelous explanation of a complex concept."
- Outstanding: "His leadership was truly outstanding."
- Remarkable: "You showed remarkable leadership." (Adverb: Remarkably well).
- Breathtaking: "The company achieved breathtaking growth."
- Impressive: "The level of detail was truly impressive."
- Extraordinary: "He showed extraordinary leadership." (Adverb: Extraordinarily fast. Note: Do not pronounce the first "A").
- Commendable: "Your dedication to the tight deadline is highly commendable."
- Noteworthy: "It is noteworthy that the company expanded into three new markets."
- Top-notch: "The training program was top-notch this year."
- Admirable: "Your admirable effort did not go unnoticed."
- Phenomenal: "The product launch generated phenomenal results."
- Superb: "The service is superb."

## The Problem with "Awesome"

### Summary

Originating from 'awe', 'awesome' historically described terrifying grandeur but has suffered semantic bleaching through casual overextension to trivial contexts. In professional communications, replace 'awesome' with context-specific alternatives such as 'impressive', 'outstanding', 'brilliant', 'exceptional', or 'striking'.

### The History of "Awesome"

The word comes from the noun "awe" (meaning amazed or impressed). Historically, "awesome" and "awful" meant similar things: something so huge and powerful that it was terrifying. Today, we only use "awesome" to mean "good," and we use it to describe everything from a sandwich to a movie. Because it is used for everything, it has lost its descriptive power.

### Better Alternatives for the Workplace

When you replace "awesome" with a specific adjective, you sound more intelligent and observant.

- Instead of "He has an awesome resume," say: He has an impressive resume.
- Instead of "That's an awesome school," say: That's an outstanding school.
- Instead of "You did an awesome job," say: You did a brilliant job.

#### The Top 10 Alternatives

- Amazing
- Brilliant
- Astonishing
- Exceptional
- Fabulous
- Magnificent
- Marvelous
- Outstanding
- Remarkable
- Striking

## Hollywood Accent Secrets: Word Stress

### Summary

Sentence-level pitch and tonic stress determine emotional intent, as shifting emphasis between different words fundamentally alters implied subtext and factual narrative. Polished vocal delivery requires analyzing texts beforehand to identify core informative content words rather than emphasizing secondary function words.

### The Power of Word Stress

When you stress a specific word in a sentence, you change the emotion, the feeling, and the meaning of what you are trying to say.

Example 1: - Incorrect: "I got on the plane." (Sounds overly dramatic or emotional). - Correct: "I got on the plane." (A simple, factual statement).

Example 2: - Scenario A: "They ordered another drink." (Meaning: They were already drunk, but they ordered one more). - Scenario B: "They ordered another drink." (Meaning: They were just having a good time).

[!WARNING] If you are acting (or speaking in a meeting) and you stress the wrong word, you change the story you are trying to tell.

### How to Practice

- Analyze the lines: Before you speak, look at the sentence and ask yourself, "Where should I put the stress?"
- Find the true meaning: Determine what the most important piece of information is.
- Master the rules: Learn the foundational rules of English rhythm and melody so you know why words are stressed.

[!TIP] "Master the rules like a pro, so that you can break them like an artist." - Pablo Picasso

## 8 Daily Habits for a Perfect Accent

### Summary

Developing an authentic American accent requires consistent oral repetition, shadowing native audio recordings, reviewing voice memos to audit pacing and errors, and learning International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA) symbols. Progress is reinforced by reviewing a personal vocabulary notebook daily, watching subtitled native media, and actively eliminating speech-diluting filler words.

### Anita's 8 Practice Techniques

- Be consistent and persistent: Anita practices for at least 1 hour a day, 5 days a week. You must practice regularly and not give up.
- Listen and Repeat: Use audio accent reduction materials. Listen to the native speaker, pause, and repeat exactly what they said.
- Read out loud and record yourself: Read articles out loud while recording your voice. Listen back to check if your pacing is too slow and if you are making pronunciation errors.
- Learn the IPA: Learn the International Phonetic Alphabet. If you don't know how to pronounce a word, you can check the dictionary and read the exact phonetic symbols.
- Talk to yourself: You don't need a conversation partner. Talk to yourself in English about your day or your thoughts.
- Keep a vocabulary notebook: Write down new expressions and review five pages of your notebook every single day.
- Watch TV with subtitles: Watch American shows, pause when you hear an unfamiliar slang word or expression, and write it down.
- Eliminate filler words: Filler words "water down" your speech and make you sound unconfident (e.g., like, basically, anyways, whatever). Anita uses an app to track and eliminate her filler words.

### The Reality of Language Learning

- Talent vs. Hard Work: "Hard work beats talent when talent doesn't work hard." You do not need a "good ear for languages" if you are willing to practice.
- The Plateau: Language progress is not a straight line. You will practice for weeks with no visible progress, and then suddenly "jump" to the next level of fluency. Don't quit during the plateau.

## 5 Steps to Perfect Pronunciation

### Summary

Pronunciation training advances sequentially from isolated phoneme drills to identifying and reading target sounds within authentic non-textbook articles. Learners then reinforce motor patterns through spoken self-talk, recorded spoken self-assessments, and finally interactive conversations monitored with periodic 30-second focus checks.

### Step 1: Practice with a Book or Video

Start by listening to a teacher or an audio file and repeating the target sound. The goal is to train your mouth muscles to create the sound perfectly in isolation. (Example: Practicing the short "A" in apple or bad).

### Step 2: Get Away from the Book

Textbook sentences are artificial. Find a real-life text (like a news article or a speech). Print it out, and underline every instance of your target sound. Read the paragraph out loud 10 times, training your brain to spot the sound in natural sentences.

### Step 3: Speak to Yourself Out Loud

You are not ready for a conversation yet. Start talking to yourself in English while doing chores (like washing dishes or driving). Pay attention to your pronunciation. - Thought: "I need to call my friend back." - Correction: "Wait, did I say 'back' or 'buck'? Let me say 'back' again."

### Step 4: Record Yourself Speaking

Speak freely into your phone's voice memo app for 2 to 3 minutes about any topic. Listen to the recording specifically listening for the sound you are practicing. If you hear a mistake, pause the recording and say the sentence correctly.

### Step 5: Speak with People

Now you are ready for real conversations. The goal here is to relax and communicate. You don't need to overthink every word. Just occasionally, for 30 seconds at a time, remind yourself to focus on your target sound.

## American Brands: Pronunciation and Origins

### Summary

American brand pronunciations feature specific vowel patterns: 'YouTube' uses an /uː/ ('oo') sound rather than British /juː/, 'Instagram' reduces its medial vowel to a schwa, and 'WhatsApp' pairs initial stress with short /æ/. Brand names reflect distinct histories: 'tube' referenced cathode-ray television tubes, 'Netflix' derives from movie slang 'flicks', and 'Nike' retains a voiced final /iː/.

### The Brands

- YouTube: In American English, "tube" is pronounced with an "oo" sound (not "tyoob" like in British English). Why "tube"? Before flat screens, televisions had a physical glass tube inside them, so people called the TV "the tube."
- Twitter: To "tweet" or "twitter" means to make a short, high-pitched sound like a bird. It also means to talk about silly, unimportant things.
- Instagram: A combination of "Instant camera" and "Telegram." Reduce the first "A" to a schwa (uh): In-stuh-gram.
- Netflix: "Net" refers to the internet. "Flicks" is an informal slang word for movies. (e.g., A chick flick is a romantic comedy movie). Make sure the "i" in flicks is a neutral sound, not an "ee" sound.
- WhatsApp: Don't confuse this with the greeting "What's up". "App" uses the short "A" sound (like Apple). "Up" uses the short "U" sound (like Jump). We stress the first word: WhatsApp.
- Apple: Open your mouth wide and keep your tongue down. A-pple.
- PayPal: "Pal" is an informal word for friend. Use a big "A" sound for Pal.
- Amazon: Stress the first syllable and reduce the second "a" to an "uh" sound. Am-uh-zon.
- Facebook: Stress the first syllable. The double "O" is pronounced like the word "book" or "foot", not like "food".
- Walmart: Pronounced with an "aw" sound (like the word "wall"). Wall-mart.
- McDonald's: Reduce the "A" in Mc to a schwa (uh). The "O" is pronounced as an "ah" sound (like "hot"). Muck-Dah-nalds.
- Nike: Unlike "bike" or "mike", the "e" at the end of Nike is pronounced. Nai-kee.

## Pronouncing Tricky States and Cities

### Summary

U.S. place names derived from French feature silent consonants, seen in 'Illinois' (silent 's', ending in /ɔɪ/), 'Arkansas' (/ˈɑːrkənsɔː/), and 'Des Moines' (silent 's' in both words). Two-word toponyms place primary stress on the second word ('New York'), intervocalic 't' before 'l' flaps to a soft tap ('Seattle'), and '-land' endings reduce to schwa ('Maryland').

### Silent Letters and French Origins

Many places in the US were named by French explorers, which results in silent letters. 1. Illinois: The "S" is silent. The "ois" ending is pronounced "wah". Ill-ih-noy. 2. Des Moines: The capital of Iowa. The "S" in both words is silent. Deh-Moyn. 3. Arkansas: The "S" is silent. It looks like Kansas, but it kept its French pronunciation. Ar-kan-saw.

### Native American Origins

- Chicago: Originally referred to wild onions. The French spelled it with a "Ch", which is pronounced as an "Sh". Shi-cah-go.
- Connecticut: The second "C" is completely silent. Kuh-net-ih-cut.

### Important Rules

- Two-word names: When a place has two words, stress the second word and say them together without pausing.
- New York, Las Vegas, San Diego.
- Flap T: When a "T" is between a vowel and an "L", it becomes a soft "D" or "R" sound (Flap T).
- Seattle, little, bottle.
- Reduced "Land": For states and countries ending in "land", reduce the "a" to a schwa sound (uh). Do not pronounce it like the word "land."
- Maryland, England, Scotland.

### Difficult Pronunciations

- Albuquerque: Al-buh-ker-kee.
- Tucson: The "C" is silent. Too-sahn.
- Boise: Boy-see.
- Iowa: Reduce the "o". Eye-uh-wuh.
- Nevada: Neh-vah-duh.

## 10 Mispronounced Business Words

### Summary

Workplace vocabulary pairs distinguish word class via stress: 'AN-a-lyze' (verb) versus 'uh-NAL-y-sis' (noun), and 'EX-e-cute' (/ks/) versus 'ex-EC-u-tive' (/ɡz/). 'De-VEL-op' places stress on the second syllable with a reduced 'o', 'suite' is pronounced identically to 'sweet', 'quote' begins with /kw/, and 'figure' is articulated as 'fig-yer' (/ˈfɪɡjər/).

### The Top 10 Words

- Analyze vs. Analysis:
- Analyze (Verb): Stress the first syllable.
- Analysis (Noun): Stress the second syllable and reduce the first "A" to a schwa (uh). Uh-nal-uh-sis.
- Execute vs. Executive:
- Execute (Verb): Stress the first syllable. The "X" makes a "KS" sound.
- Executive (Noun): Stress the second syllable. The "X" makes a "GZ" sound.
- Develop: Stress the second syllable. Reduce the "O" to a schwa (uh). De-vel-uhp.
- Focus: The "O" is pronounced like "Oh." Round your lips. Reduce the "U" to a schwa. Foh-kuhs.
- Quote: An estimate of costs. The "QU" makes a "KW" sound. Kw-oat.
- Suite vs. Suit:
- Suite (Hotel room / Office number): Pronounced exactly like "sweet".
- Suit (Business clothing): Pronounced like "soon" with a T. Soot.
- Cooperate: Insert a small "W" sound to connect the two O's. Co-wah-per-ate.
- Increase / Decrease: Make sure the final "S" is sharp, not a "Z".
- If it's a Verb, stress the second syllable: "They in-creased his salary."
- If it's a Noun, stress the first syllable: "He got a salary in-crease."
- Employee: Words ending in "ee" generally stress the final syllable. Em-ploy-ee.
- Figure: The "U" makes an "er" sound, not a "yoor" sound. Fig-yer. In the past tense, the "D" connects to the next word. "I figured it out."

## Mastering the American "ER" Sound

### Summary

American English produces an identical retroflex /ɜːr/ phoneme across the spelling patterns EAR ('learn'), UR ('hurt'), OR ('work', 'world'), IR ('girl'), and ER ('her', 'were'). 'Were' must be pronounced with this vowel rather than rhyming with 'where', and 'work' must be articulated distinctly from 'walk' by sustaining the tight /ɜːr/ posture.

### The Rule

In American English, the following spellings all make the exact same "ER" sound:

- EAR: Learn, Early, Earth
- UR: Hurt, Thursday
- OR: Work, Word, World, Worth
- IR: Girl, First, Circle
- ER: Her, Were, German, Perfect

### Common Mistakes

- Were: A major mistake is pronouncing "were" like "where". We do not say "We where happy." We say "We wer happy."
- Work vs. Walk: "Work" uses the ER sound. Do not pronounce it like "walk" or "ward".
- World: Do not say "wah-rold."

### Practice Sentences

Read these sentences out loud, ensuring that the target words all have the exact same "ER" sound:

- "The nurse is working early on Thursday."
- "It's not a perfect world."
- "The girls were working early."
- "Is the first one worse than the third one?"
- "Don't worry about learning all the words."

## Pronouncing Numbers and Dates

### Summary

Numbers ending in '-teen' stress the second syllable (four-TEEN), whereas '-ty' multiples stress the initial syllable (FOR-ty). Medial 't' in 20, 30, 40, 70, 80, and 90 softens into a flap 'd' ('thir-dy'), while 50 and 60 preserve crisp aspirated 't' sounds. Multi-unit expressions (prices, years, phone number groupings) stress the terminal digit, and decade ordinals insert an extra /iː/ syllable ('twen-tee-eth').

### 1. Word Stress for -Teen vs. -Ty

- Teen (13-19): Stress the second syllable. (four-TEEN, fif-TEEN).
- Ty (20-90): Stress the first syllable. (FOR-ty, FIF-ty).

### 2. The "Ty" becomes a "Dy"

When a number ends in zero, the "ty" often changes to a soft "dy" sound (a flap T). - We do not say "thir-ty", we say "thir-dy". - 30, 40, 70, 80, and 90 all use the "dy" sound. - Exceptions: 50 and 60 keep a strong "T" sound.

### 3. Stress the Last Number

When giving a year, price, or time, always stress the final number in the sequence. - Year: "1990". "1880". - Price: "It costs 17.99." - Time: "It's 5:15."

#### Phone Numbers

When giving a phone number, group the numbers and stress the last number of each group. Pronounce zeros as "oh". - Incorrect: 5-5-5-2-2-8 - Correct: 310 (pause) 555 (pause) 8239.

### 4. Ordinal Numbers (The "TH" sound)

When a number ends with a zero (20, 30, 40), we insert an extra syllable (an "e" sound) before the "TH". - We don't say "twen-tyth". We say "twen-tee-eth". - "Her birthday is July 20th." - "It is his 40th birthday."

## The Rhythm and Melody of American English

### Summary

American spoken cadence relies on stressing meaning-bearing content words while reducing grammatical function words (pronouns, prepositions, auxiliaries). In phrase structures, nouns take precedence over preceding verbs and adjectives, establishing the rhythmic peaks that govern natural sentence comprehension.

### General Rules of Word Stress

In a sentence, we emphasize the words that carry the most meaning.
