- Pronouns and Prepositions are reduced: Words like I, it, him, with, to are usually not stressed.
- Nouns get more stress than Verbs: If a sentence has a verb and a noun, the noun usually receives the main stress.
- Nouns get more stress than Adjectives: If a sentence has an adjective and a noun, the noun still receives the main stress.

### Analyzing a Sentence

Look at how the stress shifts as we build a sentence:

- "I watched it."
- Watched is stressed because I and it are pronouns.
- "I watched a video."
- We have a verb (watched) and a noun (video). We stress video more than watched.
- "I watched a good video."
- We have an adjective (good) and a noun (video). We stress video more than good.
- "I watched a good video with him."
- With (preposition) and him (pronoun) are reduced. The main stress remains on video.
- "I watched a good video with my friend."
- Friend is a noun. Now, the sentence ends with a strong noun, so friend receives stress as well.

## Eliminating Filler Words

### Summary

Filler words such as 'like', 'actually', 'kind of', and 'um' undermine professional credibility, obscure meaning, and signal hesitation. Speakers should replace vague conversational crutches with exact terminology, track personal usage via recorded speech, and embrace silent pauses rather than vocalizing hesitation.

### The Problem with Filler Words

Filler words are words that do not add any meaning to your sentence. Examples include: kind of, sort of, like, actually, the thing is, um, and stuff like that.

Using these words makes you sound: - Less intelligent - Less educated - Less confident (unsure of what you want to say) - Like a teenager

[!WARNING] Professional leaders and good communicators know exactly what they want to say and they use precise vocabulary instead of fillers.

### Replacing and Eliminating

- Be Precise: Instead of saying "It cost like $100," say "It cost approximately $100."
- Don't water down descriptions: If you say, "I was like tired," the word like has zero meaning. Either you were tired or you were not. Same with "We kind of decided to go to the beach." Just say, "We decided to go to the beach."

### 3 Tips to Stop Using Fillers

- Record your voice: Talk for a few minutes, play it back, and count your filler words. Then, re-record it and try to eliminate them.
- Embrace silence: If you are not sure what you are going to say, don't say anything. Don't fill the silence with an "um." Just pause.
- Improve your vocabulary: The more words you know, the easier it is to express yourself accurately without relying on "stuff like that."

## 8 Steps to English Fluency

### Summary

Achieving advanced fluency demands concrete operational goals, embracing errors as learning mechanisms, and mastering grammatical rules incrementally. Active verbal and written production must take priority over passive listening, guided by the realization that fluency advances through plateaus followed by sudden developmental leaps.

### The 8 Tips and Proverbs

- Have a specific plan.
- Proverb: "A goal without a plan is only a dream."
- Action: Don't just say "I want to be fluent." Define exactly what you will do today, this week, and this month.
- Don't be afraid to make mistakes.
- Proverb: "Fortune favors the bold."
- Action: If you are afraid to speak, your brain will freeze. Be brave and take risks.
- Don't do too much at once.
- Proverb: "Don't bite off more than you can chew."
- Action: Learn systematically. Master one grammar rule or pronunciation sound before moving to the next.
- Practice regularly.
- Proverb: "Practice makes perfect" AND "There's no elevator to success. You must take the stairs."
- Action: Stop relying on passive knowledge (listening). You must practice actively (speaking and writing).
- Practice efficiently.
- Proverb: "Tell me and I'll forget. Show me and I may remember. Involve me and I'll understand."
- Action: Be fully involved. Keep a journal, record your voice, and use the language creatively.
- Have realistic expectations.
- Proverb: "Rome wasn't built in a day" AND "Slow and steady wins the race."
- Action: The learning curve is a plateau, not a straight line. You will not see progress every day, but if you stay consistent, you will experience sudden "jumps" in fluency.
- Make English a priority.
- Proverb: "Where there's a will, there's a way" AND "First things first."
- Action: Stop procrastinating. Do the most important things first.
- Maintain a positive mindset.
- Proverb: "Hard work beats talent when talent doesn't work hard."
- Action: Stop telling yourself you are "too old" or "not talented." Your attitude determines your success.

## The Secret to True Fluency

### Summary

Language learners typically split between 'grammarians', who have strong structural knowledge but lack casual fluency, and 'conversationalists', who speak confidently with idioms but exhibit fossilized structural flaws. Breaking fluency plateaus requires attacking personal weaknesses: analytical students must practice spontaneous colloquial speech, while conversationalists must methodically study foundational grammar.

### The Two Types of Students

Category 1: The Grammarian - Strengths: Has studied English formally for years. Grammar is perfect. Excellent foundation in tenses and writing. - Weaknesses: Lacks everyday vocabulary and idioms. Insecure when speaking casually or making "small talk" with native speakers.

Category 2: The Conversationalist - Strengths: Very social, learns by listening to movies and people. Speaks confidently and uses lots of slang and idioms. - Weaknesses: Lacks a foundation in grammar rules. Makes many structural errors, making them feel insecure in formal, professional environments (like writing emails or giving presentations).

### The Secret to Fluency

Do the thing that you do not want to do.

Stop avoiding your weaknesses. - If you are in Category 1, you must force yourself outside of your comfort zone. Stop being analytical and afraid of making mistakes. Speak, experiment, and use idioms. - If you are in Category 2, you must go back to the basics. Sit down with a textbook or a teacher and study the grammar rules you have been avoiding for years.

[!IMPORTANT] "Life is easy when you live it the hard way, and hard when you live it the easy way." Do the hard work now, and the rest of your English journey will be easy.

## Linking: Connecting Words for Fluency

### Summary

Consonant-to-vowel linking connects words into smooth acoustic streams by shifting terminal consonants onto subsequent initial vowels (e.g., 'hold on' becomes 'hol-don', 'speak English' becomes 'spea-Kenglish'). This phonetic blending enforces natural sentence stress, creates melodic fluidity, and eases articulation by moving difficult final consonant stops into initial positions.

### The Rule: Consonant to Vowel

When a word ends in a consonant and the next word begins with a vowel, you push that final consonant over so it becomes the beginning of the next word.

- I speak English -> "I spea Kenglish."
- Hold on -> "Hol Don."
- Take a walk -> "Ta Ka walk."
- Five hours (The H is silent, so it acts like a vowel) -> "Fi Vours."

### The 3 Benefits of Linking

- You sound more native: Beginners speak word-by-word. Fluent speakers link words together for smooth, natural speech. (Remember: linking does not mean speaking fast. It just means speaking smoothly).
- It forces you to use correct word stress: When a sentence is linked together into one long sound, you are forced to emphasize the key words so people can understand you.
- Consonants become easier to pronounce: Pronouncing consonants at the end of a word is physically difficult (e.g., cooked, worked, is). By linking, you move that difficult consonant to the beginning of the next word, making it much easier to say.
- My car is -> "My ca Ris."
- I cooked it -> "I cook Tit."
- I worked out -> "I work Tout."

### Practice Sentences

Read these out loud, pushing the bolded consonant to the start of the next word: 1. I love dit. 2. I live i na napartment. 3. It's five o'clock. (Fi Vo'clock) 4. Did you nee dit?

## Fixing Fossilized Grammar Mistakes

### Summary

Fossilized grammatical errors cannot be resolved by theoretical awareness alone; they require targeted one-on-one professional feedback and intensive oral repetition to reprogram speech motor habits. Informal language exchange partners should be utilized for conversational immersion rather than rule explanations, which require formal grammar resources.

### 1. Get Professional Feedback

You need someone who is dedicated to correcting your mistakes. Friends and colleagues will not correct you because they just want to understand your message, and it feels rude to interrupt. - Action: Hire a one-on-one tutor (Lisa recommends platforms like italki). Group classes are not effective for this because the teacher cannot focus on your specific, individual errors.

### 2. Retrain Your Brain

If you have been making the same mistake for years, it has become a habit. Knowing the rule is not enough to break the habit. - Action: You must reprogram your brain through repetition. If you realize you frequently mess up the past conditional ("If I had studied, I would have passed"), you must write down the correct sentence and say it out loud 50 times. You have to train your mouth muscles and brain to default to the correct pattern automatically.

### 3. Find a Language Exchange Partner

A language exchange partner is a free way to get speaking practice. You speak English for 30 minutes, and then you speak their target language for 30 minutes. - Warning: Do not expect a language exchange partner to explain why something is wrong. Native speakers do not know grammar rules; they only know what "sounds right." Use them for correction, but use a professional teacher or textbook to learn the rules.

### 4. Review Grammar Books

Sometimes we make mistakes simply because we forgot the rules or never learned them properly. - Action: Buy a high-quality grammar book (like Understanding and Using English Grammar or Grammar in Use). Do the boring exercises. After learning a rule, create your own sentences applying the rule to your real life.

## 10 Fluency Tips from a Hollywood Performer

### Summary

Accelerating oral fluency requires actively soliciting real-time corrections, self-correcting mid-sentence, and undertaking challenging public tasks in English. Learners should audit personal voice recordings, convert commuting time into focused podcast listening, and maintain a rigorous vocabulary notebook.

### Marina's Intense Routine

- Ask for corrections: Tell everyone you meet (makeup artists, random people on set) to correct your English. "Did I say it correctly? How would you say it?"
- Correct yourself immediately: If you notice you made a mistake mid-sentence, stop talking and say the sentence again correctly.
- Do something difficult: Force yourself outside your comfort zone. Marina started posting all her Instagram stories and captions in English. It took hours in the beginning, but it forced her to learn.
- Challenge yourself daily: Read difficult news articles or try recording a YouTube video in English.
- Embrace failure: "Everything you start in the beginning, you will suck." Marina says she is a perfectionist, but she fails on purpose so she can eventually be perfect.
- Massive repetition: Do the same exercise "millions of times" until it becomes natural.
- Record yourself: Record your voice, listen back, identify why it sounds terrible, and record it again.
- Listen to podcasts: Turn your car into a classroom. Never listen to music while driving; only listen to useful English audio.
- Keep a vocabulary notebook: Watch movies with English subtitles. Pause constantly, look up words in Google Translate, write them in a notebook, and read the notebook repeatedly.
- Be patient: Just like learning to do a physical split in gymnastics, fluency takes daily stretching. You can't achieve it in two days.

## Man vs. Men: The /ae/ and /e/ Sounds

### Summary

The /æ/ vowel in 'man' requires an open jaw, a tense vocal tract, and the tongue tip positioned behind the lower teeth, whereas /e/ in 'men' utilizes a relaxed, slightly opened mouth. British English frequently shifts /æ/ to /ɑː/ before 's' ('cl-ah-ss'), while American English maintains a broad /æ/ across pairs like 'man'/'men', 'pan'/'pen', and 'bad'/'bed'.

### 1. Mouth Position

- /ae/ (Man): The mouth is open very wide. It is a big, tense sound.
- /e/ (Men): The mouth is only open a little bit. It is a relaxed sound.

### 2. Tongue Position

- /ae/ (Man): The tip of the tongue touches the bottom teeth. The tongue is wide, tense, and pulled down.
- Brand examples: Apple, Amazon, Samsung.
- /e/ (Men): The tongue is very relaxed.
- Brand examples: FedEx, Pepsi.

### 3. British vs. American English

In British English, the /ae/ sound is often pronounced as a deep "ah" sound, especially if it is followed by an "s". - Class: British (cl-ah-ss), American (cl-ae-ss) - Glass: British (gl-ah-ss), American (gl-ae-ss)

### Practice Word Pairs

Read these pairs out loud, ensuring your mouth opens much wider for the first word than the second word: - Man / Men - Pan / Pen - Bad / Bed - And / End - Gas / Yes (Notice the vowel difference)

## Desert vs. Dessert

### Summary

The noun 'desert' (arid terrain) places primary stress on the first syllable (DE-sert), whereas the verb 'desert' (to abandon) and the confectionery noun 'dessert' both place primary stress on the second syllable (de-SSERT). All three lexical items pronounce their medial sibilant as a voiced /z/.

### The Three Meanings

- DE-sert (Noun): A dry area of land with sand.
- Stress: First syllable.
- Example: The Sahara is a massive de-sert.
- de-SSERT (Verb): To abandon or leave someone/something.
- Stress: Second syllable.
- Example: He de-sert-ed the army. The beach was completely de-sert-ed.
- de-SSERT (Noun): A sweet food eaten after a meal.
- Stress: Second syllable.
- Example: I had chocolate cake for de-ssert.

[!TIP] Notice that the verb "to desert" and the noun "dessert" (sweet food) are pronounced exactly the same way!

## Pronouncing "Water" (American Accent)

### Summary

Pronouncing American 'water' involves tightly rounding the lips for /w/, opening the mouth into a broad 'ah' (/ɑː/) vowel, and articulating the intervocalic 't' as a voiced flap tap (/ɾ/). The word finishes with a retracted tongue producing a strong retroflex American /ər/ ('wah-der').

### Step-by-Step Breakdown

- The W: Round your lips tightly. (W-w-w).
- The A: Open your mouth really big to create an "ah" sound. (Wah).
- The Flap T: Because the "t" is surrounded by two vowels (a and e), Americans do not pronounce a sharp "T". Instead, it becomes a "Flap T", which sounds like a very quick "D" or a rolled "R" in other languages. (Wah-der).
- The R: At the end of the word, pull your tongue back to create a strong American "R". (Wah-derrr).

## Review: Pronouncing Numbers in American English

### Summary

Number pronunciation differentiates '-teen' suffixes (second-syllable stress) from '-ty' multiples (first-syllable stress). Medial 't' in 20, 30, 40, 70, 80, and 90 converts to a flap /ɾ/ ('for-dy'), while 50 and 60 retain sharp /t/ articulations. Numerical sequences (years, prices, times) place tonic prominence on the final digit, and round ordinals insert an /iː/ before the suffix ('twen-tee-eth').

### Rule 1: Word Stress (-Teen vs. -Ty)

- -Teen: Stress the second syllable. (four-TEEN, fif-TEEN).
- -Ty: Stress the first syllable. (FOR-ty, FIF-ty).

### Rule 2: The "Ty" becomes a "Dy"

When a number ends in zero (like 20, 30, 40), the "T" changes to a soft "D" sound (a Flap T). - We say thir-dy, for-dy, nine-dy. - Exceptions: 50 and 60 keep the strong "T" sound (fif-ty, six-ty).

### Rule 3: Stress the Last Number

When saying a group of numbers (like a price, a year, or a time), always stress the final number. - "He was born in 1990." - "The total is $17.99." - "It is 5:15."

### Rule 4: Ordinal Numbers

For numbers ending in zero (20, 30, 40), we must add an extra vowel sound (an "ee") before the "TH" ending. - 20th is pronounced twen-tee-eth (not twen-tyth). - 40th is pronounced for-tee-eth.

## Weather and Temperature Vocabulary

### Summary

Meteorological vocabulary requires stressing the second syllable in 'ther-MOM-eh-ter', compressing 'temperature' into three syllables ('TEM-pruh-chur'), and stressing the initial noun in compound terms ('weather forecast'). Cooling systems distinguish individual units ('air conditioner') from integrated structures ('air conditioning').

### Measuring Temperature

- Thermometer: Do not say "thermo-meter". Stress the second syllable. Ther-mom-eh-ter. (This applies to other instruments too: speedometer, odometer).
- Temperature: This word only has 3 syllables. The "ture" ending is pronounced "chur". Tem-pruh-chur.
- Celsius: Stress the first syllable. Sell-see-us.
- Fahrenheit: Americans use Fahrenheit, not Celsius. 35 degrees Celsius is equal to 95 degrees Fahrenheit. Fair-en-height.

### Keeping Cool

- Air Conditioner: A single cooling unit (e.g., in a window).
- Air Conditioning: A system installed throughout an entire building.
- Air-Conditioned: An adjective describing a room. "I work in an air-conditioned building."
- The AC: The most common abbreviation. "Please turn on the AC." (Stress the "C").

[!NOTE] Americans love air conditioning. If you visit the US in the summer, buildings are often kept extremely cold (freezing), so it's a good idea to bring a sweater!

### Compound Nouns

When a noun is made of two words, stress the first word: - Weather forecast - Outdoor thermometer

## Pronouncing "THE" Correctly

### Summary

The definite article 'the' reduces to a schwa ('thuh', /ðə/) before words beginning with consonant sounds ('the dog'), but shifts to a long 'E' ('thee', /ðiː/) before words beginning with vowel sounds ('the apple'). When 'thee' precedes a vowel, link the adjacent sounds smoothly using an unwritten transitional /j/ glide ('thee-y-end').

### Rule 1: The "TH" Sound

The tip of your tongue must lightly touch the bottom of your upper teeth. Do not bite your tongue. Just let the air flow out so that it lightly tickles your teeth.

### Rule 2: "Thuh" vs. "Thee"

The pronunciation changes depending on whether the following word begins with a consonant or a vowel sound.

- Before a Consonant -> "Thuh": Reduce the vowel to a schwa (uh).
- The dog, the car, the meeting.
- Before a Vowel -> "Thee": Pronounce the vowel like a long "E".
- The apple, the end, the internet, the afternoon.

### Rule 3: The Connecting "Y"

When you say "Thee" before a vowel, you must connect the two words using a very small, invisible "Y" sound. Do not stop talking between the two words. - The(y)apple - The(y)afternoon - The(y)internet

### Practice Sentences

Read these out loud, using "Thee" and a connecting "Y" sound: 1. "This is the end of the film." 2. "The traffic is heavy in the afternoon." 3. "He is the only person who knows the truth." 4. "I can't find the answer to the question."

## Money and Finance Vocabulary

### Summary

Financial vocabulary contains silent consonants in 'debt' (silent 'b', /dɛt/), 'mortgage' (silent 't', /ˈmɔːrɡɪdʒ/), and 'budget' (silent 'd', /ˈbʌdʒɪt/). 'Interest' drops its medial 'e' and articulates 'tr' as /tʃr/ ('in-chrest'), while 'owe' (debt obligation) must be grammatically distinguished from 'own' (legal possession).

### Silent Letters

- Debt: The "B" is completely silent. Do not say "depth." Det.
- Mortgage: The "T" is completely silent, and the "A" is reduced to an "ih" sound. Mor-gihj.
- Budget: The "D" is silent, and the "U" sounds like "uh". Buh-jit.

### Tricky Pronunciations

- Interest: The first "E" is silent. Also, the "TR" combination makes a "CHR" sound. In-chrest. (e.g., Interest rate).
- Withdraw: This is difficult because you must pronounce the "TH" and the "D" back-to-back. Also, the "AW" makes an "ah" sound, and the "DR" makes a "JR" sound. With-jrah.
- ATM: Stands for Automated Teller Machine. Stress the final letter: ATM.
- Deposit: Stress the second syllable. The "O" sounds like "ah". De-pah-zit.

### Owe vs. Own

Do not confuse these two! - Owe: You borrowed money and must pay it back. ("I owe the bank $100.") - Own: You possess something. ("I own a car.") - Common Mistake: Do not say "I own you five dollars." It is "I owe you five dollars."

### Idiom: Champagne Taste on a Beer Budget

This is a funny expression used to describe someone who loves very expensive, luxury items (champagne) but does not have the money to afford them (beer budget).

## Fixing a Monotone Voice

### Summary

Monotone vocal delivery stems from cultural conditioning, introversion, or cognitive fatigue from grammatical monitoring. Correcting it requires physical gesturing, exaggerating lip and jaw mobility on vowels, and lengthening the duration and pitch of stressed keywords to create energetic acoustic contour.

### Why Do People Sound Monotone?

- Cultural Differences: Americans are very expressive. In other cultures, being reserved is preferred.
- Introversion: Shy people naturally use less body language and speak more quietly.
- Language Insecurity: When you are focusing on grammar, you forget about emotion.

### 4 Tips for a Dynamic Voice

#### 1. Move Your Body

"Motion creates emotion." When you are excited, you naturally move. - Professional voice actors stand up when recording. Try standing up during important phone calls. - Pull your shoulders back and use your hands when speaking.

#### 2. Move Your Face

Action heroes in movies barely move their mouths. Do not imitate them! - Exaggerate your lip and jaw movements. Open your mouth wide on the vowel sounds. - A mobile, active face creates a clear, energetic voice.

#### 3. Borrow Passion

If you are giving a presentation on a boring topic, pretend you are talking about something you love. Imagine your favorite sports team just won a championship or you just got your dream job. Use that same level of energetic body language and apply it to the boring topic.

#### 4. Stress the Keywords

When you stress a word, you make it louder, longer, and higher in pitch. By stretching out the keywords, you automatically break the monotone rhythm. - "It is very nice to meeeeet you." - "Thank you all for cooooming."

[!TIP] Enjoy speaking. Remember that you have something important to say, and you deserve to be heard. Use a strong, powerful voice.

## The Suffix "-Esque"

### Summary

The suffix '-esque' attaches to nouns to signify 'resembling', 'in the style of', or 'like'. Standard applications include 'picturesque' (visually charming like a picture), 'statuesque' (tall, poised, and dignified like a statue), and 'modelesque' (resembling the physique of a fashion model).

### Common Examples

- Picturesque: Beautiful, like a picture. ("We visited a small, picturesque village.")
- Statuesque: Tall, beautiful, and dignified like a statue.
- Modelesque: Resembling the physique or style of a fashion model. ("Those exercises will help you achieve a modelesque physique.")

## Idioms and Advanced Grammar in Context

### Summary

Conversational idioms include 'bring up' (introduce a subject), 'beat yourself up' (engage in excessive self-blame), 'set the tone for' (establish an ongoing mood), and 'get over the hump' (pass the hardest phase). Formal dramatic emphasis employs negative inversion, fronting negative adverbials ('never', 'seldom', 'at no time') followed by inverted auxiliary-subject order ('At no time do I think...').

### Key Idioms and Phrasal Verbs

- To bring up: To introduce a subject into conversation. ("I'm glad you brought that up.")
- To beat yourself up: To blame or criticize yourself unnecessarily. ("Don't beat yourself up over failing the exam.")
- Funny: Can mean humorous, but often means strange, unusual, or slightly sick. ("My stomach feels funny." / "That green drink looks funny.")
- To set the tone for: To establish an attitude or mood for how things will continue. ("Eating protein sets the tone for the rest of your day.")
- To equate [A] with [B]: To consider two things to be the same. ("People equate happiness with money.")
- In one sitting: Without stopping until you finish. ("She read the entire book in one sitting.")
- A rush: A sudden feeling of intense energy. ("A sugar rush" or "A caffeine rush").
- To get something in: To find time to do something in a busy schedule. ("I try to get a workout in during my lunch break.")
- To drop off: To decrease or decline. ("My motivation to exercise dropped off after a few weeks.")
- To reap the benefits: To collect the positive results of your hard work.
- To get over the hump: To survive the most difficult part of a process. ("The first 10 days of a diet are hard, but then you get over the hump.")
- A craving: An intense desire for a specific food. ("I have a sugar craving.")

### Advanced Grammar: Inverted Word Order

Native speakers sometimes invert the subject and the verb for special, dramatic emphasis, particularly after negative adverbs (like never, seldom, rarely, not only, at no time).

Standard vs. Inverted: - Standard: I think you would sit down and eat eight oranges at no time. - Inverted: At no time do I think you would sit down and eat eight oranges. - Standard: I have never seen such a beautiful thing. - Inverted: Never have I seen such a beautiful thing. - Standard: She not only speaks Spanish, but also Italian. - Inverted: Not only does she speak Spanish, but also Italian.

## Vocabulary and Idioms for Age

### Summary

Age-related idioms include 'wet behind the ears' (immature or inexperienced), 'over the hill' (past one's prime), and 'May-December romance' (partnership between people with a significant age difference). When using age as a hyphenated prenominal adjective, 'year' remains singular (e.g., 'a six-year-old child'), while decade descriptions use possessive phrasing ('in her late 40s').

### Age Idioms

- Wet behind the ears OR Green: Young, naive, and lacking life experience.
- Over the hill: Past your prime; your best, most capable years are behind you.
- From the cradle to the grave: From birth to death; your entire life.
- Robbing the cradle: Being romantically involved with someone much younger than you.
- A senior moment: A temporary moment of confusion or forgetfulness.
- Act your age: A command telling someone to stop behaving immaturely.
- Underage: Too young to legally do something (like drink or vote).
- Of age / Come of age: To reach the legal age of adulthood.
- A May-December romance: A relationship between a young person (Spring/May) and an older person (Winter/December).

### Grammar: Discussing Age

Decades: Use in + possessive pronoun + decade + s. - She is in her 20s. (Between 20 and 29). - They are in their 50s. - Modifiers: Use early, mid, and late to be specific. (e.g., "He is in his late 40s").

Adjectives: When describing someone's age using a hyphenated adjective before a noun, do NOT use a plural "s" on "year". - Incorrect: I have a six-years-old son. - Correct: I have a six-year-old son.

### Stages of Life

- Newborn: 1 to 2 weeks old.
- Infant: 1 month to 1 year old.
- Toddler: 1 to 3 years old (learning to "toddle" or walk awkwardly).
- Preschooler: 3 to 5 years old.
- Kindergartner: 5 to 6 years old.
- Tween: 9 to 12 years old (pre-teen).
- Teenager: 13 to 19 years old (ends in "teen").
- Young Adult: 18 to 25 years old.
- Middle-aged: Roughly 35 to 60.
- Senior / Elderly: 60+. (Also called a senior citizen).

## 12 Advanced English Verbs

### Summary

Sophisticated English verbs include 'abate' (diminish in intensity), 'belie' (give a false impression of), 'beseech' (urgently entreat), 'coax' (patiently persuade), and 'cower' (recoil in fear). Public and competitive actions utilize 'decry' (publicly condemn), 'galvanize' (provoke into action), 'garner' (gather praise or support), 'mar' (impair quality), and 'vie' (compete fiercely).

### The Vocabulary List

- Abate: To become less intense or widespread; to decrease.
- Example: "The storm finally began to abate."
- Belie: To give a false impression of something; to contradict.
- Example: "Her calm smile belied the extreme nervousness she felt inside."
- Beseech: To urgently and anxiously ask someone to do something; to beg.
- Example: "I beseech you to reconsider before it's too late."
- Coax: To persuade someone to do something gently and patiently.
- Example: "I managed to coax the frightened cat out of the tree."
- Cower: To crouch down or bend low in fear.
- Example: "The dog cowered in the corner during the thunderstorm."
- Decry: To publicly criticize or express strong disapproval of something.
- Example: "The public decried the government's decision to raise taxes."
- Flinch: To make a quick, nervous movement as a reaction to pain, fear, or surprise.
- Example: "He didn't even flinch when the doctor gave him the shot."
- Galvanize: To shock or excite someone into taking action.
- Example: "The coach's speech galvanized the team to win the game."
- Garner: To collect or gather (especially information, support, or praise).
- Example: "The new movie garnered excellent reviews from critics."
- Mar: To damage or spoil the appearance or quality of something.
- Example: "The beautiful view was marred by a giant construction site."
- Muster: To gather enough courage, energy, or support (often used with "up").
- Example: "He finally mustered up the courage to ask her on a date."
- Vie: To compete fiercely with someone to achieve something. (Present participle: vying).
- Example: "Several highly qualified candidates are vying for the manager position."

## 12 Advanced Descriptive Words

### Summary

Advanced descriptive terms denote challenging conditions ('bleak' for desolate, 'daunting' for intimidating) and pervasive presence ('ubiquitous'). Character and ethical evaluations are articulated using 'egregious' (flagrantly bad), 'sanctimonious' (hypocritically self-righteous), 'callous' (unfeeling), 'adamant' (unyielding), and 'distraught' (deeply agitated).

### The Vocabulary List

- Bleak: Depressing, desolate, and not hopeful.
- Example: "After the layoffs, the company's future looked bleak."
- Daunting: Intimidating, overwhelming, and seemingly difficult to deal with.
- Example: "Starting a whole new career at 50 is a daunting task."
- Undaunted: Not discouraged or intimidated by difficulty or danger.
- Example: "Despite the high risks, she remained undaunted."
- Unwitting(ly): Not aware of the full facts; done unintentionally or by mistake.
- Example: "She unwittingly threw away the winning lottery ticket."
- Egregious (ih-gree-jus): Outstandingly bad; shocking.
- Example: "His egregious disregard for safety rules put everyone at risk."
- Sanctimonious: Behaving as if you are morally superior to everyone else (often in an annoying way).
- Example: "I am tired of his sanctimonious lectures about my diet."
- Hapless: Extremely unlucky and deserving of pity.
- Example: "The hapless traveler lost his passport on the first day of his trip."
- Trite: Overused, lacking originality, and boring (a cliche).
- Example: "I know it sounds trite, but health really is the most important thing."
- Ubiquitous (yoo-bik-wih-tus): Present, appearing, or found everywhere.
- Example: "Smartphones have become completely ubiquitous in modern society."
- Adamant: Refusing to be persuaded or to change your mind.
- Example: "He was adamant about not attending the party."
- Callous: Emotionally insensitive and cruel; showing no concern for others' suffering.
- Example: "His callous remarks hurt her feelings deeply."
- Distraught: Extremely worried, nervous, or upset.
- Example: "She was incredibly distraught after losing her dog."

## Understanding Jokes and Wordplay

### Summary

English humor relies heavily on homophones and idiomatic double meanings, exemplified by 'guts' (viscera vs. courage), days being 'numbered' (calendar numbers vs. impending end), and 'outstanding in his field' (pasture vs. professional excellence). Conversational terminology around humor includes 'getting' a joke (comprehending), having it 'go over one's head' (missing the point), delivering the 'punchline', and telling predictable 'corny' or 'dad jokes'.

### Vocabulary for Jokes

- To get it: To understand a joke. ("I don't get it.")
- To go over my head: To completely miss the humor or meaning. ("Everyone laughed, but it went over my head.")
- The punchline: The final sentence or phrase that makes the joke funny.
- To butcher a joke: To ruin a joke by telling it badly or forgetting the details.
- To crack up: To laugh very hard. ("That movie cracked me up.")
- Corny: Silly, old-fashioned, or predictable. ("A corny joke.")
- A dad joke: A clean, corny joke that relies on obvious wordplay. Fathers famously tell them.

### Jokes with Double Meanings (Idioms)

- Why don't skeletons fight each other? Because they don't have the guts.
- Guts: The inside of a stomach (which skeletons don't have).
- Idiom: To have the courage to do something.
- Why was the calendar nervous? Because its days were numbered.
- Literal: A calendar has numbers on it.
- Idiom: If your days are numbered, something bad is going to happen to you soon (you will be fired, die, etc.).
- Why do cows wear bells? Because their horns don't work.
- Horns: Animal horns vs. car horns.
- Why did the scarecrow win an award? Because he was outstanding in his field.
- Outstanding in his field: Being excellent at a specific profession.
- Literal: A scarecrow is physically standing outside in a farm field.
- Why are frogs so happy? Because they eat whatever bugs them.
- Bugs: Insects (which frogs eat).
- Idiom: To "bug" someone means to annoy or bother them.
- Why did the cookie go to the doctor? Because it felt crummy.
- Crummy: Made of crumbs/falling apart.
- Idiom: To feel "crummy" is a casual way to say you feel sick or unwell.
- What did the grape say when stepped on? Nothing. It just let out a little wine.
- Wine / Whine: Homophones. Wine is the drink. To "whine" means to complain in an annoying, high-pitched voice.
- I stayed up all night wondering where the sun went. Then it dawned on me.
- Dawn: Sunrise.
- Idiom: If something "dawns on" you, you suddenly realize or understand it.
- Did you hear about the restaurant on the moon? Great food, but no atmosphere.
- Atmosphere: Oxygen/air around a planet.
- Atmosphere: The mood, ambiance, or design of a restaurant.

## 4 Tips for a Better Accent

### Summary

Professional accent clarity requires mastering the stress patterns of everyday industry-specific terms, intentionally decelerating pacing to articulate difficult consonant transitions, and prioritizing content words over function words. Communicators should prioritize operational intelligibility and vocal confidence over chasing accent perfection.

### Tip 1: Master Key Workplace Words

Make a list of the 30-50 words you use every day in your profession (medical terms, engineering terms, company names). Make sure you know exactly which syllable to stress. - Example: Develop (de-VEL-op, not DE-vel-op). - Example: Refill (RE-fill, not re-FILL).

### Tip 2: Slow Down

Many non-native speakers speak too fast because they think it makes them sound more fluent. - Fast speech often results in missing or muffled consonant sounds. - It takes physical time to move your tongue and lips between complex sounds (e.g., from the "v" to the "m" in five minutes). - Professional speech is clear speech. Slowing down makes you sound more authoritative.

### Tip 3: Stress Content Words, Not Function Words

Do not apply strong stress to pronouns (I, me, you) or prepositions (to, for, at). Stress the verbs and nouns (the content words) because they carry the meaning. - Incorrect: I agree with YOU. (This implies no one else agrees). - Correct: I AGREE with you.

### Tip 4: Change Your Mindset

Do not be paralyzed by a fear of being judged for your accent. - You are speaking because you are a hired expert in your field; you deserve to be there. - Most people do not care about your accent as long as you are speaking clearly. - Ignore negative comments from insecure or jealous individuals. Speak with a strong, confident voice.

## Listening for the Letter "O"

### Summary

In unstressed syllables, the letter 'O' typically reduces to a schwa (/ə/), as heard in the terminal syllable of 'doctor' (/ˈdɑːktər/) and the initial syllable of 'control' (/kənˈtroʊl/). When stressed, 'O' realizes three distinct phonemes: /ɑː/ ('doctor'), /oʊ/ ('control'), or /ʌ/ ('color').

### The Unstressed "O" (The Schwa)

When the letter "O" is in an unstressed syllable, it usually reduces to a relaxed "uh" sound (the schwa). - doctor (doc-tuhr) - control (cuhn-trol)

### The Stressed "O": 3 Possibilities

When an "O" is in the stressed syllable, it can make one of three sounds: 1. The "AH" Sound: Doctor (dahk-ter). Open your mouth wide. 2. The "OH" Sound: Control (cun-trohl). 3. The "UH" Sound: Color (cuh-ler).

[!TIP] Do not rely strictly on spelling! The only way to know the correct pronunciation is to listen to native speakers or check a phonetic dictionary.

## Powerful Vocabulary Building Strategies

### Summary

Broad vocabulary development relies on reading narrative fiction for contextual richness, immediately incorporating new words into spoken personal sentences, and reading across varied non-specialized disciplines. Physical paper dictionaries reinforce memory via marginal annotations, root browsing, and pencil-dot tracking of recurrently forgotten terms.

### 4 Tips for Vocabulary Acquisition

#### 1. Read a Novel in English

Do not just read textbooks. Find a fiction novel that truly excites you. Because you want to know what happens in the story, you will be highly motivated to keep reading, even when it is difficult.

#### 2. Use a Physical, Paper Dictionary

Using an online dictionary is fast, but it is often a passive experience. Using an "old school" paper dictionary provides several benefits: - You can physically underline words when you learn them. - When you leaf through the pages later, you see all the words you underlined a year ago, which is highly motivating. - You see related root words directly above and below the word you are searching for. - The Dot Method: Put a pencil dot next to a word. If you look it up again and see a dot, you know you forgot it. After two or three dots, write it in a dedicated notebook.

#### 3. Immediately Make a Sentence

When you look up a new word, do not just read the definition and close the book. Say the word out loud and immediately use it in an original sentence related to your own life.

#### 4. Read a Wide Variety of Subjects

If you only read business books, you will struggle to talk about politics, art, or everyday events. Read articles on diverse topics so you can converse with anyone about anything.

## Does Perfect Grammar Matter?

### Summary

Grammatical precision is essential in academic writing and career advancement where structural flaws impair credibility, though communicative effectiveness suffices in casual social interactions. Common errors include adjective-adverb confusion ('spoke good' versus 'spoke well'), improper pronoun cases ('me and her went'), and homophone misspellings like 'should of' instead of 'should have'.

### When Grammar Matters

- Academic Settings: Universities require excellent grammar for essays, research papers, and grades.
- Professional Settings: Studies show that professionals with strong grammar get promoted more frequently. Employers trust employees with good grammar to handle client emails and reports.

### When Grammar Doesn't Matter (As Much)

If you are traveling as a tourist, ordering in a restaurant, or casually chatting with friends online, perfect grammar is unnecessary. The only goal is basic communication.

[!NOTE] Do not let a fear of making mistakes paralyze you or stop you from speaking. However, you should still strive for excellence. Do not settle for "good enough" if your goal is true professional fluency.

### Mistakes Native Speakers Make

Native speakers do make grammar mistakes, but they are usually different from the mistakes ESL learners make. Native speakers often confuse homophones or use overly casual structures.

- Adjectives vs. Adverbs:
- Incorrect: He speaks English good. Please drive careful.
- Correct: He speaks English well. Please drive carefully.
- Pronoun Subject vs. Object:
- Incorrect: Me and her went to the store. / He asked my friend and I to help.
- Correct: She and I went to the store. / He asked my friend and me to help.
- Past Simple vs. Past Participle:
- Incorrect: I have never swam there. / He could have forgot.
- Correct: I have never swum there. / He could have forgotten.
- Possessive Apostrophes:
- My parents' house (Apostrophe after the 's' for plurals).
- My mother's house (Apostrophe before the 's' for singulars).
- Homophones (Spelling Mistakes):
- Writing should of instead of should have.
- Confusing they're, their, and there.

## Real Conversations: 10 Idioms in LA

### Summary

Conversational idioms include 'debut' (silent 't', pronounced /deɪˈbjuː/), 'out of this world' (extraordinary), 'check out' (investigate), and 'stick with me' (remain vividly remembered). Social terminology distinguishes being a 'snob' (discerning enthusiast in coffee or beer) and describes community cultural hubs as a 'scene' or an eclectic 'mishmash'.

### Vocabulary & Idioms

- To debut (day-byoo): To introduce something to the public for the first time. The "T" is silent. ("The restaurant is debuting its new menu tonight.")
- Out of this world: Absolutely amazing or fantastic. ("The sushi was out of this world.")
- To check [something] out: To investigate or examine something you are interested in. ("I need to go check out that new coffee shop.")
- I got to [verb]: I had the opportunity/chance to do something. ("I got to try some amazing local brews.") Note: Do not confuse with "I've got to", which means "I must."
- A [noun] snob: Someone who likes high-quality things and knows a lot about a specific subject (e.g., a beer snob, a coffee snob, a music snob).
- To stick with me: To stay in my memory; not easy to forget. ("That incredible meal will stick with me for a while.")
- The [noun] scene: The current events and culture of a specific community in a city. ("The LA food scene is very diverse.")
- Background: Your past, history, or childhood. ("Tell me about your professional background.")
- Upbringing: How your parents raised you; how you grew up. ("His strong work ethic is a result of his upbringing.")
- A mishmash: A confused or unique mixture of different things.

## 13 Professional English Expressions

### Summary

Professional workplace expressions include working 'in the field' (specialized domain), 'getting into' an industry, having interviews 'lined up' (scheduled), and not 'backing out' on agreements. Professional development is described through a specific 'skill set', managing 'time-sensitive' projects while 'on deadline', being 'strong at' a task, and 'moving one's way up'.

### Expressions for the Workplace

- In the field: Working in a specific profession. ("I have been in the PR field for 8 years.")
- To get into it: To start or enter a profession. ("I got into PR by accident.")
- To line up: To arrange, plan, or schedule something. ("I have three job interviews lined up for next week.")
- To back out (on someone): To cancel a promise or agreement. ("The company backed out of the job offer at the last minute.")
- Skill set: The specific combination of skills needed for a job. ("He doesn't have the right skill set for this position.")
- To be on deadline: To have to finish a task by a strict time limit. ("Journalists are always working on deadline.")
- To be strong AT [something]: To be very good at something. Note: Do not say "strong IN" or "good IN".
- To move my way up: To get promoted; to climb the corporate ladder. ("I started as an intern and moved my way up.")
- Time-sensitive: Urgent; must be done by a specific deadline. ("These documents are time-sensitive and must be signed today.")
- Supply and demand: The economic relationship between the availability of a product/job and the desire for it. ("The supply of writers is overwhelming the demand.")
- X, Y, or Z: Used when giving a list of hypothetical examples. ("Our product is better because of X, Y, or Z.")
- In a [adjective] fashion: In a particular manner or way. ("Please handle this in a professional fashion.")

## 14 Idioms for Overcoming Difficulty

### Summary

Expressions of resilience contrast 'pull together' (unite as a collective) with 'pull yourself together' (regain emotional control), alongside 'weathering the storm' through hardship. Navigating adversity involves maintaining a constructive 'mindset', resolving to 'stick it out' to completion, and recognizing that there is 'no use' in unproductive worrying.

### Idioms of Resilience and Positivity

- To pull together: To work hard as a group to achieve something. ("We need to pull together to fix the economy.")
- Alternative: Pull yourself together (Calm down emotionally).
- Moving forward: Looking toward the future; progressing. ("We need to be careful moving forward.")
- Mindset: Attitude; how you see the world. ("You need to keep a positive mindset.")
- There's no use in [verb+ing]: It is not helpful; it's useless. ("There's no use in panicking.")
- To pick up [a hobby]: To start learning a new skill or hobby. ("I just picked up painting.")
- A bunch of: A lot of. ("I bought a bunch of paint and canvases.")
- To lighten the mood: To make the atmosphere less serious and more happy. ("I told a joke to lighten the mood.")
- Day-to-day life: Your normal, everyday routine.
- For the time being: Temporarily; for now. ("The gym is closed for the time being.")
- To fall out of shape: To lose your physical fitness because you stopped exercising.
- To weather the storm: To survive and successfully deal with a major crisis. ("It's a bad recession, but we will weather the storm.")
- No matter what: Unconditionally; regardless of what happens. ("I will achieve my goal no matter what.")
- To stick it out: To endure a difficult situation all the way to the end without giving up. ("This class is hard, but I will stick it out.")

## 15 Idioms with the Word "Under"

### Summary

Idioms with 'under' describe mild illness ('under the weather'), intense scrutiny ('under the microscope'), illicit cash payments ('under the table'), and unnoticed actions ('under the radar'). Conflict and pressure are captured by 'getting under someone's skin' (irritating), 'throwing someone under the bus' (betraying an ally), working 'under the gun' (deadline stress), and regarding past disputes as 'water under the bridge'.

### The "Under" Idioms

- Under the weather: Feeling a little bit sick.
- Under the microscope: Being watched or judged very carefully by others.
- To get under one's skin: To deeply annoy or irritate someone. ("Her complaining gets under my skin.")
- Under the table: Getting paid in secret cash to avoid taxes (illegal).
- Under the radar: Doing something secretly so nobody notices.
- To come under fire: To be severely criticized or attacked for a decision.
- To throw [someone] under the bus: To betray a friend or colleague for your own personal benefit.
- Under one's breath: Speaking very softly (often complaining) so no one else can hear.
- Right under my nose: Directly in front of you; extremely obvious. ("He stole the money right under my nose.")
- Under the gun: Feeling intense pressure to meet a strict deadline.
- Water under the bridge: A past conflict or problem that is now forgiven and forgotten. ("We argued years ago, but it's water under the bridge.")
- To sweep it under the rug: To hide a problem and pretend it doesn't exist instead of fixing it.
- To go under the knife: To have surgery.
- Under the influence: Drunk; intoxicated (often related to driving).
- Under pressure: Experiencing intense stress.

## 16 Casual Idioms in Context

### Summary

Colloquial estimation and context rely on 'give or take' (approximately), 'given the fact that' (considering), and applying an experiential 'rule of thumb'. Interpersonal interactions emphasize maintaining a 'cool head' in crises, establishing 'common ground' across differences, and describing remarkable events as 'unreal'.

### Vocabulary and Expressions

- Give or take: Plus or minus; approximately. ("It's a one-hour drive, give or take a few minutes.")
- Given the fact that: Considering; because of. ("The hotel will be expensive given the fact that it's in New York.")
- How do you deal with it?: How do you handle this stressful situation? What is your solution?
- The wait (noun): The amount of time you must wait for a table. ("How long is the wait?")
- To keep a calm/cool head: To stay calm in a stressful emergency.
- A rule of thumb: A general, unwritten rule that most people follow. ("As a rule of thumb, leave a 20% tip at a restaurant.")
- To hit the mark: To achieve a goal accurately.
- To make a living: To earn enough money to pay for your basic needs (rent, food). ("It's hard to make a living on minimum wage.")
- A people person: Someone who is highly social, friendly, and loves talking to others.
- A big night out: A special evening spent outside the house to celebrate something.
- To find common ground: To find shared interests or similarities with someone you might otherwise disagree with.
- You name it: Everything is included; anything you can think of. ("We sell clothes, electronics, furniture-you name it.")
- Oh my gosh / Oh my goodness: A polite, clean alternative to saying "Oh my God."
- There's no place like home: An expression meaning your home is the best, most comfortable place in the world.
- It's unreal: It is amazing, fantastic, or unbelievable. ("The weather in LA is unreal.")
- To make it: To achieve a massive, difficult dream (like becoming a famous actor). ("He moved to Hollywood to try to make it.")

## Idioms for Health and Modern Lifestyle

### Summary

Lifestyle and health discussions feature phrases such as working 'for hours on end' (uninterrupted duration), combating a 'sedentary' routine, and exercising to 'offset' physical inactivity. Problem-solving and negotiation utilize 'wiggle room' (negotiation leeway), enduring 'relentless' strain, and managing a 'perfect storm' of coinciding difficulties.

### Vocabulary & Idioms

- To address: To deal with an issue or give it attention. ("The boss addressed the problem at the meeting.")
- From head to toe: The entire body. ("The child was covered in mud from head to toe.")
- For hours on end: For a long time continuously, without stopping. ("He plays video games for hours on end.")
- Relentless: Persistent, constant, harsh (doesn't stop). ("The relentless noise kept me awake.")
- Worst-case scenario: The absolute worst or most serious thing that could possibly happen in a situation.
- Sedentary: Inactive; sitting a lot. ("A sedentary job.")
- To offset: To compensate for something; to restore balance. ("He exercises to offset his sedentary job.")
- Couldn't be more truthful: It is completely true. (Opposite: Nothing could be further from the truth - It is completely false).
- Wiggle room: Extra space to negotiate, change your mind, or have options. ("Is there any wiggle room on the price?")
- A flight of stairs: One continuous set of stairs between floors.
- To crawl around: To walk on your hands and knees like a baby.
- An aisle seat: The seat next to the walkway on an airplane. Note: The 'S' is silent (eye-ull).
- You kind of want to [verb]: A casual way of giving advice; "I suggest you do this." ("You kind of want to listen to your lawyer.")
- To hang out: To spend time casually.
- To begin with: First of all; my first point. ("I don't want to go because it's too cold to begin with.")
- The perfect storm: A rare combination of several negative events that creates a uniquely terrible result.

## 20 Slang Words and Idioms

### Summary

Conversational praise uses idioms like 'out of this world' and 'to die for' (superlative quality), 'put to shame' and 'blow out of the water' (decisively outperforming competitors), and having a spicy 'kick to it'. Idiomatic discourse features 'I gotta say' (emphatic assertion), growing 'by leaps and bounds' (rapid advancement), and reporting speech casually with 'they go'.

### Conversational Expressions

- I gotta say: I must say; used to emphasize a point. ("I gotta say, that test was hard.")
- Out of this world: Amazing; fantastic.
- Got to [verb]: Had the opportunity to. ("I got to try Thai food.")
- As opposed to: Compared to; instead of. ("You should study as opposed to playing video games.")
- To move on: To move forward; to change the topic. ("Let's move on to the next question.")
- To put [something] to shame: To completely outperform or be much better than something else. ("Your cooking puts mine to shame.")
- You can't go wrong: It is always a good, safe choice. ("You can't go wrong with the black dress.")
- To pop up: To appear suddenly and unexpectedly. ("New stores are popping up everywhere.")
- By leaps and bounds: Very quickly (usually referring to growth or improvement). ("Her English is improving by leaps and bounds.")
- Born and raised: A phrase indicating your true home and identity. ("I'm a California girl, born and raised.")
- I couldn't take it: I could not tolerate it; it was too much. ("The food was too spicy; I couldn't take it.")
- To die for: Extremely good. ("That dessert is to die for.")
- Phenomenal: Extraordinary; unusually great.
- To have a kick to it: To taste surprisingly spicy. ("This chicken has a kick to it.")
- A signature dish: The most famous or popular food item a restaurant makes.
- To blow [someone] out of the water: To easily destroy the competition. ("We won 9 to 1; we blew them out of the water.")
- Hors d'oeuvres (or-dervs): Small appetizer snacks (French origin).
- The place to be: A very popular, trendy location.
- Here and there: In various places; occasionally. ("You make some mistakes here and there.")
- They go: A highly casual slang term meaning "They said." ("I go 'no', and he goes 'yes'.")

## 21 Advanced Words and Idioms

### Summary

Acting and career discourse employs expressions such as dwindle (shrink over time), typecast (repeat-role stereotyping), book (secure a role), wear different hats, and thrown to the wolves. Crucial pronunciations include the silent 'b' in subtle (suh-tul), the short 'uh' in Southerner (suh-ther-ner vs. 'ow' in south), and versatile (ver-suh-tul).

### Vocabulary and Expressions

- To dwindle: To decrease, shrink, or become smaller over time. ("My bank account is dwindling.")
- To typecast: To always cast an actor in the exact same type of role. ("He is always typecast as the villain.")
- Subtle (suh-tul): Barely noticeable; soft. The 'B' is silent.
- To stand out: To be very noticeable.
- To get stuck: To be trapped in one place without changing. ("I got stuck in traffic.")
- To book (a job): To be hired for an acting role. (Also means to reserve a hotel/flight).
- Southerner (suh-ther-ner): A person from the southern region of the US. Note: "South" has an "ow" sound, but "Southern" has an "uh" sound.
- To wear/put on different hats: To have a variety of different roles or responsibilities at work.
- Versatile (ver-suh-tul): Adaptable; having many different uses.
- High-strung: Easily excited, angry, or stressed. ("That dog is very high-strung.")
- To get out of your way: To move aside so you can pass.
- Thrown to the wolves: Abandoned in a highly difficult, competitive, or dangerous situation.
- Mean: Unkind, cruel.
- To go about your business: To do your normal routine without paying attention to others.
- To take [something/someone] on: To do a challenging task, or to fight someone.
- Laid-back: Relaxed, casual, not easily stressed.
- Chill: Calm, not highly emotional.
- Vibe: The mood, feeling, or atmosphere of a place. ("LA has a laid-back vibe.")
- All year round: Continually throughout the entire year.

## Advanced Business Vocabulary (NYT)

### Summary

Economic reporting uses business idioms like turnover rate, lose its sizzle, and sweeten the deal, alongside market verbs such as abate, freeze hiring, and drive up. Descriptive phrases like frenzied, steep competition, through the roof, and the tables have turned capture labor dynamics and shifting corporate leverage.

### Advanced Vocabulary

- Turnover (rate): The rate at which employees leave a company (quit or fired) and are replaced. ("A high turnover rate is expensive for companies.")
- To lose its sizzle: To lose its excitement or heat. ("The hot job market is losing its sizzle.")
- Frenzied: Wild, uncontrolled, highly excited. ("The children were in a frenzy.")
- Wage: Salary; how much money you make at work.
- To abate: To reduce, lessen, or become less intense. ("The storm finally abated.")
- Billboard: A massive outdoor sign used for advertising.
- Steep: Having a sharp angle (a steep hill), OR being unreasonably expensive (a steep price). "Steep competition" means intense competition.
- To drive up: To cause something to increase rapidly. ("Inflation is driving up gas prices.")
- To sweeten the deal: To offer extra money or perks to make a business transaction more attractive to the other party.
- Through the roof: Rising to a very high, unexpected level (usually prices or demand). ("Sales went through the roof.")
- To die down: To gradually become less strong or noisy. ("The party died down around 2 AM.")
- To freeze [prices/hiring]: To hold something at a fixed level; stopping all increases. ("The company froze hiring.")
- Altogether: Completely; totally. ("They stopped hiring altogether.")
- The tables have turned: The situation has completely reversed (someone weak is now strong).

## Idioms in the News: Fast Food Wars

### Summary

Journalistic coverage uses idioms such as hit the road, on the run, par for the course, follow suit, and trumpet, along with nouns like woes and casual terms like burger joint. When monetary values modify a noun, the singular form is required (e.g., a 5-dollar meal, a 2-million-dollar house vs. costs 5 dollars).

### Vocabulary & Expressions

- To hit the road: To leave or start a journey (usually driving). ("We need to hit the road before it gets dark.")
- On the run: Constantly moving; very busy. ("I'm always on the run, so I eat lunch in my car.")
- Par for the course: Typical; exactly what you would normally expect to happen (comes from golf terminology). ("Rejection is par for the course when applying for jobs.")
- A joint: A highly casual restaurant or bar. ("Let's eat at the new burger joint.")
- In the midst of: In the middle of; during. ("I am in the midst of a major project.")
- To lure: To attract, tempt, or persuade someone to do something. ("The store lured customers with huge discounts.")
- Woe(s): Deep trouble, problems, or sorrow. ("The company is facing financial woes.")
- Month-long / Year-long: Lasting for the entirety of that time period. ("A month-long sale.")
- To follow suit: To copy someone; to do the exact same thing someone else just did. ("McDonald's lowered prices, and Wendy's followed suit.")
- To trumpet: To announce something loudly, forcefully, and proudly. ("The government trumpeted the success of the new policy.")

### Grammar Note: Pluralizing Money

When the dollar amount modifies a noun (acts as an adjective), you remove the "S" from dollars. - Correct: It is a 5-dollar meal. (Modifies "meal") - Correct: The meal costs 5 dollars. - Correct: It is a 2-million-dollar house. - Correct: The house costs 2 million dollars.

## Professional English & Design Vocabulary

### Summary

Corporate design terminology includes on staff, up front, way over budget, and value engineering (VE) for cost-cutting, paired with idioms like behind the scenes, start from scratch, and a dagger to the heart. In family lineage, generation increments use great- rather than 'grand-grand' (e.g., great-grandmother, great-uncle).

### Professional Expressions

- On staff: Employed by a company. ("We have 10 architects on staff.")
- ASAP: As soon as possible.
- To have [something] in mind: To have a specific plan or idea. ("Do you have a budget in mind for this project?")
- Up front: Paid or communicated in advance. ("They didn't tell us the budget up front.")
- To go over budget: To spend more money than was originally planned.
- Way over budget: Using "way" instead of "very" or "too much" (e.g., "way too hot", "way different").
- Value engineering (VE): A business term for cutting costs or doing something cheaper.
- A way to put it: A specific manner of expressing or saying something. ("That's a nice way to put it.")
- A dagger to the heart: Something that hurts you deeply and emotionally.
- A misconception: An incorrect view or belief based on misunderstanding.
- Behind the scenes: What happens in private, away from public view.
- Blood, sweat, and tears: Extremely hard work requiring immense effort and suffering.
- You would think that: It is logical to expect it, but it's not actually true. ("He's an adult; you would think he'd make better choices.")
- To appreciate: To recognize the value or beauty of something. ("I appreciate modern art.")
- A fixer-upper: A house that is cheaper but needs a lot of repairs. (Opposite: Move-in ready).
- It doesn't speak to me: I don't feel a personal connection to it; it is not my style.
- Might as well: The most logical, practical thing to do in a situation.
- To start from scratch: To start from the very beginning, creating something entirely new.
- To get into the field: To choose and enter a specific profession.
- Vividly: Clearly; producing a strong, powerful image in your mind. ("I vividly remember my first day of school.")

### Grammar Note: Family Tree

Do not say "Grand grand mother." - Grandmother -> Great-grandmother -> Great-great-grandmother. - The brother of your grandmother is your Great uncle.

## Descriptive News Vocabulary: Wildfires

### Summary

Disaster journalism utilizes precise descriptive vocabulary: unprecedented, swath (swawth), render, billow, wreckage, incinerate, and the contrast between quaint or lush settings and grim aftermaths. Many English verbs of transformation are formed systematically by adding the suffix -en to adjectives, such as flatten, shorten, soften, and widen.

### Vocabulary of Nature and Destruction

- Unprecedented: Completely new; it has never happened before. ("The scale of destruction was unprecedented.")
- To flatten: To make completely flat or level; to destroy a building so it collapses.
- A swath (swawth): A large strip, section, or area of land. ("The fire destroyed a huge swath of the forest.")
- To render: To cause something to become a certain condition. ("The flood rendered the roads unusable.")
- Quaint: Charming, old-fashioned, or unusual in a highly pleasant way. ("A quaint little village.")
- Whimsy (noun) / Whimsical (adj): Playful, imaginative, and fun.
- Lush: Full of healthy, abundant green plants. ("A lush tropical garden.")
- To billow: To move in large waves (used for smoke, curtains, or sails). ("White smoke billowed from the wreckage.")
- Wreckage: The broken, ruined parts of something that has been destroyed. ("The wreckage of the car.")
- Grim: Highly serious, depressing, unpleasant, and showing no hope. ("The doctor gave a grim prognosis.")
- Prized: Highly valued and important. ("My most prized possession.")
- To back up onto [something]: When the backside of a house or building directly faces another area. ("The house backs up onto the beach.")
- To incinerate: To burn something completely until it turns to ash.

### Creating Verbs from Adjectives

Many verbs are created by adding "-en" to an adjective: - Flat -> Flatten - Short -> Shorten - Soft -> Soften - Sharp -> Sharpen - Wide -> Widen - Hard -> Harden - Bright -> Brighten

## Accent Reduction Coaching

### Summary

Effective professional accent reduction prioritizes immediate phonetic clarity over total accent erasure. Ensuring listeners grasp the message without distraction prevents pronunciation from overshadowing professional expertise.

### Key Concepts

- Clarity over Perfection: The goal of accent reduction is not necessarily to erase your identity, but to ensure you are easily understood the first time you speak.
- Focus on the Message: When you speak clearly, listeners focus on the content of your message rather than the way you are pronouncing the words.

## Public Speaking with a Foreign Accent

### Summary

Media presentation requires authoritative, conversational delivery over rigid, word-for-word memorization that sounds robotic. Key vocabulary includes succinct (suk-sinkt), come off as, get your point across, and idioms like a sports nut or the informal collective noun folks.

### Public Speaking Advice (Ross's Tips)

- Do not sound rehearsed: If you memorize a speech "word for word," you will sound like a robot. People might think you are "covering something up" (hiding the truth).
- Be an authority: You were hired because you are an expert. Focus on your expertise, not your accent.
- Be relatable: People want to work with someone they feel comfortable with. If you speak naturally, you "come off as" a regular person.

### Vocabulary & Expressions

- Folks: An informal word for people. Note: "My folks" means "My parents".
- A [noun] nut: An obsessed, highly enthusiastic fan of something. ("He's a sports nut," or "She's a health nut.")
- One (pronoun): A formal way of saying "someone." ("How does one get a job here?")
- To get your point across: To successfully communicate your message so people understand you.
- Succinct (suk-sinkt): Clearly expressed in very few words; brief and direct.
- Work ethic: A strong belief that hard work is important and morally good. ("He has a great work ethic.")
- To come off as: To appear, seem, or give a certain impression. ("He came off as arrogant.")
- An adage (ad-ij): A wise saying, proverb, or accepted truth. ("There is an old adage...")

## Idioms and the Word "Going On"

### Summary

The phrase 'going on' has three distinct functions: occurring (so much going on), approaching a milestone (going on four years), and casual greeting (what's going on?). Spoken English frequently features colloquial constructs like singular there's preceding plural nouns alongside idioms such as you can't beat it and the difficult pronunciation rural (rur-ull).

### Vocabulary & Idioms

- You can't beat it: It is the absolute best; nothing is better. ("You can't beat LA weather.")
- Teeny-tiny: Very, very small.
- Rural (rur-ull): In the countryside; away from the city. Note: This is notoriously difficult to pronounce. The L is very soft.
- To pass along: To give or transmit from one person to another. ("My mom passed along her love of horses to me.")
- Practically: Almost; virtually. ("I've watched practically every video.")
- Driven: Highly hard-working, ambitious, and motivated.
- A side project: A secondary job or creative project done in addition to your main job.
- Pace of life: The speed at which daily life happens. ("The pace of life is slower in the country.")
- To get away with it: To do something wrong, risky, or bad, but not get caught or punished for it.

### The 3 Meanings of "Going On"

- Happening: "There's so much going on in this city."
- Approaching a time/age: "I've lived here going on four years." (Almost 4 years). "He's going on 12."
- How are you?: "What's going on?" (Casual greeting. Answer: "Not much.")

### Grammar Note: "There's" for Plurals

In highly casual spoken English, native speakers often use "There's" (There is) with plural nouns, even though it is technically grammatically incorrect. - Strict Grammar: "There are interesting people to meet." - Casual Speech: "There's interesting people to meet."

## 20 Modern Slang Words

### Summary

Modern social vernacular uses slang like spill the tea (share gossip), slide into the DMs, no cap (no lie), sus (suspicious), cringe, fire, and stan (obsessive fan). Nuanced social stances are expressed through phrases like you do you, I feel you, low-key (secretly) versus high-key (openly), and keep it 100.

### Slang & Expressions

- Tea / To spill the tea: Gossip. ("Do you have any tea on Aunt Linda? Spill the tea!")
- Slide into the DMs: To send a direct message (DM) to someone on social media, usually with romantic or flirtatious intentions.
- Don't be a stranger: (Not slang, but common) Stay in touch; don't disappear.
- Cringe / Cringy: The physical feeling of being embarrassed for someone else when they do something awkward.
- You do you: Be yourself; do whatever makes you happy (often said when giving advice).
- I feel you / I feel that: I understand you; I agree with you.
- I see where you're coming from: I understand your point of view.
- I'm not feeling it: I don't agree with it, or I'm not enjoying it. ("I'm not feeling this party.")
- Lame: Boring, uncool, or not exciting.
- Mood: Used as a caption for a picture to say "I currently feel the exact same way as this picture."
- Fire: Extremely good; fantastic. ("That food was fire.")
- Sus / Sketchy: Suspicious; untrustworthy.
- Low-key: Secretly; you don't want everyone to know. ("I low-key have a crush on him.")
- High-key: Obvious; everyone knows it.
- Thirsty: Desperate for attention (often romantic/sexual attention). Used negatively.
- No cap: No lie; I am telling the absolute truth. ("I can do 100 pushups, no cap.")
- Yas: A highly enthusiastic way to say "Yes!"
- Gucci: Good, nice, or fine. ("Don't worry bro, it's all gucci.")
- Stan: An obsessed, mega-fan of a celebrity (originates from an Eminem song). ("He is a total Kendrick Lamar stan.")
- Keep it 100: Keep it 100% real; be completely honest with me.
- Savage: Someone who is brutally honest and does not care about other people's feelings.
- To tell [someone] off: To verbally attack someone or tell them exactly why you are angry at them. ("I got fired because I told my boss off.")

## Linking Consonants: Held T and D

### Summary

When a word ends in /t/ or /d/ and the following word begins with a consonant, native speakers hold the stop without releasing air (e.g., good job, that man, can't go). When the subsequent word begins with a vowel sound, the final consonant links smoothly into the vowel (good effort becomes goo-deffort).

### The Rule: Holding the Consonant

When a word ends in a 'D' or a 'T', and the next word begins with a consonant, do not release your tongue, but also do not skip the letter completely. You must "hold" the sound.

Examples with 'D': - Good job (Hold the D, don't say "goo-duh job", but don't say "goo job"). - Good morning - Red car

Examples with 'T': - That man - That way - I can't go (This is why "can" and "can't" are so hard to differentiate! We do not clearly pronounce the 'T' in "can't go." We just abruptly stop the airflow).

Note: If the following word starts with a VOWEL, the rule changes and you link them smoothly (e.g., "Good effort" becomes "Goo-deffort").

## Controversial Modern Expressions

### Summary

Modern buzzwords and overused phrases like adulting, fur baby, literally (used for hyperbole), my bad, that's my truth, and a Karen provoke strong stylistic criticism. Workplace clichés such as circle back, with all due respect, and faux-apologies like 'I'm sorry you feel that way' frequently sound evasive or insincere.

### The Expressions

- Adulting (verb): Doing normal grown-up responsibilities like paying bills or doing laundry. ("Adulting is hard today.")
- Why people hate it: It sounds immature; adults should just do their chores without complaining.
- Fur baby: A pet dog or cat.
- Why people hate it: People who don't like pets find it silly to compare an animal to a human child.
- I was today years old when...: A humorous way to say you just learned an obvious fact today.
- It is what it is: A phrase meaning the situation cannot be changed, so you must accept it.
- Why people hate it: It sounds like giving up and immediately ends a conversation. It is severely overused.
- Literally: True or factual.
- Why people hate it: People constantly use it incorrectly for exaggeration (e.g., "I literally died laughing").
- My bad: A casual way to say "My mistake" or "I'm sorry."
- Why people hate it: It is grammatically incorrect (an adjective after a possessive pronoun instead of a noun).
- That's my truth: Used to describe one's personal experience or feelings.
- Why people hate it: Truth is objective (a fact). Feelings are subjective. You cannot have a personal "truth."
- I'm obsessed with [something]: I really love it; I'm excited about it.
- Why people hate it: It is an extreme exaggeration that sounds fake or insincere.
- With all due respect: A phrase used right before heavily criticizing someone.
- Why people hate it: The "respect" usually feels completely fake.
- Bestie: Best friend.
- To circle back: Corporate jargon meaning "let's discuss this later."
- Why people hate it: It is annoying office-speak that usually means "I don't want to deal with this right now."
- I'm sorry you feel that way: A non-apology.
- Why people hate it: The speaker is refusing to take responsibility for their actions and is instead blaming the victim's feelings.
- A Karen: A pushy, demanding, entitled woman who complains constantly (especially to store managers).
- Why people hate it: It has ruined a perfectly nice, normal name for real women named Karen.

## 10 Advanced Adjectives (Part 10)

### Summary

Advanced descriptive adjectives include unanimous (yoo-nan-uh-mus, fully agreed), secular, sedentary, lucrative, fleeting, stagnant, idle, and delinquent (overdue or law-breaking). Stress placement differentiates structure: adjective-plus-noun phrases stress the noun (little boy), whereas compound nouns stress the first element (high school, karate class).

### Advanced Words

- Unanimous (yoo-nan-uh-mus): Everyone in the group completely agrees; having the exact same opinion. ("The jury's decision was unanimous.") Do not confuse with 'anonymous' (unknown).
- Secular: Having absolutely no connection with religion. ("He went to a secular high school.")
- Sedentary: Sitting down a lot; lacking physical activity. ("He needs to change his sedentary lifestyle.")
- Lucrative: Highly profitable; making a lot of money. ("He is looking for lucrative business ideas.")
- Harsh: Too strong, angry, or unkind. ("His harsh words made the child cry.")
- Fleeting: Lasting for only a very short time before passing. ("He had a fleeting interest in karate.")
- Stagnant: Not moving, flowing, or growing. ("The politician promised to fix the stagnant economy.")
- Sole: Only one; single. ("The sole purpose of my trip is to see my grandparents.")
- Idle: Not working, not active, or not being used. ("The workers were idle while the machine was broken.")
- Delinquent:
- Meaning 1: Breaking the law (usually a minor crime committed by a young person).
- Meaning 2: Late or overdue (used for payments). ("They lost their home because of delinquent mortgage payments.")

### Pronunciation Note: Compound Nouns vs. Adjective+Noun

- Adjective + Noun: Stress the NOUN. (e.g., little boy)
- Compound Noun: Stress the FIRST word. (e.g., high school, business ideas, karate classes).

## Advanced Alternatives to "Very" (Part 2)

### Summary

Replace basic 'very + adjective' constructions with precise descriptive alternatives: exorbitant (very expensive), sweltering (very hot), superb (very good), appalling or atrocious (very bad), horrendous (very terrible), excruciating (very painful), and minuscule (very small). Specific vivid terms include pouring for rain and stunned for surprise.

### 10 Advanced Upgrades

- Very rainy -> Pouring ("It's been pouring for over an hour.")
- Very expensive -> Exorbitant ("The prices in this store are exorbitant.")
- Very good -> Exceptional or Superb ("The food is superb.")
- Very hot -> Sweltering or Boiling ("The afternoons are sweltering.")
- Very surprised -> Stunned ("I was stunned by the news.")
- Very bad -> Atrocious or Appalling ("His grammar is atrocious.")
- Very terrible -> Horrendous ("He committed a horrendous crime.")
- Very painful -> Excruciating or Unbearable ("She had an excruciating headache.")
- Very small -> Minuscule ("Your problems are minuscule compared to theirs.")
- Very old -> Ancient ("That is an ancient building.")

## Professional Apologies (Part 2)

### Summary

Professional correspondence elevates apologies using formal constructions like we deeply regret, please accept my sincerest apologies, and apologize for the delayed response. Natural lateness idioms distinguish active transit (running late, on my way) from arrival acknowledgments (sorry to have kept you waiting).

### Formal Apology Expressions

Instead of the basic "Sorry," use these sophisticated alternatives, especially in emails: 1. We regret...: Highly formal. ("We regret that we are unable to give you a refund.") 2. We deeply / sincerely regret...: ("We deeply regret the misunderstanding.") 3. We apologize for the negative experience you had. 4. I want to extend my sincerest apologies for the poor service you received. (Note: "Poor service" is a polite way to say "bad service.") 5. Please accept my sincerest apologies that your experience was anything less than you should expect.

### Apologizing for Delays

- To get back to [someone]: To respond or reply.
- Casual: "I'm sorry it took me so long to get back to you."
- Formal: "My sincerest apologies for not getting back to you sooner."
- Delay:
- "I'm sorry for the delay in responding to your email."
- "I apologize for the delayed response."

### Apologizing for Being Late

- To keep [someone] waiting:
- Texting before arriving: "I'm sorry to keep you waiting."
- When you finally arrive: "I'm sorry to have kept you waiting."
- Running late: The most natural way to say you are delayed.
- "I'm running five minutes late."
- On [one's] way: Currently traveling to the location.
- "She's on her way."

## 10 Advanced Adjectives for People (Part 6)

### Summary

Character description relies on precise traits: positive attributes include tactful, trustworthy, tenacious, witty, and conscientious. Negative or neutral personality traits encompass frugal (strictly economical), gullible (easily duped), outspoken, conceited (arrogant), and greedy.

### Advanced Vocabulary

#### Positive Traits

- Tactful: Always knows the polite, correct thing to say in sensitive or difficult situations. ("Please find a tactful way to give her the bad news.")
- Trustworthy: Honest; someone you can completely trust. ("My trustworthy neighbor.")
- Tenacious: Hard-working and determined; someone who never gives up even when facing obstacles.
- Witty: Funny in a fast, highly intelligent way. ("A witty sense of humor.")
- Conscientious: Honest and extremely hard-working; someone who carefully ensures they do things correctly.

#### Neutral / Negative Traits

- Frugal: Very careful about spending money (not necessarily selfish, just economical).
- Gullible: Easily fooled or tricked because they trust everyone too much. ("The salesman convinced the gullible customer to buy the broken car.")
- Outspoken: Speaking very directly with strong opinions, rarely caring what others think.
- Greedy: Always wanting more (usually money or food) and never being satisfied.
- Conceited: Arrogant; feeling that you are much better than everybody else.

## 10 Advanced "-ous" Adjectives (Part 8)

### Summary
