The English suffix -ous is consistently reduced to an unstressed /əs/ ('us') sound, found in adjectives like monotonous, tedious, hazardous, anonymous, vigorous, tremendous, studious, ambiguous, and oblivious. Note the initial-syllable stress in infamous (IN-fuh-mus), meaning renowned for negative reasons.

### Advanced "-ous" Words

- Monotonous: Always the same; boring and flat. ("A monotonous voice.")
- Tedious: Boring due to endless, slow repetition (often used for jobs). ("A tedious task.")
- Hazardous: Highly dangerous or risky. ("Smoking is hazardous to your health.")
- Infamous (IN-fuh-mus): Famous for a very bad or evil reason. ("The infamous criminal.") Note: Stress the FIRST syllable.
- Anonymous: Having an unknown or hidden name. ("An anonymous letter.")
- Vigorous: Highly energetic, physical, and strong. ("Vigorous exercise.")
- Tremendous: Extremely large, impressive, or great in amount. ("Tremendous progress.")
- Studious: Loving to study and read. ("His brother is very studious.")
- Ambiguous: Unclear; having two or more possible meanings. ("The poem is intentionally ambiguous.")
- Oblivious: Completely unaware of what is happening around you. ("He was oblivious to his surroundings.")

## Easily Confused Phrasal Verbs

### Summary

Prepositional shifts radically alter verbal meaning: fill in for (substitute) versus fill out (complete a form) versus fill in on (update); come down to (hinge upon) versus come down with (fall ill); and put up (erect) versus put up with (tolerate). Automotive expressions contrast pull up (arrive alongside), pull over (stop curbside), and pull into (enter a spot).

### Phrasal Verb Sets

#### 1. Fill

- To fill [someone] in: To provide missing information to someone. ("Please fill me in on what happened.")
- To fill in FOR [someone]: To temporarily substitute for someone at work. ("Can you fill in for me tomorrow?")
- To fill in: To write in a blank space. ("Fill in the blank.")
- To fill out: To complete an entire document or form. ("Fill out the application.")
- To fill up: To make completely full with liquid. ("Fill up the gas tank.")

#### 2. Walk & Call

- To walk in ON [someone]: To accidentally enter a room while someone is doing something private.
- To call [someone] out: To verbally challenge someone's bad behavior; to demand they explain themselves. ("I had to call him out for lying.")

#### 3. Come & Break

- To come down to: To ultimately depend on. ("The decision comes down to money.")
- To come down with: To become sick. ("I am coming down with a cold.")
- To break out in: To suddenly develop a skin rash or sweat. ("I broke out in a rash.")
- To break out of: To escape. ("He broke out of prison.")

#### 4. Put

- To put up: To build or erect. ("Put up a building/tent.")
- To put [someone] up: To let someone stay at your house. ("I'll put you up in the guest room.")
- To put up with: To tolerate something highly annoying. ("I can't put up with this noise!")

#### 5. Car Phrasal Verbs

- To pull up: To stop your car next to a location. ("The bus will pull up here.")
- To pull over: To move to the side of the road and stop (usually by the police). ("I was pulled over.")
- To pull into: To steer into a parking spot or garage.

#### 6. Turn

- To turn in: To go to bed, OR to submit an assignment.
- To turn to [someone]: To seek help or advice from someone. ("I turn to my brother for advice.")
- To turn into: To transform or become. ("Ice turns into water.")

## 23 Phrasal Verbs with "GET"

### Summary

The verb get spans diverse idioms: get around to (finally tackle), get away with (avoid consequences), get down to (focus seriously), get through to (make someone understand), and get it together (organize oneself). For transportation, use get in / out of for cars and get on / off for public transit and open vehicles (buses, planes, trains, motorcycles).

### Phrasal Verbs with GET

#### Travel & Action

- Get around: To travel from place to place. ("It's easy to get around by train.")
- Get around to it: To finally do a delayed/procrastinated task. ("I'll clean my room when I get around to it.")
- Get away: To escape or take a vacation. (Noun: a getaway).
- Get to it: To start doing something. ("I'll get to it tomorrow.")
- Get down to: To begin doing something seriously. ("Let's get down to business.")

#### Conflict & Consequences

- Get away with [something]: To avoid punishment for doing something wrong. ("He got away with murder.")
- Get back at [someone]: To take revenge on someone.
- Get to [someone]: To deeply annoy or upset someone. ("The noise is getting to me. Don't let it get to you.")
- Get [someone] down: To make someone feel sad or depressed. ("Rainy days get me down.")

#### Communication & Relationships

- Get back to [someone]: To reply to a message.
- Get back with [someone]: To resume a romantic relationship after a breakup.
- Get together: To meet socially. (Noun: a get-together).
- Get through to [someone]: To succeed in making someone understand your point. ("I can't get through to him.")

#### Recovery & Progress

- Get over [someone/something]: To recover emotionally or physically. ("Get over a breakup / Get over the flu.")
- Get it over with: To quickly finish an unpleasant task so you don't have to deal with it anymore.
- Get through [something]: To survive or finish a difficult period. ("Get through a crisis.")
- Get it together: To organize your life, stop making foolish decisions, and improve.
- Get on: To make progress. ("How are you getting on at school?")
- Get on with it: To continue doing something after being interrupted.

#### Transportation Rules

- Get IN / Get OUT OF: Used for Cars.
- Get ON / Get OFF: Used for Buses, Planes, Trains, Boats, Motorcycles.

## Advanced Newspaper Vocabulary

### Summary

Legal and journalistic reporting uses precise terms such as sue, allegedly (uh-LEDGE-id-lee), fraudster, broker a deal, incentivize, perpetrator, and corporate behemoth. Narrative transitions like on the heels of and executive idioms like bring someone up to speed provide concise structural clarity.

### Journalistic & Legal Vocabulary

- To sue: To take legal action against someone in court.
- Allegedly (uh-LEDGE-id-lee): Said to have happened, but without proof. ("He allegedly stole the money.")
- Fraud: The crime of using dishonest methods to get something valuable (money). (Person: a fraudster).
- Behemoth (buh-HEE-muth): A huge creature, or metaphorically, a massive and powerful organization (like Amazon or Apple).
- To broker: To arrange a deal or agreement between two groups. ("They brokered a peace agreement.")
- Third party: A person or company involved in an agreement but not one of the main two participants.
- To incentivize: To motivate someone to do something, usually with a reward (like money). ("The company incentivized employees with bonuses.")
- Misleading: Making you believe something that is not true. ("A misleading advertisement.")
- Scheme: A clever plan, especially to do something bad or illegal.
- Perpetrator: A person who commits a crime or does something illegal.
- On the heels of: Happening soon after something else. ("The disaster comes on the heels of the earthquake.")
- To renew: To begin again with more energy. ("Renewed efforts.")
- To quell: To suppress, crush, or reduce completely. ("Police quelled the riot.")
- Guidelines: Rules or instructions.
- To bring someone up to speed: To give someone the most recent, latest information. ("Please bring me up to speed on the project.")

## Conversational Fluency with Drake (Part 2)

### Summary

Casual regional discourse distinguishes condos (owned with a mortgage) from apartments (rented from a landlord), and uses idioms like the outskirts, laid-back, and cost estimations like you're looking at. In conversational American speech, /t/ after /n/ is regularly dropped, turning winter into winner, twenty into twenny, and interview into innerview.

### Key Expressions & Vocabulary

- The Strip: A famous stretch of land or boulevard known for entertainment (e.g., the Las Vegas Strip, the Sunset Strip).
- To picture: To imagine or visualize. ("I can't picture myself living there.")
- Condo vs. Apartment:
- Condo: You own it. You pay a mortgage (loan payment to the bank).
- Apartment: You rent it. You pay rent to a landlord.
- Laid-back: Relaxed; not easily stressed. ("He has a very laid-back personality.")
- Outskirts: The outer edges of a city, usually quieter or less developed. ("I live on the outskirts of town.")
- You're looking at...: A conversational way to say "This is the approximate cost." ("If you want a new car, you're looking at $30,000.")
- Pretty decent: Good or satisfactory, but not amazing. ("The food was pretty decent.")
- Just as [adjective]: Equally. ("The summer is just as bad as the winter.")
- Rough / Tough: Difficult, cruel, or hard to endure. ("That's a tough situation." / "I had a rough day.")

### Pronunciation Note: Dropping the 'T'

In American English, when a 'T' directly follows an 'N', native speakers frequently drop the 'T'. - Winter -> Winner - Twenty -> Twenny - Interview -> Innerview - International -> Innernational

## Fitness & Body Vocabulary with Isaiah

### Summary

Anatomical and athletic descriptions contrast physique and build with specific physical states like lean, bulked up, toned, and having a washboard stomach or defined glutes. Conversational idioms include sweet tooth, indulge in, make up for, throw a curveball, and the suffix -esque (e.g., modelesque, picturesque).

### Fitness & Body Vocabulary

- Physique: The form, structure, or shape of a person's body. ("He has an athletic physique.")
- Build: Similar to physique. ("He has a lean build.")
- Lean: Having very little body fat.
- Bulked up / Bulky: Having very large, thick muscles (like a bodybuilder).
- Definition: Highly visible muscles due to low body fat. ("She has great muscle definition.")
- Toned: Firm muscles with low fat (similar to definition).
- Washboard stomach: A stomach with highly visible abdominal muscles (looks like an old-fashioned washboard).
- Glutes / Booty: The buttocks muscles. (Booty is slang; glutes is anatomical/fitness terminology).
- To get in shape / Tip-top shape: Perfect physical condition.

### Conversational Expressions & Verbs

- To better [oneself]: To improve. ("I'm trying to better myself.")
- To uphold: To maintain; to keep from deteriorating. ("To uphold an image.")
- To pinpoint: To locate exactly.
- -esque (suffix): Resembling or like. ("Modelesque" = like a model. "Picturesque" = like a picture).
- Fine line: A very small difference between two things. ("There's a fine line between confidence and arrogance.")
- To attain: To achieve a goal.
- Tedious: Boring and repetitive. ("This job is tedious.")
- Outstanding: Excellent; superior. ("I feel outstanding.")
- A lot that goes into...: It requires a lot of effort and complex parts. ("There's a lot that goes into planning a wedding.")
- Anywhere between: Used for estimating a range. ("It costs anywhere between 10 and 20 dollars.")
- Go about your day: Continue your normal daily routine.
- Sweet tooth: A strong craving or desire for sugary foods.
- To indulge in: To allow yourself to enjoy a pleasure. ("I indulged in some chocolate.")
- To make up for [something]: To compensate for doing something bad by doing something good later.
- To throw a curveball: To completely surprise someone with an unexpected challenge.
- That guy: Slang for a person (usually a man) behaving badly or annoyingly. ("Don't be that guy.")
- Rates: The cost for services. ("What is your hourly rate?")
- To go by [name]: To use a specific name/nickname. ("His name is Robert, but he goes by Bob.")

## Newspaper Vocabulary: The Lottery Winner

### Summary

Financial journalism uses terms like lump sum, record-setting, splashy, and drop money (spend heavily), alongside the phrasal verb come forward. For large numerals, retain the singular form with explicit numbers (two million dollars, five thousand people) but pluralize general indefinite quantities (millions of dollars, thousands of people).

### Money & Real Estate Expressions

- To drop [money]: (Slang) To spend a large amount of money on something very expensive. ("He dropped 25 million on a mansion.")
- Mansion: A massive, highly expensive house.
- To not waste any time / To waste no time: To do something very quickly, often surprisingly fast. ("He wasted no time finding a new job.")
- To come forward: To present yourself publicly or to the authorities. ("A witness came forward.")
- Record-setting: Achieving something that has never been done before. ("A record-setting prize.")
- Lump sum: A single, complete payment of money, rather than smaller monthly or yearly installments.
- Splashy: Highly colorful, showy, or designed to attract a lot of attention. ("A splashy wedding.")
- To amount to: To add up to a specific total. ("The bill amounted to $200.")
- Priciest: The superlative form of "pricey"; the most expensive.
- Slight: Small in amount. ("A slight discount," "A slight headache.")
- To list: To make a property publicly available for sale. (Noun: A listing).

### Grammar Rule: Pluralizing "Million", "Thousand", "Hundred"

- If you provide a specific number, DO NOT add an 'S':
- "Two million dollars."
- "Five thousand people."
- If you do NOT provide a specific number, you MUST add an 'S':
- "Millions of dollars."
- "Thousands of people."

## Public Speaking & Communication Tips

### Summary

Executive speech requires projecting volume, maintaining an upright posture, using measured gestures, and emphasizing the final three words of a thought with deliberate cadence. Effective presentations follow a clean tripartite structure—introduction, three body points, and conclusion—while actively eliminating hesitation.

### Key Communication Tips

- Pacing: Speak at a medium pace. Emphasize the last three words of your sentence and pause briefly.
- Volume: Imagine a volume dial. If you usually speak at a '6', consciously turn it up to an '8'. You must project your voice to reach the back of the room.
- Gestures: Use your hands about once per sentence. Keep them "softball-sized" or "football-sized." Stiff, robotic bodies look unenthusiastic.
- Posture: Put your body into a confident posture (shoulders back, chin up), even if you are terrified. Acting confident physically often tricks your brain into feeling confident.
- Organization: Stick to the classic structure: Introduction, Body (3 points), Conclusion.
- Speaking Up: In meetings, don't sit silently. You must "speak up" (participate and contribute).

### Vocabulary Used in the Interview

- Cadence: The flow and rhythm of your speech. ("A professional speaking cadence.")
- Jittery: Highly nervous, shaking, and unable to relax. ("I feel jittery before a presentation.")
- To get it over with: To quickly finish an unpleasant task so you no longer have to worry about it.
- Composed: Calm and in control of your emotions (the opposite of jittery).
- To project [your voice]: To speak loudly and clearly so everyone in a large room can hear you.
- To come across: How you appear or are perceived by other people. ("She comes across as very confident.")
- As you go: While you progress or do something. ("You will figure it out as you go.")

## 16 Workplace Expressions

### Summary

Corporate communication employs functional idioms such as prior to (before), jump into, sign off on (approve officially), get on a call, take off (succeed rapidly), and blanket term. Essential homophones must be distinguished in professional writing: site (physical location), sight (vision), and cite (to reference).

### Professional Expressions

- To take off: To quickly become successful or popular. ("Her career really took off.")
- Prior to: A formal way to say "before." ("Prior to the meeting...")
- To jump into: To start doing something quickly or immediately. ("Let's jump into the presentation.")
- I should say: A phrase used to politely correct yourself when you make a speaking mistake.
- To pop up: To appear suddenly or unexpectedly in multiple places. ("New restaurants are popping up everywhere.")
- The fabric (of a place): The unique identity, culture, and history of a city or organization.
- Right on the water: Located directly next to the ocean or a lake.
- High-grossing: Making a massive amount of money/profit. ("A high-grossing movie.")
- As well: Also; in addition. ("I'll take the salad as well.")
- To fly in: To arrive by airplane. ("The CEO is flying in tomorrow.")
- To sign off on [something]: To officially approve a project or decision. ("We can't start until the director signs off on it.")
- To get on a call: To speak on the phone or a video meeting instead of communicating via email. ("Let's get on a call to figure out the details.")
- Blanket term: A single word or phrase used to describe a broad range of related things.
- All of the above: Everything that was previously mentioned in a list.
- Site vs. Sight vs. Cite:
- Site: A location (Construction site).
- Sight: Vision (Eyesight).
- Cite: To quote or mention.

## Confident Intonation & Uptalk

### Summary

Statements of fact, commands, lists, and confirmations require falling intonation at sentence ends to signal authority and finality. Uptalk—rising pitch on declarative statements—mistakenly conveys doubt, insecurity, or a request for validation, whereas rising intonation should be strictly reserved for yes/no questions and unfinished thoughts.

### Falling Intonation (Confidence)

A downward pitch at the end of a sentence communicates certainty, completion, and authority. Use falling intonation for: 1. Facts: "My name is John." (Pitch goes down on John). 2. Commands: "Please close the door." 3. The end of a list: "I bought apples, bananas, and pears." (Pitch falls on pears). 4. Confirmations: "Yes, that's right."

### Rising Intonation

An upward pitch at the end of a sentence communicates that something is incomplete or questioning. 1. Yes/No Questions: "Did you finish the report?" 2. Unfinished Thoughts: "If I have time..." (Pitch goes up, waiting for the rest of the sentence).

### The Danger of Uptalk (Upspeak)

Uptalk is the habit of using rising intonation at the end of a statement, making it sound like a question. - Example: "My name is Alex?" "I think we should hire candidate B?" - Why it's bad: It makes the speaker sound unsure, insecure, weak, and less intelligent. It forces the listener to constantly validate the speaker. - The Fix: Consciously force your pitch down at the end of your declarative sentences to sound like a confident leader.

## Speaking Like a Leader

### Summary

Executive presence requires replacing apologies with appreciation (e.g., 'Thank you for hearing me out' instead of 'Sorry to disagree') and articulating achievements through measurable business outcomes. Eliminate hedges like 'I think' or 'Does that make sense?' in favor of decisive phrasing: 'Based on the data,' 'I anticipate,' and 'What are your thoughts?'.

### Habit 1: Over-Apologizing

Apologizing for minor things makes you appear weak and indecisive. Substitute "I'm sorry" with "Thank you." - Instead of "Sorry to disagree" -> "Thank you for hearing me out." - Instead of "Sorry you had to do extra work" -> "Thank you for putting in that extra effort." - Instead of "Sorry to interrupt" -> "I'd like to jump in here for a moment." - Instead of "Sorry to bother you" -> "Do you have a minute to talk?"

### Habit 2: Minimizing Accomplishments

While humility is valued in some cultures, in the American workplace, minimizing your success can hurt your career. - Don't deny compliments; simply say "Thank you." - Talk about your achievements in terms of beneficial outcomes: "Our team managed to cut customer complaints by 50%."

### Habit 3: Uncertain Speech

Eliminate words that make you sound unsure. - Instead of "I think / I feel" -> "I predict / I anticipate / I envision." - Instead of "I think..." -> "Based on the data, it appears that..." - Instead of "I don't know" -> "I don't have that information right now" OR "I'm not currently in a position to answer that." - Instead of "I'll try" -> "I'll make it happen" OR "I'll do my best to ensure it's completed."

### Habit 4: Creating Doubt with Questions

Stop asking weak questions at the end of your statements out of habit. - Instead of "Does that make sense?" (which implies you explained it badly or they are too dumb to understand) -> Ask, "What are your thoughts?" or "Do you have any questions?"

## The "L" Sound After Long Vowels

### Summary

When /l/ follows a long vowel at the end of a word (e.g., feel, school, smile, mail), American pronunciation inserts a subtle schwa transition, sounding like fee-ul, schoo-ul, and may-ul. The American 'dark L' requires lightly holding the tongue tip behind the upper teeth without excessive muscular tension.

### The Technique: Adding the "uh" Sound

If you pronounce words like "feel" as a single, hard syllable, it will sound foreign. You must transition from the long vowel to the L by creating a slight "uh" sound. - Feel -> Sounds like Fee-ul - School -> Sounds like Schoo-ul - Smile -> Sounds like Smy-ul - Mail -> Sounds like May-ul

Important Note on the American L: The American L is not "heavy" or strong. Do not push your tongue hard against your teeth. Lightly touch your tongue behind your top teeth and hold it there longer.

## Dropping the 'T' After 'N'

### Summary

In standard conversational American English, /t/ immediately following /n/ is frequently dropped in unstressed syllables. This elision naturally transforms twenty into twenny, interview into innerview, international into innernational, and winter into winner.

### The Rule: N + T = N

When a 'T' directly follows an 'N', native speakers frequently drop the 'T' entirely. The word simply flows as if the 'T' does not exist.

While pronouncing the 'T' is perfectly acceptable and grammatically correct, dropping the 'T' sounds much more natural and conversational to American ears.

Examples: - Twenty -> Twenny - Interview -> Innerview - Atlanta -> Adlanna (or Ad-lan-uh) - International -> Innernational - Plenty -> Plenny - Winter -> Winner

## Word Stress: Nouns vs. Adjectives

### Summary

Adjective-plus-noun phrases place primary stress on the noun (new PHONE, good DAY), whereas compound nouns (noun + noun) place primary stress on the first element (CELL phone, PARKING lot, LAPtop). When an adjective precedes a compound noun, the first word of the compound noun still retains the main stress (new CELL phone, big BEDroom).

### Rule 1: Adjective + Noun

When you have an adjective followed by a noun, stress the SECOND word (the noun). - New PHONE - Big HOUSE - Good DAY - Nice JOB - Interesting VIDEO

### Rule 2: Noun + Noun (Compound Nouns)

When you have two nouns put together to create a single meaning (a compound noun), stress the FIRST word. - CELL phone (Not cell PHONE) - PARKING lot - BEDroom - LAPtop - YOUTube video

### Rule 3: Adjective + Compound Noun

When you put an adjective in front of a compound noun, you still stress the first part of the compound noun. - New CELL phone - Big BEDroom - Interesting YOUTube video

## Should You Speak English Fast or Slowly?

### Summary

Rushing spoken English frequently signals anxiety and degrades clarity by slurring consonant clusters and distorting syllable stress. Authoritative communication relies on a controlled, deliberate cadence that slows down to accentuate key stressed syllables.

### Why Fast Speaking is Often a Mistake

Nervous and insecure people tend to rush their speech to "cover up" their grammar or pronunciation mistakes. In contrast, highly confident, successful leaders speak at a measured, controlled pace.

### 4 Questions to Ask Yourself Before Speeding Up

- Am I pronouncing all the English sounds correctly? If you are skipping difficult consonant combinations, speaking faster will just make you sound sloppy and incomprehensible.
- Do I follow the rules of correct word stress? Native speakers talk fast, but they still slow down to heavily stress the correct syllables and words. If you don't know the stress rules, do not speed up.
- Who is my audience? You can be sloppy when casually chatting with friends. But if you are in a professional setting or speaking to a client, clarity is much more important than speed.
- Do people ask me to repeat myself? If people frequently say "Excuse me?" or "What did you say?", it is a massive red flag. Your primary goal is communication, not speed. Slow down immediately until people can understand you on the first try.

## Advanced Vocabulary Test

### Summary

Comprehensive vocabulary testing reinforces retention of advanced lexical items required for academic comprehension, media literacy, and precise spoken nuance. Active recall strengthens the transition from passive recognition to spontaneous usage in formal discourse.

## Relaxed Vowels: The Hollywood Method

### Summary

Natural American speech hinges on relaxed, neutral vowels: the unstressed /ʌ/ ('uh') sound in son, done, and mother must not broaden into an open /ɑ/ ('ah'), as illustrated by contrasting Don with done. Similarly, the relaxed /ɪ/ in live and his requires loosened lips and tongue, preventing distortion into a tense /i/ ('ee').

### The "Uh" Sound (Relaxed)

A major mistake non-native speakers make is opening their mouths too wide and pronouncing an "Ah" sound instead of a relaxed "Uh" sound. - Son (Not San) - Done (Not Don) - Mother (Not Mah-ther) - Much (Not Mah-ch) - Love

Practice drill: "I think Don is done." (Notice the wide mouth for Don, and the relaxed mouth for done).

### The "Ih" Sound (Relaxed)

Another common mistake is making the short "ih" sound tense and turning it into an "ee" sound. Do not stretch your lips into a smile. - Live (Not Leave) - His (Not He's)

Practice drill: "I'm his mother." (Relax the tongue and lips entirely for 'his').

## Polyglot Wisdom with Steve Kaufmann

### Summary

Sustained language acquisition requires engaging deeply with compelling native content, accepting errors outside one's comfort zone, and prioritizing high-frequency vocabulary. Idiomatic fluency relies on conversational phrasings like colloquial (co-LO-kwee-ul), get going, and understanding American pronunciation shifts such as process (PRAH-sess vs. British PRO-sess).

### Steve's Language Learning Philosophy

- You must be engaged: Don't force yourself to study boring textbooks. Find books, movies, or podcasts that "grab you." If the content is interesting, your appetite for learning will grow.
- Attitude is everything: You must truly believe that you will succeed and achieve fluency. If you doubt yourself, you will fail.
- Leave your comfort zone: "If you're unwilling to make mistakes, you will continue to make mistakes." You must step outside your comfort zone and try using new, difficult structures.
- Vocabulary is key: Steve focuses heavily on learning as many advanced words as possible so he can understand native speakers and read newspapers.

### Idioms & Expressions from the Interview

- Quotable: Saying things that are interesting, memorable, and worth repeating. ("He is a very quotable person.")
- To get going: To start doing something, especially after a delay. ("Let's get going on that project.")
- To engage in / with: To actively participate in something or use it. ("We engaged in a long conversation.")
- Colloquial (co-LO-kwee-ul): Informal, everyday conversational language. ("To use a colloquial expression...")
- To grab someone: To deeply interest or affect someone. ("That movie really grabbed me.")
- In the back of your mind: A thought that is always present in the background, even if you aren't actively focusing on it. ("It's been in the back of my mind for weeks.")
- To get anywhere: To make progress or become successful. ("This discussion isn't getting anywhere.")
- To get there: To achieve your goal or to arrive. ("I hope I get there one day.")
- Unwilling: Refusing or not wanting to do something. ("He is unwilling to help.")
- Comfort zone: A safe situation where there are no risks. ("You must step outside your comfort zone.")
- In the long run: Eventually; after a long period of time. ("It's hard work, but it will pay off in the long run.")

### Pronunciation Note

- Process: Americans pronounce the 'O' as an 'Ah' sound -> PRAH-sess. (Canadians and British often say PRO-sess).

## Upgrading "Very": 10 Advanced Words

### Summary

Replace basic intensifiers with high-impact vocabulary: fascinating (very interesting), transparent (very open), straightforward (very honest), destitute or impoverished (very poor), prosperous (very rich), petrified (very scared), brilliant (very intelligent), indignant (very angry), gigantic (very big), and cramped (very crowded). Fluent delivery links consonant codas into initial vowel onsets (e.g., hear da for heard a).

### 10 Advanced Substitutions

- Very interesting -> Fascinating ("That's a fascinating story.")
- Very open -> Transparent ("Thank you for being transparent with me.")
- Very honest -> Straightforward or Frank ("He gave straightforward responses.")
- Very poor -> Destitute or Impoverished ("An impoverished neighborhood.")
- Very rich -> Well-off or Prosperous ("A prosperous city.")
- Very scared -> Petrified or Terrified ("I am petrified of snakes.")
- Very intelligent -> Brilliant ("My professor is brilliant.")
- Very angry -> Indignant or Enraged ("The indignant customer complained.")
- Very big -> Gigantic or Enormous ("Gigantic waves.")
- Very crowded -> Cramped or Jam-packed ("I hate cramped elevators.")

### Pronunciation Note: Linking

When speaking fluently, native speakers connect the final consonant of one word to the beginning vowel of the next word. - "I heard a..." sounds like "I hear da..."

## Rhythm & Reduction: Or, Than, From

### Summary

Connected speech rhythmically reduces function words: or reduces to /ər/ in binomial pairs like sink or swim, make or break, and give or take (soup or salad sounds like super salad). Similarly, than reduces to /ðən/ (easier said than done, better late than never), and from reduces to /frəm/ in fixed idioms (from scratch, from the get-go).

### Reducing "Or"

In natural speech, the word "or" is reduced so heavily that "Soup or salad" sounds exactly like "Super salad." - Sink or swim: Succeed or fail on your own without help. - Like it or not: It will happen even if you disagree. - Take it or leave it: The final offer; no negotiation. - Give or take: Approximately. ("It costs $100, give or take.") - Make or break: Resulting in total success or total failure. ("A make-or-break interview.") - For better or worse: Accepting the bad results along with the good ones. - Rain or shine: Happening regardless of the weather.

### Reducing "Than"

"Than" is usually reduced to a short "thun" or "then" sound. - Easier said than done: Sounds simple but is hard to do. - Better safe than sorry: Wiser to be careful than to take a risk. - More often than not: Usually; most of the time. - Better late than never: Better to do something late than not at all.

### Reducing "From"

"From" is reduced unless it is the final word in a sentence ("Where are you from?"). - From head to toe: Completely covered. ("Dressed in black from head to toe.") - From scratch: Beginning without using anything already prepared. ("I baked the pie from scratch.") - From the get-go: From the very beginning. ("I liked the idea from the get-go.") - From bad to worse: Getting increasingly bad.

## Rhythm & Reduction: Pronouns & "And"

### Summary

The conjunction and typically reduces to an unstressed /n/ link in classic binomial pairs: bacon 'n' eggs, part and parcel, wear and tear, ins and outs, and spick and span. Unstressed possessive pronouns undergo severe vowel reduction, such as our reducing to /ɑr/ ('are' or 'er') rather than /aʊ.ər/.

### Reducing "And"

In natural speech, the 'A' and the 'D' in the word "and" are dropped. It simply becomes a soft 'N' sound that links two words together. - Bacon and eggs -> Bacon 'n' eggs - Salt and pepper -> Salt 'n' pepper - Husband and wife -> Husband 'n' wife - Trial and error -> Trial 'n' error

### Idioms using "And" Pairs

- Part and parcel: An essential, integral part of something. ("Stress is part and parcel of this job.")
- Hustle and bustle: Busy, noisy activity. ("I love the hustle and bustle of New York.")
- The ins and outs: The detailed complexities of a situation. ("Learn the ins and outs of the software.")
- By and large: Generally speaking. ("By and large, it was a good year.")
- Safe and sound: Free from danger; unharmed. ("They arrived home safe and sound.")
- On pins and needles: Very anxious, nervous, or excited while waiting. ("I was on pins and needles waiting for the test results.")
- Wear and tear: Natural damage that happens over time with use. ("The carpet shows wear and tear.")
- Touch and go: An uncertain situation that could end well or badly. ("The surgery was touch and go.")
- Give and take: Making compromises to get along. ("Marriage is about give and take.")
- Smoke and mirrors: Deception used to make things look better than they are. ("The company's profits were just smoke and mirrors.")
- Spick and span: Extremely clean. ("I cleaned the kitchen until it was spick and span.")
- Odds and ends: Small, miscellaneous, random leftover items.
- Ups and downs: Good times and bad times.
- Night and day: Completely totally different. ("The two brothers are like night and day.")

### Reducing Pronouns

Pronouns are rarely stressed. The word "Our" is almost never pronounced as "Oww-er" in flowing speech. It is reduced to "Are" or "Er." ("This is our house.")

## The HiNative App & Foreign Language Diaries

### Summary

Maintaining a target-language journal actively converts passive vocabulary into spontaneous production, while targeted native feedback verifies natural syntax and nuanced word choice. A vital grammatical constraint requires the prepositional phrase looking forward to to be followed by a noun or gerund (e.g., looking forward to seeing you, never the bare infinitive see).

### The Diary Technique

When you keep a journal in English, you are forced to figure out how to express your own, unique thoughts. This is far more effective than reading textbook dialogues. When you don't know how to say something, look it up, write it down, and you will remember it.

### The HiNative App

The main problem with keeping a diary is that no one corrects your grammar. Lisa recommends the HiNative app to solve this. - You can post sentences and ask native speakers: "Does this sound natural?" - You can ask about the difference between two confusing words. - You can request voice recordings of native speakers pronouncing difficult words (like schedule or curriculum). - You can record your own voice and ask for feedback on your accent.

### Common Grammar Mistake: "Looking Forward To"

- Incorrect: I am looking forward to see you.
- Correct: I am looking forward to seeing you.
- Rule: The phrase "looking forward to" must be followed by a noun or a gerund (an -ing verb).

## Pronouncing "Daughter" and "Water"

### Summary

In American English, vowel digraphs au, aw, and ough produce a deep open /ɔ/ ('aw') in words like daughter, water, and bought. Intervocalic /t/ converts to a voiced flap /d/ (daw-der, wah-der), the rhotic coda /r/ remains fully pronounced, and rapid connected speech drops initial /h/ in unstressed pronouns (bought her becomes botter).

### Rule 1: The "Aw" Vowel Sound

The combination of AU, AW, or OUGH is frequently pronounced with a deep, open "Aw" sound. - You do not say "ow" for daughter. It is Daw. - Other examples: August, Auto, Author, Saw, Law, Bought, Thought.

### Rule 2: The Fast "D" (Flap T)

In American English, when a "T" is located between two vowel sounds, it softens and changes into a fast "D" sound (sometimes called a flap T). - Because the gh is silent in daughter, the t is sandwiched between the au and the e. - Daughter -> Daw-der - Water -> Wah-der - Better -> Bed-er - Later -> Lay-der

### Rule 3: The American "R" is Never Silent

Unlike British English, where the "R" at the end of a word is often dropped (e.g., Wa-tuh), the American 'R' is hard and fully pronounced at all times. - Daughter -> Daw-der - Water -> Wah-der

### Pronunciation Note: Dropping the 'H' in Pronouns

When speaking quickly, native speakers frequently drop the 'H' sound at the beginning of pronouns like her and him. - "I bought her a car" -> Sounds like "I botter a car."

## Confident Professional English

### Summary

Executive conciseness requires pruning wordy phrases (e.g., replacing due to the fact that with because, and for the purpose of with to), while cutting filler expressions like kind of like and habitual side particles (basically, technically). An intentional silent pause should replace reflexive filler words, projecting confidence and clarity.

### Tip 1: Don't Be Verbose (Be Concise)

"Verbose" means using more words than necessary. In a professional setting, get straight to the point. Eliminate bulky phrases: - Due to the fact that -> Because - In the event that -> If - For the purpose of -> To - The fact that -> (Eliminate completely)

### Tip 2: Avoid Filler Words

Filler words like "kind of like," "you know," and "sort of" make you sound uncertain. - Unprofessional: "We kind of like need to update the system." - Professional: "We need to update the system."

### Tip 3: Avoid "Side Particles"

Side particles are words that people repeat out of habit that add zero real value to the sentence. Using them too often is distracting. - Examples: Basically, technically, actually, essentially, quite frankly. - Unprofessional: "Basically, the report is due on Monday." - Professional: "The report is due on Monday."

### Tip 4: Avoid Disclaimers (Qualifications)

A disclaimer is a phrase that instantly lowers the value of what you are about to say. People use them when they are nervous about being judged. - Examples: "This may sound like a silly idea, but...", "I'm sure my suggestion isn't very good, but...", "It's just my opinion, but..." - Action: State your opinion confidently without diminishing it first.

### The Solution: The Silent Pause

To break these bad habits, replace the filler word or side particle with a silent pause. When you feel the urge to say "basically," say the word pause or period in your head, take a breath, and then deliver your sentence.

## Political Vocabulary & Expressions

### Summary

Political and leadership discourse relies on vivid idioms such as pass the baton, vet candidates, vote up and down the ticket, endorse, and hanging by a thread. Figurative expressions like have a corner on, cover a lot of territory, at stake, and dare I say establish nuanced, persuasive positions.

### Key Political Vocabulary & Idioms

- To pass the baton: To pass leadership or responsibility to the next person. ("The retiring CEO passed the baton.")
- To vet someone: To thoroughly investigate someone's background to see if they are suitable for a position. ("They vet all candidates.")
- Up and down the ticket: Supporting political candidates at all levels (President, Governor, Mayor, etc.) within the same party.
- To endorse: To officially and publicly support someone or something. ("The union endorsed the candidate.")
- Dare I say: Used to introduce a slightly controversial, surprising, or bold opinion. ("The new design is, dare I say, brilliant.")
- The press: Journalists and news organizations.
- At stake: At risk; something important that is being decided. ("There is a lot of money at stake.")
- To plunder: To steal or take by force, usually in a chaotic situation.
- Folks: A casual, warm, and friendly word for "people." ("How are you folks doing today?")
- Like taking candy from a baby: Extremely easy to do (and often unfair).
- Hanging by a thread: In a very unstable, dangerous, or precarious situation. ("His job is hanging by a thread.")
- The highest office: The presidency. (Office = a position of political authority).
- To wrap up: To finish or complete something. ("Let's wrap up this meeting.")
- To have a corner on something: To have exclusive dominance or control over something. ("Nobody has a corner on good ideas.")
- To cover a lot of territory: To discuss many topics or do many different things.
- Stranded: Left helpless without a way to move forward or escape. ("Stranded at the airport.")

## 13 English Proverbs About Work

### Summary

Traditional proverbs encapsulate core cultural work values: opportunity (make hay while the sun shines, if you snooze, you lose), diligence (the harder you work, the luckier you get), pragmatism (if it ain't broke, don't fix it), and risk balance (nothing ventured, nothing gained; hope for the best but prepare for the worst).

### Work & Opportunity Proverbs

- Make hay while the sun shines: Take action while a good opportunity exists before it goes away.
- Failing to plan is planning to fail: Planning is a crucial component of success.
- The harder you work, the luckier you get: Success looks like "luck" to outsiders, but it is actually the result of hard work.
- If it ain't broke, don't fix it: (Slang grammar). If something is working well, don't try to change it.
- Where there's a will, there's a way: If you have a strong desire to achieve something, you will find a solution.
- Necessity is the mother of invention: Difficult situations and urgent needs inspire people to invent solutions.
- If you snooze, you lose: If you don't act quickly, you will miss out on the opportunity.
- Don't bite off more than you can chew: Don't take on more responsibilities than you can handle.
- Nothing ventured, nothing gained: You have to take risks if you want to achieve big things.
- Hope for the best but prepare for the worst: Keep a positive attitude but have a backup plan in case of a disaster.
- Don't cross a bridge until you come to it: Don't worry about future problems before they actually happen.
- Hard work never killed anyone: A sarcastic response to someone making lazy excuses.
- Hard work without talent is a shame, but talent without hard work is a tragedy: (A quote). Use your gifts, or they are wasted.

## Advanced Vocabulary: Tech & Loneliness

### Summary

Sociological and psychological discourse employs terms such as stoke (fuel emotions), binge, striking, interlinked, and physiological (distinguished by an audible initial /p/, unlike silent-p psychological). Unstressed syllables across academic vocabulary routinely reduce to the schwa /ə/ (e.g., Har-vuhrd, Stan-fuhrd, Tech-nol-uh-gy).

### Key Vocabulary

- Recipe (Pronunciation: Res-uh-pee, not receipt): A formula or set of conditions that lead to a result. ("A recipe for loneliness.")
- Interlinked: Connected together; having an effect on each other.
- To stoke: To fuel or increase something (like a fire, fear, or anger). ("The media stoked the public's anger.")
- To binge: To do something excessively for an extended period, like eating, drinking, or watching TV. ("Binge-watching Netflix.")
- Striking: Noticeable in an impressive, surprising, or attractive way. ("The results were striking.")
- Detached: Emotionally distant; disconnected. ("Socially detached.")
- Physiological: Relating to the body's physical and biological functions (e.g., heart rate, breathing). Note: The 'P' is pronounced, unlike in 'psychological' where the 'P' is silent.
- Distress: Extreme unhappiness, worry, or physical pain.
- To endure: To suffer through a difficult or painful situation for a long time.

### Accent Practice: The Schwa Sound

In American English, vowels in unstressed syllables are frequently reduced to a short "uh" sound (the schwa). - Harvard -> Har-vuhrd - Stanford -> Stan-fuhrd - Princeton -> Prince-tuhn - American -> Uh-mer-i-cuhn - Technology -> Tech-nol-uh-gy

## Wall Street Vocabulary

### Summary

Financial reporting uses specialized verbs and idioms: rally, soar, leap, and drive the market, along with on track for and all-time high. Crucial grammatical distinctions separate intransitive rise (prices rise) from transitive raise (companies raise prices), while identical adjacent consonants blend seamlessly in connected speech (e.g., speak Spanish as spea-kpanish).

### Financial & Business Vocabulary

- To drive: To cause something to happen or move. ("Intel drove the stock market higher.")
- To rally / A rally: To recover and become stronger after a period of weakness. ("The stock market rallied.")
- S&P 500: The Standard & Poor's 500; a list of 500 large US companies used to track the performance of the stock market.
- Rise vs. Raise: Rise goes up on its own (intransitive: "The prices rose"). Raise is making something go up (transitive: "They raised the prices").
- On track for: Moving in the right direction to achieve a goal. ("She is on track for a promotion.")
- Straight: Consecutive; happening one after another without a break. ("Three straight winning weeks.")
- All-time high: The highest level ever recorded.
- To soar: To rise or increase extremely quickly. ("Gas prices soared.")
- By far: Used to emphasize that something is significantly greater than everything else. ("By far the best pizza.")
- Unemployment benefits: Money provided by the government to people who have lost their jobs.
- Pace: The speed or rate at which something happens. ("The pace of the meeting.")
- Layoffs / To be laid off: Losing a job because the company cannot afford to keep the worker (not because the worker did a bad job).
- Disconcerting: Worrying, uncomfortable, disturbing, or upsetting. ("The news was disconcerting.")
- Leap: A sudden, large jump or increase.
- 4-year high: The highest level in the past four years.

### Pronunciation Tip: Blending Consonants

When one word ends with a consonant and the next word begins with the exact same consonant, do not pronounce the consonant twice. Blend them into one continuous sound. - Chipmaker stock -> Chipmaker stock - Speaks Spanish -> Speak Spanish - I'm married -> I'married

## Professional Voice Tips from Siri

### Summary

Vocal authority requires active mouth articulation, deep diaphragmatic breathing, and lowering pitch by relaxing throat muscles. Advanced industry vocabulary includes the talent (voice performer), ubiquitous, second nature, and straddle a line, balanced by maintaining natural diction without artificial over-annunciation.

### Susan's Tips for a Professional Voice

- Move your mouth: Don't mumble or speak with a tight mouth. Move your lips, face, and jaw to give yourself room to articulate.
- Deep breathing: If you are nervous, breathe in for 5 counts through your nose, hold it, and breathe out for 5 counts through your mouth.
- Lower your pitch: When people get nervous, their pitch goes up and they talk too fast. A lower, deeper voice sounds more authoritative and relaxed.
- Morgan Freeman's Tip: Yawn frequently before a presentation to completely relax your throat muscles. This naturally lowers your pitch.
- Articulate, but don't overdo it: You should pronounce consonants clearly so that people understand you, but you don't want to sound like a fake radio announcer ("announcery").
- Practice reading aloud: Most things that are written (books, magazines) are difficult to read aloud because the sentences are long. Practicing reading aloud trains you to look ahead and prepare your breath.

### Advanced Vocabulary & Expressions

- The talent: An industry word for the performer or voice-over artist.
- To articulate: To pronounce everything clearly and move your mouth well.
- To straddle: To stand or sit with a leg on either side of something, or to exist across multiple categories. ("It's a tough line to straddle.")
- Second nature: Something you have done so many times that it is an automatic, thoughtless habit. ("Driving is second nature to me.")
- To loosen up: To relax and become less tense.
- Ubiquitous: Present or found everywhere. ("Siri's voice is ubiquitous.")
- To take off: To suddenly become very successful or popular. ("Her business took off.")
- Never ever: An extreme, emphatic version of "never."

## Why English Vowels Are Confusing

### Summary

Because English is a stress-timed language, vowels in unstressed syllables collapse into the neutral schwa /ə/, making distinct spellings like Adam and atom or lesson and lessen phonetically identical. Natural connected speech links final consonants into opening vowels (final answer -> fi-na-lanswer) and holds stop consonants before next consonants (big cactus).

### The Rule: Reduced Vowels (The Schwa)

In English, vowels in an unstressed syllable are reduced to a short, lazy "uh" sound (the schwa). This means that completely different vowels often sound identical.

Example: Adam vs. Atom - The 'A' in Adam and the 'O' in Atom are both unstressed. - Therefore, both the 'A' and the 'O' are reduced to "uh." - Furthermore, the 'D' and the 'T' are between two vowels, turning them both into a fast flap-T. - Result: Adam and Atom are pronounced exactly the same.

Example: Lesson vs. Lessen - The 'O' and the 'E' are unstressed and sound exactly the same.

### Connected Speech Rules

Native speakers rarely pronounce words individually; they link them together. - Connect a consonant to a vowel: Final answer -> Fi-na-lanswer. - Connect similar consonants: With the elevator -> With-e-elevator. - Hold stop consonants: Big cactus -> Hold the 'G' slightly before making the 'C' sound.

## Real-Life Linking & Reduction

### Summary

Authentic conversational English aggressively reduces grammatical function words: to becomes /tə/ ('tuh'), can becomes /kən/ ('k'n'), and are becomes /ər/ ('er'). Unlike non-rhotic accents, American English preserves a strong, vocalic postvocalic /r/ at the ends of words like nature and fundraiser.

### Real-Life Reductions

Native speakers aggressively reduce function words (prepositions, auxiliary verbs, and pronouns). - To: Reduced to "tuh." ("I love any opportunity tuh dance.") - Can: Reduced to "k'n." ("When I k'n dance in nature.") - Are: Reduced to "er." ("The surfers er beautiful.")

### The Hard American 'R'

The American "R" is never silent at the end of a word (unlike in British English). It is a strong, sustained sound that acts almost like a vowel. - Nature -> Nay-cher - Fundraiser -> Fund-ray-zer

### Real-Life Linking

Native speakers connect the end of one word to the beginning of the next, making multi-word phrases sound like one long word. - Epidemic of loneliness -> Epidemicov loneliness. - As a matter of fact -> Asamatterofact. - From time to time in different ways -> From-time-tuh-time-in-different-ways.

### The Flap 'T' After 'R'

When an 'R' is followed by a 'T', and then followed by a vowel, the 'T' softens into a fast 'D' sound. - Art on the street -> Ar-don the street. - Party -> Par-dy. - Started -> Star-ded.

## 20 Commonly Mispronounced Words

### Summary

Non-phonetic English orthography requires mastering counterintuitive pronunciations: awry (uh-rye), colonel (ker-nul), cocoa (co-co), genre (zhon-ruh), hors d'oeuvre (or-derv), thorough (thur-oh), and warm (worm). Unstressed syllables elide vowels in words like automatically (au-to-mat-ic-lee), while stress falls strictly on the second syllable in develop (de-vel-up).

### Pronunciation Guide

- Awry: (Uh-rye). Meaning: Not going as planned. ("Our plans went awry.")
- Cocoa: (Co-co). The 'a' is completely silent.
- Colonel: (Ker-nul). Pronounced exactly the same as a kernel of corn.
- Automatically: (Au-to-mat-ic-lee). Drop the 'a'. (Same for practically, actually).
- Country: (Cun-tree). The 'ou' sounds like the 'u' in jump.
- Develop: (De-vel-up). Stress the second syllable, not the first or third!
- Entrepreneur: (On-truh-pre-nur). A French loanword; the 'en' sounds like 'on'.
- Figure: (Fig-yer). Not fi-goor.
- Formula: (Form-you-luh). The 'u' says "you."
- Document: (Doc-you-ment). The 'u' says "you."
- Accumulate: (Ac-cyoum-you-late). Both 'u's say "you."
- Cuba: (Cyou-buh). The 'u' says "you."
- Monument: (Mon-you-ment). The 'u' says "you."
- Furious: (Fyour-e-us). The 'u' says "you."
- Genre: (Zhon-ruh). Meaning: A category of art.
- Hors d'oeuvre: (Or-derv). Meaning: An appetizer. (Plural: Hors d'oeuvres).
- Increase / Decrease / Release / Lease: The 's' is a sharp 'S' sound, not a buzzing 'Z'.
- Literally: (Lit-er-uh-lee). Pronounce the 't' as a fast flap-D. Meaning: Exactly, without exaggeration.
- Thorough: (Thur-oh). Meaning: Complete and detailed.
- Warm: (Worm). Not wahrm.

## Actor Training: The Repetition Method

### Summary

Pronunciation mastery requires shifting from conscious analysis to automatic muscle memory by repeatedly rehearsing short monologues. Critical American speech mechanics include maintaining a fully rhotic postvocalic /r/, stressing the first word in compound nouns, distinguishing relaxed /ʌ/ from open /ɔ/ (hunt vs. haunt, cut vs. caught), and sounding medial /ju/ in words like particular.

### Pronunciation Corrections

In the video, Lisa corrects a Russian actor's English monologue. Key corrections include: - The American 'R': The 'R' in American English is never silent. (Mirror, Start, More, Over). - Compound Nouns: When two nouns form one word or phrase, stress the first noun. (Acting class, Social network). - Vowel Confusion: Be careful not to confuse short, relaxed vowels with open vowels. - Hunt (relaxed "uh") vs. Haunt (open "aw"). - But (relaxed "uh") vs. Bought (open "aw"). - Cut (relaxed "uh") vs. Caught (open "aw"). - The "You" Vowel: The letter 'U' is frequently pronounced "you" in the middle of words (Particular, Reputation, Popular, Regular).

### The Monologue Repetition Method

To truly improve your accent, you must move from knowing the rules to making them automatic. 1. Find a short text: Pick a 1-2 minute monologue, speech, or work presentation. Do not read a whole book. 2. Mark it up: Go through the text and underline difficult words, linking opportunities, and intonation changes. 3. Repeat it 50 times: Start by reading it slowly and perfectly. Read it over and over, going slightly faster each time. 4. Memorize it: Once it is memorized, the correct mouth movements will become muscle memory. You are reprogramming your brain to stop thinking about the pronunciation.

## The American "T" Sound (Fast D)

### Summary

When /t/ is positioned between two vowel sounds—either internally within words like water (wah-der) and better (bed-er), or across word boundaries like yes it is (yes i-dis) and what a (wha-da)—it systematically converts into a voiced alveolar flap ('Fast D').

### The Rule: T Between Two Vowels

When a 'T' (or 'TT') is located between two vowel sounds, it changes into a "Fast D." - Water -> Wah-der - Better -> Bed-er - Auto -> Au-do - City -> Cid-y - Computer -> Com-pyou-der

### The Rule Applies Across Words

This rule does not just apply within a single word. In flowing, connected speech, if a word ends in a 'T' and the next word begins with a vowel, the 'T' is sandwiched between two vowels and becomes a Fast D. - Yes it is -> Yes i-dis - At eleven -> A-deleven - What a nice day -> Wha-da nice day - Get in or get out -> Ge-din or ge-dout - I bought it at a computer store -> I baugh-di-da-da computer store

## Advanced English Words (Part 3)

### Summary

Upgrade repetitive 'very + adjective' constructions with specific alternatives: hilarious (very funny), stunning (very beautiful), deafening (very loud), stuffed (very full of food), crucial (very important), bewildered (very confused), and obese (very fat). Adjacent identical consonant sounds merge across word boundaries without doubling (e.g., was stuffed becomes wastuffed).

### 10 Advanced Words

- Very funny -> Hilarious ("That YouTube video was hilarious.")
- Very beautiful -> Stunning or Gorgeous ("You look stunning in that dress.")
- Very loud -> Deafening ("The explosion caused a deafening noise.")
- Very full (with food) -> Stuffed ("I am stuffed after that big meal.")
- Very new -> Brand new ("It's a used car, but it looks brand new.")
- Very sure -> Certain or Positive ("He is certain that he's right.")
- Very important -> Crucial ("It's crucial that you call a doctor.")
- Very confused -> Bewildered ("He had a bewildered look on his face.")
- Very fat -> Obese ("The obese man went on a diet.")
- Very good -> Outstanding ("You did an outstanding job.")

### Pronunciation Note: Linking Identical Consonants

When one word ends with the same consonant sound that the next word begins with, do not pronounce it twice. Connect them and say it once. - Was stuffed -> Wastuffed - He's certain -> He'certain

## Advanced English Words (Part 5)

### Summary

Advanced descriptive adjectives include reckless, abrupt, eager, eloquent, flawed, eccentric, gloomy, inevitable, and illegible (impossible to decipher). Fluent connected speech links final consonant codas directly into initial vowel onsets (caused an accident -> cause-dan-accident; ended abruptly -> ende-dabruptly).

### 10 Advanced Words

- Reckless: Careless; not worried about danger or consequences. ("His reckless driving caused an accident.")
- Abrupt / Abruptly: Very sudden and unexpected. ("The meeting ended abruptly.")
- Eager: Very enthusiastic and excited about something. ("She was eager to help us.")
- Eloquent: Speaking beautifully and persuasively; a talented speaker. ("He gave an eloquent speech.")
- Flawed: Imperfect or damaged. ("The diamond is valuable even though it's flawed.")
- Eccentric: Strange, weird, or behaving differently than normal people. ("He lost his job because of his eccentric behavior.")
- Gloomy: Dark, depressing, or pessimistic. ("I'm tired of this gloomy weather.")
- Inevitable: Unavoidable; certain to happen. ("Mistakes are inevitable.")
- Outgoing: Friendly and socially confident; the opposite of shy. ("His sister is very outgoing.")
- Illegible: Impossible to read because the handwriting is so bad or unclear. ("His writing is illegible.")

### Pronunciation Note: Linking into Vowels

Native speakers constantly link words ending in consonants to words beginning with vowels. - Caused an accident -> Cause-dan-accident - Ended abruptly -> Ende-dabruptly - Eager to help us -> Eagertohel-pus

## Advanced English Words (Part 7)

### Summary

Expand precise descriptive vocabulary with mediocre, famished (extremely hungry), prompt, bland, filthy, reluctant, savvy, and fickle (frequently changing preferences). Structural rhythm dictates primary stress on the first element of compound nouns (acting class, social network).

### 10 Advanced Words

- Mediocre: Average, unexceptional, or slightly bad. ("The food in that restaurant is mediocre.")
- Famished: Extremely hungry. ("After the hike, I was famished.")
- Prompt: Immediate; without delay. ("Thank you for your prompt reply.")
- Bland: Boring, uninteresting, or lacking flavor. ("If you don't add spices, the soup will be bland.")
- Filthy: Extremely dirty. ("Please clean this filthy room.")
- Reluctant: Unwilling and hesitant to do something. ("He was reluctant to go to the doctor.")
- Remote: Distant and far away, OR a very small chance of happening. ("They traveled to a remote island." / "There's a remote chance of rain.")
- Savvy: Knowledgeable and practical, especially about business or technology. ("The savvy businessman made a lot of money.")
- Blurry: Not clear; out of focus. ("Without her glasses, the letters were blurry.")
- Fickle: Frequently changing your mind, especially regarding emotions or tastes. ("The fickle customer changed his order many times.")

### Pronunciation Note: Compound Nouns

When two nouns are put together to create a single concept (a compound noun), stress the first word heavily. - Acting class (Not acting class) - Social network (Not social network)

## Advanced English Words (Part 9)

### Summary

Sophisticated formal adjectives include derogatory, irrelevant, amateur, rudimentary, chaotic, indifferent, scarce, sporadic, trivial, and subsequent. In standard American pronunciation, the letter 'o' in words like politician, apologize, comment, and topic is articulated as an open /ɑ/ ('ah') sound.

### 10 Advanced Words

- Derogatory: Highly critical, negative, or disrespectful. ("He apologized for his derogatory comment.")
- Irrelevant: Not related or not important to the subject being discussed. ("His speech was filled with irrelevant details.")
- Amateur: Doing something for pleasure rather than as a paid profession; not professional. ("He is an amateur photographer.")
- Rudimentary: Very basic, simple, or at a beginner level. ("I have a rudimentary understanding of physics.")
- Chaotic: Extremely confusing and completely lacking order. ("Driving in that city is chaotic.")
- Indifferent: Uncaring, passive, and having no opinion or interest. ("He behaves as if he is indifferent to her.")
- Scarce: Lacking; not enough available. ("During the war, medical supplies were scarce.")
- Sporadic: Happening occasionally, irregularly, and unpredictably. ("His school attendance was sporadic.")
- Trivial: Insignificant, shallow, and not important. ("Stop arguing about such a trivial topic.")
- Subsequent / Subsequently: Happening after something else. ("His first novel was popular, but his subsequent ones were not.")

### Pronunciation Note: The "Ah" Vowel

In American English, the letter "O" is frequently pronounced as a wide, open "Ah" sound. - Politician -> Pahlitician - Apologize -> Apahlogize - Derogatory -> Derahgatory - Comment -> Cahmment - Topics -> Tahpics - Stop -> Stahp

## Complete Course Review: Accurate English

### Congratulations!

You have completed the entire Accurate English study notes collection. These notes cover everything from the deepest intricacies of the American accent to highly advanced vocabulary and idiomatic expressions used by educated native speakers.

This final section is designed to test your retention and mastery of the entire collection through active recall.

### Section 1: American Pronunciation Mastery

1. The "Fast D" (Flap T) In American English, what happens to the letter 'T' when it falls between two vowel sounds? * Recall Check: How do you pronounce "water", "better", and "city"? What about the phrase "what a nice day"?

2. The American 'R' Unlike British English, the American 'R' is never silent. * Recall Check: Pronounce the words "mirror", "daughter", and "nature". Are you holding the 'R' sound strongly at the end?

3. The Schwa (Reduced Vowels) In an unstressed syllable, what sound do English vowels usually make? * Recall Check: Pronounce "Harvard", "Stanford", and "American". Does the unstressed vowel sound like a lazy "uh"?

4. Connected Speech (Linking) Native speakers rarely pronounce words in isolation. * Recall Check: How do you connect a word ending in a consonant to a word beginning with a vowel? (e.g., "caused an accident"). What happens when two identical consonants are next to each other? (e.g., "was stuffed", "speaks Spanish").

Stop using the word "very"! Can you recall the advanced single-word substitutes for the following?

- Very beautiful -> __________ (S... or G...)
- Very funny -> __________ (H...)
- Very poor -> __________ (D... or I...)
- Very rich -> __________ (W... or P...)
- Very scared -> __________ (P... or T...)
- Very confused -> __________ (B...)
- Very full (with food) -> __________ (S...)
- Very loud -> __________ (D...)
- Very angry -> __________ (I... or E...)
- Very important -> __________ (C...)

(Answers: 1. Stunning/Gorgeous, 2. Hilarious, 3. Destitute/Impoverished, 4. Well-off/Prosperous, 5. Petrified/Terrified, 6. Bewildered, 7. Stuffed, 8. Deafening, 9. Indignant/Enraged, 10. Crucial).

### Section 4: Difficult Pronunciations

Can you correctly pronounce the following commonly mispronounced words? * Colonel (Hint: it sounds exactly like a piece of corn). * Awry (Hint: not awe-ree). * Genre (Hint: French loanword). * Entrepreneur (Hint: French loanword). * Automatically (Hint: how many syllables?). * Develop (Hint: which syllable is stressed?).

### Final Challenge: The Actor's Method

To truly master this material, passive reading is not enough. Choose a single, short text or one of the study notes above. Mark up the linking points, the flap T's, and the reduced vowels. Then, read it aloud 50 times until the rhythm and pronunciation become absolute second nature.

Great job completing the course! Keep practicing!
